\title[Embedding projection algebras in partition monoids]{Which projection algebras embed in a partition monoid?\\ Wiring obstructions and the first non-embeddable examples}
\author{M.~Kinyon}
\date{Draft of \today}

\begin{document}
\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
The Wagner--Preston theorem embeds every inverse semigroup in a symmetric inverse monoid. For regular $*$-semigroups the natural analogue of the symmetric inverse monoid is the partition monoid $\P_X$, and we ask which regular $*$-semigroups, and which projection algebras, embed in one. A necessary condition is the projection condition: projections with a common lower bound commute. We show that it is far from sufficient, and that the further obstructions are of a single kind, which we call wiring: a matched path $a\mt c\mt b$ of projections (meaning $aca=a$, $cac=c$, and likewise for $b,c$) which the geometry of $\P_X$ forces to close, $a\mt b$, as soon as auxiliary projections commute with, lie below, or ``see alike'' suitable vertices of the path. We prove eight such quasi-identities valid in every $\P_X$, and an infinite family, the chain conditions, which shows that the quasivariety generated by the projection algebras of partition monoids is not finitely based. The eight conditions together with the projection condition characterise embeddability for projection algebras of order at most $8$: there are exactly nine minimal non-embeddable projection algebras of order at most $8$ satisfying the projection condition, and we describe them. On the other side we show that the local structure imposes no restriction at all, every finite completely simple regular $*$-semigroup being embeddable, and that for graph semigroups the projection condition and a single wiring condition are also sufficient.
\end{abstract}

\tableofcontents

%==============================================================================
\section{Introduction}\label{sec:intro}



The Wagner--Preston theorem represents every inverse semigroup faithfully by partial bijections: $S$ embeds in the symmetric inverse monoid $\mathcal I_X$ on its own underlying set. Inverse semigroups are exactly the regular $*$-semigroups in which the projections commute, the symmetric inverse monoid is the generic example of one, and the theorem says that it is also the universal one: the inverse semigroups are precisely the $*$-subsemigroups of the $\mathcal I_X$. For regular $*$-semigroups in general -- semigroups with an involution $x\mapsto x^*$ satisfying $xx^*x=x$, introduced by Nordahl and Scheiblich \cite{NS} -- the generic examples are the diagram monoids: the partition monoid $\P_X$ and its submonoids, the Brauer, Temperley--Lieb, Motzkin and partial Brauer monoids, in all of which the involution turns a diagram upside down. The partition monoid is the largest of them; it consists of the set partitions of $X\sqcup X'$, where $X'$ is a disjoint copy of $X$, under the product by concatenation, and it contains the symmetric group, the symmetric inverse monoid and the other diagram monoids as $*$-subsemigroups. In the recent structure theory of regular $*$-semigroups \cite{EM,EGMR} it plays, as a source of examples and of intuition, the role that $\mathcal I_X$ plays for inverse semigroups. It is therefore natural to ask for the analogue of the Wagner--Preston theorem: \emph{does every regular $*$-semigroup embed in some partition monoid, and if not, which ones do?}

The answer to the first question is no, and the second question is the subject of this paper. The obstructions live in the \emph{projection algebra}. Recall that the projections of a regular $*$-semigroup are the elements $p=p^2=p^*$, that they form a projection algebra under the binary operation $\theta_p(q)=pqp$, that every abstract projection algebra, in the axiomatic sense of Jones, is the projection algebra of some regular $*$-semigroup (Imaoka), and that the projection algebra determines much of the structure of $S$ \cite{Imaoka80,Imaoka81,Jones,EM,EGMR}. For an inverse semigroup the projection algebra is the semilattice of idempotents, and every semilattice embeds in some $\PA(\mathcal I_X)$; so in the inverse case the projection algebra imposes nothing, and Wagner--Preston is the statement that nothing else does either. For regular $*$-semigroups the projection algebra imposes a great deal. A $*$-embedding $S\to\P_X$ restricts to an embedding of projection algebras $\PA(S)\to\PA(\P_X)$, so every quasi-identity satisfied by the projection algebras $\PA(\P_X)$ is a necessary condition for embeddability, and all the obstructions we find are of this kind; whether an embedding of $\PA(S)$ can always be extended to one of $S$ is an open question (Section~\ref{sec:open}). We therefore study the projection algebras: \emph{which projection algebras embed in some $\PA(\P_X)$?} By a standard argument (Proposition~\ref{prop:finite}) a finite projection algebra embeds iff it satisfies all quasi-identities valid in the $\PA(\P_X)$, so the question is one of finding these quasi-identities, and of deciding whether finitely many suffice. We show that they do not, and we find the first nine of them, with a complete classification of the obstructions up to order $8$.

The question has a second ancestry. Every $\P_X$ embeds in the involuted semigroup of binary relations on $2^X$ (send a partition $\alpha$ to the relation $\{(A,B): A\sqcup B'$ is a union of blocks of $\alpha\}$), so $*$-subsemigroups of partition monoids are representable involuted semigroups in the sense of Schein \cite{Schein}, and Schein's quasi-identities for representability are necessary conditions; they carry no information here (every regular $*$-semigroup of order $\le5$ is representable by relations, including ones that embed in no $\P_X$), because what distinguishes the relations coming from $\P_X$ is that each is a Boolean subalgebra of $2^X\times2^X$. The analogy holds in the shape of the answer: Schein's characterisation of representability is an infinite system of quasi-identities, Bredikhin \cite{Bredikhin} showed that no finite system of elementary axioms can replace it, and the embeddable projection algebras, too, are the models of an infinite system of quasi-identities that cannot be replaced by a finite one. (Representable involuted semigroups in which $x^*$ is an inverse of $x$ do not seem to have been considered separately.)

One necessary condition has been known: in $\P_X$, two projections with a common lower bound in the natural order commute, i.e.\ the quasi-identity
\[
\Qone\qquad r=prp,\ r=qrq\ \Longrightarrow\ pq=qp
\]
holds in $\PA(\P_X)$ \todo{attribution of \Qone: found by East, Gould and Stokes (unpublished); the reformulation (2) of Proposition~\ref{prop:Q1} is due to the author; to be settled}. Our results are the following.

\emph{The projection condition is far from sufficient, and the further obstructions are wiring conditions.} All the conditions we find have the same shape. Call projections $p,q$ \emph{matched}, $p\mt q$, if $pqp=p$ and $qpq=q$; this is the \emph{friendly} relation of \cite{EGMR}, and in $\P_X$ it means that $p$ and $q$ have the same rank and their transversal blocks are paired off by the product. A matched path $a\mt c\mt b$ need not close ($a\mt b$ need not hold), but in $\P_X$ it is forced to close by any of a number of side conditions on auxiliary projections: that an auxiliary commutes with two vertices and sees the third as it sees one of them, that two vertices see an auxiliary alike, that an auxiliary lies above a vertex, and so on. Each such side condition is a quasi-identity in the language of projection algebras, and we prove eight of them in every $\P_X$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:main-wiring}), by arguments that analyse the blocks of the projections involved. The simplest, \Wire, has four variables and explains the smallest non-embeddable example, a projection algebra of order $6$; the others have up to seven variables.

\emph{The conditions are not finitely many.} The wiring conditions extend to an infinite family, the \emph{chain conditions} \Wn, in which the auxiliary is replaced by a chain $c\sim x_1\sim\dots\sim x_n\sim b$ of pairwise commuting projections each of which identifies $a$ with $c$; we prove all of them in every $\P_X$ by a counting invariant (Theorem~\ref{thm:main-chain}), and we exhibit for each $n$ a projection algebra of order $2n+5$ that satisfies \Wn[n-1] and all the other conditions of this paper but violates \Wn. Consequently the quasivariety generated by the projection algebras of partition monoids is not finitely based, and the class of finite embeddable projection algebras is not the class of finite models of any finite set of quasi-identities (Theorem~\ref{thm:main-nfb}).

\emph{The small examples are classified.} Up to order $8$ the obstructions are completely understood: a projection algebra of order at most $8$ embeds in some $\PA(\P_X)$ if and only if it satisfies \Qone\ together with the wiring conditions of Theorem~\ref{thm:main-wiring}, and there are exactly nine minimal non-embeddable projection algebras of order at most $8$ satisfying \Qone, one of order $6$, one of order $7$ and seven of order $8$, each the witness of one condition (Theorem~\ref{thm:main-census}). The computations behind this (an enumeration of projection algebras and of regular $*$-semigroups up to order $9$, and embedding searches into $\PA(\P_m)$ for $m\le8$) are described in Section~\ref{sec:comp}.

\emph{The local structure is no obstruction, and for graph semigroups the conditions are sufficient.} Every finite completely simple regular $*$-semigroup embeds in a finite partition monoid (Theorem~\ref{thm:main-cs}), so no condition on the principal factors can be necessary; and for the graph semigroups $Q(G,\bot)$ introduced in Section~\ref{sec:graph}, and for a layered generalisation of them, \Qone\ and \Wire\ are also sufficient (Theorem~\ref{thm:main-graph}).

\emph{The digraph conditions.} Besides the wiring conditions there is a family of conditions of a different shape, indexed by digraphs, whose simplest member is the \emph{triangle condition} \Tri: three pairwise commuting projections with common product $w$, three with common product $z$, matched crosswise, force $w=z$. In every projection algebra the triangle condition implies all the others of its family; it holds in every $\P_X$, and it fails in a $61$-element quotient of a $*$-subsemigroup of $\P_3$ that satisfies \Qone\ (Theorem~\ref{thm:main-digraph}); the hexagon condition, which was its original form, is equivalent to it on finite projection algebras but not in general.

The methods are elementary but the proofs are long, because the only tool is the block calculus of $\P_X$: a projection of $\P_X$ is a partition of $X$ with some of its classes marked, a product of projections is described by the join of their partitions, and a quasi-identity is proved by following chains of blocks. Two general devices organise these arguments: a \emph{counting invariant} attached to pairs of projections, which is constant along chains of commuting identifiers (Section~\ref{sec:chain}), and a \emph{finite descent} principle (Lemma~\ref{lem:descent}), which replaces the rank of $\P_X$ by the size of a principal down-set in an arbitrary finite projection algebra and lets several arguments be finished algebraically.

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Main theorems.}

\begin{theorem}[local structure; Section~\ref{sec:local}]\label{thm:main-cs}
Every finite completely simple regular $*$-semigroup embeds in a finite partition monoid. $\P_4$ contains non-orthodox completely simple $*$-subsemigroups.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[digraph conditions; Section~\ref{sec:digraph}]\label{thm:main-digraph}
For every set $X$ and every digraph $E$ on at least three vertices, $(\mathrm D_E)$ holds in $\P_X$ if and only if $E$ is not a star; in every projection algebra the triangle condition \Tri\ implies $(\mathrm D_E)$ for every non-star $E$, and on finite projection algebras it is equivalent to the hexagon condition. The triangle condition fails in a $61$-element regular $*$-semigroup satisfying \Qone, a quotient of a $*$-subsemigroup of $\P_3$, whose projection algebra $\PA(Q_0)$ has order $11$, and in no projection algebra of order at most $10$ satisfying \Qone.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[wiring conditions; Section~\ref{sec:wiring}]\label{thm:main-wiring}
The conditions \Wire, \Rcond, \Vcond, \Rp, \Pone, \Qcond, \Kfour\ and \Kzero\ of Section~\ref{sec:wiring} hold in every $\P_X$. In every projection algebra \Qcond\ implies \Wire\ and \Rp\ implies \Rcond; apart from this the conditions are independent: each of \Vcond, \Rp, \Pone, \Qcond, \Kfour, \Kzero\ is violated by an explicit projection algebra of order $8$ which satisfies \Qone, \Tri, all chain conditions and the other five, and \Wire, \Rcond\ have witnesses of orders $6$ and $7$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[chain conditions; Section~\ref{sec:chain}]\label{thm:main-chain}
For every $n\ge1$ the chain condition \Wn\ holds in every $\P_X$. For every $n$ there is a projection algebra $\mathfrak P^{\mathrm{ch}}_n$ of order $2n+5$ which satisfies \Wn[n-1], \Qone, \Tri\ and the eight wiring conditions, and violates \Wn.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[no finite basis]\label{thm:main-nfb}
The quasivariety generated by the projection algebras $\PA(\P_X)$, $X$ finite, is not finitely based; the class of finite projection algebras embeddable in some $\PA(\P_X)$ is not the class of finite models of any finite set of quasi-identities.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[orders at most $8$; Sections~\ref{sec:wiring} and \ref{sec:comp}]\label{thm:main-census}
A projection algebra of order at most $8$ embeds in some $\PA(\P_X)$ if and only if it satisfies \Qone\ and the eight wiring conditions, and then it embeds in $\PA(\P_7)$. The minimal non-embeddable projection algebras of order at most $8$ satisfying \Qone\ are exactly the nine algebras $\mathfrak P$ (order $6$), $\mathfrak P_7$ (order $7$) and $\mathfrak P_8^1,\dots,\mathfrak P_8^7$ (order $8$) of Sections~\ref{sec:wiring} and \ref{sec:graph}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[graph semigroups; Section~\ref{sec:graph}]\label{thm:main-graph}
A graph semigroup $Q(G,\bot)$, and more generally a layered graph semigroup, embeds in a partition monoid if and only if it satisfies \Qone\ and \Wire.
\end{theorem}

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Related work.} Regular $*$-semigroups were introduced by Nordahl and Scheiblich \cite{NS}; projection algebras were isolated by Imaoka \cite{Imaoka80,Imaoka81}, who showed that every one arises from a regular $*$-semigroup, and axiomatised in the form used here by Jones \cite{Jones}. East and Azeef Muhammed \cite{EM} describe a regular $*$-semigroup as a groupoid over its projection algebra, and East, Gray, Azeef Muhammed and Ru\v skuc \cite{EGMR} construct the free projection-generated and idempotent-generated regular $*$-semigroups over a projection algebra; the friendly relation of \cite{EGMR} is our matched relation. The partition monoid and its relatives are the principal examples throughout that work. The embedding question itself, and the conditions beyond \Qone, have not been studied in print before. Representations of involuted semigroups by binary relations go back to Schein \cite{Schein} and Bredikhin \cite{Bredikhin}, as discussed above.

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Structure of the paper.} Section~\ref{sec:prelim} collects the preliminaries: projection algebras, the reformulations of \Qone, the block calculus of $\P_X$ on which every proof rests, and two general devices, the counting invariant and finite descent. Section~\ref{sec:local} proves Theorem~\ref{thm:main-cs}, and Section~\ref{sec:digraph} the digraph conditions and the triangle condition. Section~\ref{sec:wiring} contains the eight wiring conditions with their proofs, their witnesses, and the classification up to order $8$; Section~\ref{sec:chain} the chain conditions and the non-finite basis theorem; Section~\ref{sec:graph} the graph semigroups. Section~\ref{sec:comp} describes the computations, with the evidence levels below kept apart, and Section~\ref{sec:open} the open problems.

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Evidence levels.} Throughout we distinguish three kinds of statement: a quasi-identity is \emph{proved} when it is shown to hold in every $\P_X$; it is \emph{verified for $m\le k$} when a SAT solver has shown it to hold in $\PA(\P_m)$ for all $m\le k$ (with variables allowed to coincide); and a finite algebra is \emph{non-embeddable for $m\le k$} when a search has found no embedding into $\PA(\P_m)$ for $m\le k$. Only the first is a theorem; the others are reported as such, and the main results use only proved statements.

%==============================================================================
\section{Preliminaries}\label{sec:prelim}

\subsection{Regular $*$-semigroups and projection algebras}\label{subsec:rss}

A \emph{regular $*$-semigroup} is a semigroup $S$ with a unary operation $x\mapsto x^*$ satisfying $x^{**}=x$, $(xy)^*=y^*x^*$ and $xx^*x=x$. Its \emph{projections} are the elements $p=p^2=p^*$; we write $\PA(S)$ for the set of projections. For projections $p,q$ the element $\theta_p(q)=pqp$ is again a projection, and $\PA(S)$ with the binary operation $(p,q)\mapsto\theta_p(q)$ is the \emph{projection algebra} of $S$. In the notation $p\cdot q:=\theta_q(p)$ (the value lies below the second argument) the identities satisfied by all projection algebras of regular $*$-semigroups are
\begin{align}
&p\cdot p=p, \qquad (q\cdot p)\cdot p=q\cdot p, \qquad (p\cdot q)\cdot p=q\cdot p,\label{eq:PA123}\\
&((r\cdot p)\cdot q)\cdot p=r\cdot(q\cdot p), \qquad (((r\cdot p)\cdot q)\cdot p)\cdot q=(r\cdot p)\cdot q,\label{eq:PA45}
\end{align}
and conversely an algebra $(P,\cdot)$ satisfying \eqref{eq:PA123}--\eqref{eq:PA45} is the projection algebra of some regular $*$-semigroup. In $\theta$-notation the axioms read $\theta_p(p)=p$, $\theta_p\theta_p=\theta_p$, $\theta_p(\theta_q(p))=\theta_p(q)$, $\theta_p\theta_q\theta_p=\theta_{\theta_p(q)}$ and $\theta_q\theta_p\theta_q\theta_p=\theta_q\theta_p$; in this form, with the maps acting on the right, they are Imaoka's axioms for a \emph{$P$-groupoid} \cite{Imaoka81}, whose representation theorem \cite{Imaoka80} realises every such structure as the set of projections of a regular $*$-semigroup, and Jones \cite[Section~7]{Jones} shows that they are equivalent to the axioms of his projection algebras \eqref{eq:PA123}--\eqref{eq:PA45}. We call an abstract algebra satisfying them a projection algebra; everything below about projection algebras uses only these axioms, except where finiteness is invoked explicitly.

The \emph{natural order} on projections is $q\le p$ iff $q=pq=qp$, equivalently $q=\theta_p(q)$. We write $\dn p=\{q:q\le p\}$. Two projections \emph{commute}, $p\sim q$, if $pq=qp$; then $pq$ is a projection, $pq\le p,q$, and $pq=\theta_p(q)=\theta_q(p)$. In the projection algebra alone commutation is expressed by $\theta_p(q)=\theta_q(p)$:

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:comm}
For projections $p,q$ of a regular $*$-semigroup, $pq=qp$ iff $\theta_p(q)=\theta_q(p)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
If $pq=qp$ then $pqp=pq=qpq$. Conversely let $w=pqp=qpq$. Then $(pq)(pq)^*=pqqp=w$, so by regularity $pq=(pq)(pq)^*(pq)=w\,pq=pqp\,pq=(pq)^2=p(qpq)=pw=p\,pqp=w$; symmetrically $qp=w$.
\end{proof}

We note in passing that the product of two projections is always idempotent, since $pq=(pq)(pq)^*(pq)=pqqppq=pqpq$; in general it is not a projection.

\begin{definition}[matched pairs]\label{def:matched}
Projections $p,q$ are \emph{matched}, $p\mt q$, if $\theta_p(q)=p$ and $\theta_q(p)=q$.
\end{definition}

This is the \emph{friendly} relation $p\,\mathcal F\,q$ of \cite{EGMR}; we keep a separate symbol because in $\P_X$ it comes with a bijection of transversal blocks (Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(c)) and the word ``matched'' refers to that bijection. The relation is reflexive and symmetric, not transitive, and matched pairs can be manufactured at will:

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:friends}
For all projections $p,q$: $\theta_p(q)\mt\theta_q(p)$, and $\theta_{\theta_p(q)}(q)=\theta_p(q)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Both statements follow from the axioms in $\theta$-notation. Using $\theta_p\theta_q\theta_p=\theta_{\theta_p(q)}$ and then $\theta_p(\theta_q(p))=\theta_p(q)$ twice,
\[
\theta_{\theta_p(q)}(\theta_q(p))=\theta_p\theta_q\theta_p(\theta_q(p))=\theta_p\theta_q(\theta_p(q))=\theta_p(\theta_q(p))=\theta_p(q),
\]
and symmetrically $\theta_{\theta_q(p)}(\theta_p(q))=\theta_q(p)$; so $\theta_p(q)\mt\theta_q(p)$. Likewise $\theta_{\theta_p(q)}(q)=\theta_p\theta_q\theta_p(q)=\theta_p(\theta_q(p))=\theta_p(q)$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The projection condition}\label{subsec:Q1}

In $\P_X$, as we recall in Section~\ref{subsec:PX}, two projections with a common lower bound commute. The corresponding quasi-identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Q1}\tag{PC}
\theta_p(r)=r=\theta_q(r)\ \Longrightarrow\ \theta_p(q)=\theta_q(p)
\end{equation}
is therefore necessary for embeddability. It has several equivalent forms.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:Q1}
For a regular $*$-semigroup $S$ the following are equivalent.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\arabic*)}]
\item $\PA(S)$ satisfies \eqref{eq:Q1}.
\item Every idempotent of $S$ lying above a projection in the natural order is a projection.
\item For every projection $p$, the set $S_p=\{x\in S: px=xp=p\}$ is an inverse semigroup.
\item Two projections commute if and only if they have a common lower bound.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
$S_p$ is closed under multiplication and under $*$ (if $px=xp=p$ then $px^*=(xp)^*=p$), so it is a regular $*$-semigroup with zero $p$; it is inverse iff all its idempotents are projections, and its idempotents are exactly the idempotents $e\ge p$. This gives (2)$\Leftrightarrow$(3).

(2)$\Rightarrow$(1). Let $r\le p,q$. The element $e=pq$ is idempotent, and $er=pqr=pr=r=re$, so $e$ lies above the projection $r$; by (2) it is a projection, and $pq=e=e^*=qp$.

(1)$\Rightarrow$(2). Let $e=e^2$ and let $r$ be a projection with $er=re=r$. Then $r=r^*=(re)^*=e^*r$ and likewise $r=re^*$, so the projections $f=ee^*$ and $g=e^*e$ both lie above $r$: $fr=ee^*r=er=r$, $rf=re\,e^*=re^*=r$, and similarly for $g$. By (1), $fg=gf$, that is, $ee^*e^*e=e^*ee^*$. Multiplying this on the left by $e$ gives $fg=e\,e^*e^*e=(ee^*)(ee^*)=f$, and multiplying it on the right by $e$ gives $fg=f(ge)=(fg)e=(gf)e=e^*e\,ee^*\,e=(e^*e)(e^*e)=g$ (using $ge=e^*ee=g$). Hence $ee^*=e^*e$, and $e=ee^*e=e^*ee=e^*e$ is a projection.

(1)$\Leftrightarrow$(4). If $pq=qp$ then $pq$ is a projection below $p$ and $q$; the converse is \eqref{eq:Q1} with Lemma~\ref{lem:comm}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:Sp}
In $\P_X$, an element $x$ lies above a projection $p$ iff $x$ maps every $\T(p)$-block onto itself by a block bijection and permutes the $\N(p)$-blocks, so that $(\P_X)_p\cong\prod_{A\in\T(p)}\mathcal I^*_A\times\mathcal J_p$ with $\mathcal I^*_A$ the dual symmetric inverse monoid on $A$ and $\mathcal J_p$ an inverse monoid of partial block bijections; for the projection $p$ with all blocks singletons and nontransversal, $(\P_X)_p$ is the symmetric inverse monoid $\mathcal I_X$. So \eqref{eq:Q1} says that these ``unitary'' subsemigroups are inverse.
\end{remark}

Two consequences of \eqref{eq:Q1} are used throughout: the projections above a fixed projection pairwise commute; and two distinct matched projections have no common lower bound (they would commute, and then $p=pqp=pq=q$).

\subsection{Partition monoids and the block calculus}\label{subsec:PX}

For a set $X$ let $X'=\{x':x\in X\}$ be a disjoint copy. The partition monoid $\P_X$ consists of the set partitions of $X\sqcup X'$; the product $\alpha\beta$ is obtained by identifying the lower points of $\alpha$ with the upper points of $\beta$, taking connected components, and forgetting the middle layer, and $\alpha^*$ interchanges $X$ and $X'$. A block is \emph{transversal} if it meets both $X$ and $X'$; the \emph{rank} of $\alpha$ is the number of transversal blocks.

A projection $p$ of $\P_X$ is determined by a pair $(\varepsilon_p,\T(p))$, where $\varepsilon_p$ is a partition of $X$ and $\T(p)$ is a set of $\varepsilon_p$-classes: the blocks of $p$ are $A\cup A'$ for $A\in\T(p)$ (the \emph{$\T$-blocks}) and $B$, $B'$ for the remaining classes $B$ (the \emph{$\N$-blocks}, forming $\N(p)$). We put $\supp p=\bigcup\T(p)$, so that $\rk p=|\T(p)|$. We say ``block of $p$'' for a class of $\varepsilon_p$, with the label $\T$ or $\N$ understood. For projections $p,q$ the \emph{join} $\varepsilon_p\vee\varepsilon_q$ is the join of partitions of $X$; its classes are the connected components of the graph on the blocks of $p$ and $q$ in which two blocks are adjacent when they meet. A \emph{chain} in $\varepsilon_p\vee\varepsilon_q$ is a sequence of blocks, alternately of $p$ and of $q$, in which consecutive blocks meet; two blocks lie in a common class iff they are joined by a chain. For a class $K$ we write $U_K$ for the union of the $\T(p)$-blocks contained in $K$ and $L_K$ for the union of the $\T(q)$-blocks in $K$. The following lemma is the whole of our toolkit.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:PX}
Let $p,q$ be projections of $\P_X$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item The blocks of $\theta_p(q)=pqp$ are the $\N(p)$-blocks and the sets $U_K$ for the classes $K$ of $\varepsilon_p\vee\varepsilon_q$ meeting $\supp p$; $U_K$ is a $\T$-block of $\theta_p(q)$ iff $K$ meets $\supp q$. In particular the blocks of $\theta_p(q)$ not contained in $\supp p$ are exactly the $\N(p)$-blocks; consequently, if $e\le p$ then $\supp e\subseteq\supp p$, every $\T(e)$-block is a union of $\T(p)$-blocks, and every block of $e$ not contained in $\supp p$ is an $\N(p)$-block.
\item Suppose $pq=qp$. Then every class $K$ of $\varepsilon_p\vee\varepsilon_q$ is of one of four types: \textup{(i)} $K=U_K=L_K$; \textup{(ii)} $K=U_K$ is a single $\N(q)$-block; \textup{(iii)} $K=L_K$ is a single $\N(p)$-block; \textup{(iv)} $K$ is a single block of both $p$ and $q$. The $\T$-blocks of $pq$ are the classes of type \textup{(i)}; hence $\supp(pq)=\supp p\cap\supp q$, and every $\N(q)$-block contained in $\supp p$ is a union of $\T(p)$-blocks.
\item Suppose $p\mt q$. Then every class of $\varepsilon_p\vee\varepsilon_q$ meeting $\supp p\cup\supp q$ contains exactly one $\T(p)$-block and exactly one $\T(q)$-block. In particular $\rk p=\rk q$, no $\T(p)$-block is an $\N(q)$-block, and the classes of $\varepsilon_p\vee\varepsilon_q$ induce a bijection $\T(p)\to\T(q)$. Conversely, if every class meeting $\supp p\cup\supp q$ contains exactly one $\T(p)$-block and one $\T(q)$-block then $p\mt q$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) In the product $pq$ the middle layer is partitioned by $\varepsilon_p\vee\varepsilon_q$, since every block of $p$ has a lower part and every block of $q$ an upper part in the middle; the transversal blocks of $pq$ are $U_K\cup L_K'$ for the classes $K$ with $U_K\ne\emptyset\ne L_K$, the upper nontransversal blocks are the $\N(p)$-blocks and the sets $U_K$ with $L_K=\emptyset$. Now $\theta_p(q)=(pq)(pq)^*$ is the projection whose $\T$-blocks are the upper parts of the transversal blocks of $pq$ and whose $\N$-blocks are the upper nontransversal blocks of $pq$. For the last sentence use $e=\theta_p(e)$.

(b) The projection $pq$ is symmetric, so $U_K\cup L_K'$ is a block only if $U_K=L_K$ (when both are nonempty), and the family of upper nontransversal blocks $\N(p)\cup\{U_K:L_K=\emptyset\ne U_K\}$ equals the family of lower ones $\N(q)\cup\{L_K:U_K=\emptyset\ne L_K\}$. Let $K$ be a class. If $U_K\ne\emptyset\ne L_K$, an $\N(p)$-block inside $K$ would be an $\N$-block of $pq$, hence (as $U_K\ne\emptyset$) an $\N(q)$-block; but a set which is a block of both $p$ and $q$ is a class by itself; so $K$ contains no $\N(p)$-block and dually no $\N(q)$-block, and $K=U_K=L_K$, type (i). If $U_K\ne\emptyset=L_K$ then $U_K$ is an $\N(q)$-block; $K$ is a union of $q$-blocks containing the $q$-block $U_K$ and $p$-blocks; as before the remaining $p$-blocks in $K$ would be common blocks, so $K=U_K$, type (ii). Type (iii) is dual, and if $U_K=L_K=\emptyset$ then $K$ consists of $\N(p)$- and $\N(q)$-blocks, which must coincide as above, type (iv). Finally an $\N(q)$-block $B\subseteq\supp p$ lies in a class $K$ which is not of type (i) or (iii) (these consist of $\T(q)$-blocks only) nor of type (iv) ($B$ meets $\supp p$), so $K=B$ is of type (ii).

(c) $pqp=p$ says that the upper projection of $pq$ is $p$: its $\T$-blocks $\{U_K:U_K\ne\emptyset\ne L_K\}$ are exactly the $\T(p)$-blocks, and its $\N$-blocks $\N(p)\cup\{U_K:U_K\ne\emptyset=L_K\}$ are exactly the $\N(p)$-blocks. So every class meeting $\supp p$ contains exactly one $\T(p)$-block and at least one $\T(q)$-block; by symmetry, exactly one of each. A $\T(p)$-block which is an $\N(q)$-block would be a class by itself, containing no $\T(q)$-block. The converse is read off from (a).
\end{proof}

We shall use (a) constantly in the following form: for projections $p,q$, \emph{$p$ sees $q$ as} $\theta_p(q)$, and $\theta_p(q)$ is determined by the partition of $\supp p$ into the sets $U_K$ together with the information which of them are \emph{bright} (meet $\supp q$) and which are \emph{dark}. Thus ``$p$ sees $q$ and $r$ alike'', $\theta_p(q)=\theta_p(r)$, means that $\varepsilon_p\vee\varepsilon_q$ and $\varepsilon_p\vee\varepsilon_r$ induce the same partition of $\supp p$ with the same bright parts; we then say that $p$ \emph{identifies} $q$ with $r$ and write $p\in\Id(q,r)$. Two orders of magnitude of this statement will matter: $\theta_p(q)=p$ (every class meeting $\supp p$ contains one $\T(p)$-block and is bright: $p$ is \emph{half-matched} with $q$) and $\theta_p(q)=q$ ($q\le p$).

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:order}
Let $p,q$ be projections of $\P_X$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item $q\le p$ iff every block of $p$ lies in a block of $q$ and every $\N(p)$-block is a block of $q$. In that case every block of $q$ is an $\N(p)$-block or a union of $\T(p)$-blocks, and every $\N(p)$-block is an $\N(q)$-block.
\item $\theta_p(q)=p$ iff every class of $\varepsilon_p\vee\varepsilon_q$ meeting $\supp p$ contains exactly one $\T(p)$-block and meets $\supp q$. In particular every block of $q$ then meets at most one $\T(p)$-block.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) If $q=\theta_p(q)$, Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(a) describes the blocks of $q$ as stated. Conversely, if the two conditions hold then $\varepsilon_p\vee\varepsilon_q=\varepsilon_q$, every class of $\varepsilon_q$ meeting $\supp p$ is a union of $\T(p)$-blocks, and it is a $\T(q)$-block iff it meets $\supp q$; so $\theta_p(q)=q$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(a). (b) is Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(a) read for $\theta_p(q)=p$.
\end{proof}

For a matched pair $p\mt q$, Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(c) gives a bijection $\varphi\colon\T(p)\to\T(q)$, sending a $\T(p)$-block to the $\T(q)$-block in its class of $\varepsilon_p\vee\varepsilon_q$. Restrictions of $q$ are transported to restrictions of $p$ along $\varphi$:

\begin{lemma}[transport]\label{lem:transport}
Let $p\mt q$ in $\P_X$ with bijection $\varphi\colon\T(p)\to\T(q)$, and let $x\le q$. For a set $Y$ which is a union of $\T(q)$-blocks put $\varphi^{-1}[Y]=\bigcup\{\varphi^{-1}(C):C\in\T(q),\ C\subseteq Y\}$. Then the blocks of $\theta_p(x)$ are the $\N(p)$-blocks and the sets $\varphi^{-1}[Y]$ for the blocks $Y\subseteq\supp q$ of $x$, with the label of $Y$; and the class of $\varepsilon_p\vee\varepsilon_x$ containing $\varphi^{-1}[Y]$ is the union of the classes of $\varepsilon_p\vee\varepsilon_q$ containing the $\T(p)$-blocks in $\varphi^{-1}[Y]$, which meets $\supp p$ in $\varphi^{-1}[Y]$ and $\supp q$ in $Y$. These are all the classes of $\varepsilon_p\vee\varepsilon_x$ meeting $\supp p$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By Lemma~\ref{lem:order}(a) the blocks of $x$ outside $\supp q$ are $\N(q)$-blocks and those inside are unions of $\T(q)$-blocks. The classes of $\varepsilon_p\vee\varepsilon_q$ meeting $\supp p$ each contain exactly one $\T(p)$-block $A$ and exactly one $\T(q)$-block $\varphi(A)$, and all other blocks in them are $\N$-blocks. Passing to $\varepsilon_p\vee\varepsilon_x\ge\varepsilon_p\vee\varepsilon_q$, a block $Y\subseteq\supp q$ of $x$ merges the classes of the $\T(q)$-blocks it contains, and the other blocks of $x$ lie inside single classes; since each class contains one $\T(q)$-block, which lies in one $Y$, the classes of $\varepsilon_p\vee\varepsilon_x$ meeting $\supp p$ are the unions described. Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(a) then gives the blocks $\varphi^{-1}[Y]$ of $\theta_p(x)$, transversal iff the class meets $\supp x$, i.e.\ (as the class meets $\supp q$ exactly in $Y$) iff $Y$ is a $\T$-block of $x$.
\end{proof}

Finally, a counting invariant. For equivalence relations $\pi$ on $X$ write $|\pi|$ for the number of classes of $\pi$ and $|\pi|_Y$ for the number of classes meeting a set $Y\subseteq X$. For projections $p,q,x$ put
\[
\delta_{pq}(x)=|\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_p|-|\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_q|,\qquad
\delta'_{pq}(x)=|\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_p|_{\supp p}-|\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_q|_{\supp q};
\]
both depend on $x$ only through $\varepsilon_x$.

\begin{lemma}[counting invariant]\label{lem:count}
Let $p,q$ be projections of $\P_X$, let $w$ identify $p$ with $q$ ($\theta_w(p)=\theta_w(q)$) and let $z\le w$. Then $\delta_{pq}(z)=\delta_{pq}(w)$ and $\delta'_{pq}(z)=\delta'_{pq}(w)$. Consequently, if $x\sim y$ and $y\in\Id(p,q)$, then $\delta_{pq}(xy)=\delta_{pq}(y)$ and $\delta'_{pq}(xy)=\delta'_{pq}(y)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Put $S=\supp w$, $\rho_p=\varepsilon_w\vee\varepsilon_p$ and $\rho_q=\varepsilon_w\vee\varepsilon_q$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(a) the blocks of $\theta_w(p)$ inside $S$ are the sets $K\cap S$ for the classes $K$ of $\rho_p$ meeting $S$, transversal iff $K$ meets $\supp p$; so $\theta_w(p)=\theta_w(q)$ says that $\rho_p$ and $\rho_q$ induce the same partition $\sigma$ of $S$, and that for every part $G$ of $\sigma$ the $\rho_p$-class containing $G$ meets $\supp p$ iff the $\rho_q$-class containing $G$ meets $\supp q$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:order}(a), every block of $z\le w$ is an $\N(w)$-block or a union of $\T(w)$-blocks, so $\varepsilon_z=\varepsilon_w\vee\mu$ for an equivalence relation $\mu$ all of whose nontrivial classes lie inside $S$. Now $\varepsilon_z\vee\varepsilon_p=\rho_p\vee\mu$, and its classes are of two kinds. A class of $\rho_p$ disjoint from $S$ is untouched by $\mu$ and remains a class. The classes of $\rho_p$ meeting $S$ are merged according to $\sigma\vee\mu|_S$: a path alternating $\rho_p$-steps and nontrivial $\mu$-steps has all its $\mu$-steps inside $S$, so between two consecutive $\mu$-steps it joins a point of $S$ to a point of $S$ within one $\rho_p$-class, i.e.\ within one part of $\sigma$; hence two $\rho_p$-classes meeting $S$ lie in one class of $\rho_p\vee\mu$ iff their parts of $\sigma$ lie in one class of $\sigma\vee\mu|_S$. So the classes of the second kind correspond bijectively to the classes of $\sigma\vee\mu|_S$, and such a class meets $\supp p$ iff one of its constituent $\rho_p$-classes does. The same description holds for $q$, with the same $\sigma$ and the same $\mu|_S$, and corresponding constituents (same part $G$) meet $\supp p$, resp.\ $\supp q$, simultaneously. Therefore
\[
|\varepsilon_z\vee\varepsilon_p|-|\varepsilon_z\vee\varepsilon_q|=\bigl(|\rho_p|-|\sigma|\bigr)-\bigl(|\rho_q|-|\sigma|\bigr)=\delta_{pq}(w),
\]
and the same computation restricted to the classes meeting $\supp p$, resp.\ $\supp q$ (the classes of the first kind meeting $\supp p$ are the $\rho_p$-classes meeting $\supp p$ but not $S$, whose number is $|\rho_p|_{\supp p}$ minus the number of parts $G$ whose $\rho_p$-class meets $\supp p$, and the latter number is the same for $q$) gives $\delta'_{pq}(z)=\delta'_{pq}(w)$. For the last assertion, $xy=yx$ is a projection with $xy\le y$, so the values are transported from $y$ to $xy$.
\end{proof}

Lemma~\ref{lem:count} is the engine of the chain conditions in Section~\ref{sec:chain}; it also enters the proof of \Kfour.

For a matched pair $a\mt c$ we write $A_1,\dots,A_m$ for the $\T(a)$-blocks, $C_i$ for the $\T(c)$-block in the class of $A_i$, and $\Gamma_i$ for that class of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_c$; so $\Gamma_i\cap\supp a=A_i$, $\Gamma_i\cap\supp c=C_i$, and the other classes of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_c$ meet neither support. For a matched path $a\mt c\mt b$ we index the $\T(b)$-blocks through $c$: $B_i$ is the $\T(b)$-block in the class $\Delta_i$ of $\varepsilon_b\vee\varepsilon_c$ containing $C_i$. We also put
\[
u=\theta_a(b),\qquad v=\theta_b(a),
\]
so that $u\le a$, $v\le b$, $u\mt v$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:friends}), and $a\mt b$ iff $u=a$ and $v=b$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(a) the $\T$-blocks of $u$ are the sets $D\cap\supp a$ for the classes $D$ of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_b$ meeting both supports, and $a\mt b$ iff every class of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_b$ meeting $\supp a\cup\supp b$ contains exactly one $A$ and one $B$.

\subsection{Finite descent}\label{subsec:descent}

In $\P_X$ many arguments end with a rank count: a projection below a projection of the same rank equals it. The following lemma provides the same conclusion in every finite projection algebra, with the size of a principal down-set in place of the rank.

\begin{lemma}[finite descent]\label{lem:descent}
Let $P$ be a projection algebra.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item If $p\mt q$ then $\theta_q$ maps $\dn p$ into $\dn q$, $\theta_p$ maps $\dn q$ into $\dn p$, and the two maps are mutually inverse bijections.
\item If $P$ is finite, $q\le p$, and $p,q$ lie in one component of the graph of $\mt$, then $q=p$.
\item If $P$ is finite, $\theta_p(a)=p$, and $a,p$ lie in one component of the graph of $\mt$, then $a\mt p$.
\item If $P$ is finite and $a\mt c\mt b$, then $a\mt b$ iff $\theta_a(b)=a$ iff $\theta_b(a)=b$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
(a) $\theta_q(e)\le q$ for every $e$. For $e\le p$ the axiom $\theta_p\theta_q\theta_p=\theta_{\theta_p(q)}$ gives $\theta_p(\theta_q(e))=\theta_p\theta_q\theta_p(e)=\theta_{\theta_p(q)}(e)=\theta_p(e)=e$, and symmetrically. (b) By (a) and induction along a chain $q=x_0\mt x_1\mt\dots\mt x_k=p$, $|\dn q|=|\dn p|$; as $\dn q\subseteq\dn p$, the two sets coincide, so $p\le q$ and $q=p$. (c) Put $a'=\theta_a(p)\le a$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:friends}, $a'\mt\theta_p(a)=p$, so $a'$ lies in the component of $p$, hence of $a$, and (b) gives $a'=a$, i.e.\ $\theta_a(p)=a$. (d) If $u=\theta_a(b)=a$ then $v=\theta_b(a)\le b$ and $v\mt u=a\mt c\mt b$, so $v=b$ by (b); symmetrically.
\end{proof}

In $\P_X$ the down-set $\dn p$ depends only on $\rk p$, so (b) is the rank count; but the lemma holds in every finite projection algebra, and this has consequences for the logical relations among our conditions: a quasi-identity whose proof in $\P_X$ uses only the block calculus and rank counts along matched chains often has a finite-algebraic twin which is not derivable equationally. We shall meet one instance in Section~\ref{subsec:K0}.

%==============================================================================
\section{The local structure is unconstrained}\label{sec:local}

A natural first guess for a condition beyond \eqref{eq:Q1} is a restriction on the ``local'' pieces of $S$, that is, on its principal factors, which are completely $0$-simple regular $*$-semigroups. Some care is needed in saying what such a restriction could be. The zero of a principal factor is not in general an element of $S$, and when it is -- when $S$ contains a completely $0$-simple $*$-subsemigroup together with its zero -- \eqref{eq:Q1} already forces that subsemigroup to be a Brandt semigroup, since two distinct matched projections have no common lower bound (Section~\ref{subsec:Q1}). So the zero pattern of a principal factor is not local information: it is decided by how the $\D$-class sits inside $S$, and it interacts with the rest of $S$, as the triangle condition of Section~\ref{sec:digraph} shows. What remains genuinely local is the nonzero part of a principal factor: a set of projections of a $\D$-class with pairwise nonzero products, together with the sandwich entries and the maximal subgroup. These are exactly the completely simple $*$-subsemigroups of $S$, and a condition on them would be a condition on finite completely simple regular $*$-semigroups. There is none.

Recall that a completely simple regular $*$-semigroup is a Rees matrix semigroup $\mathcal M[G;I,I;P]$ with involution $(i,g,j)^*=(j,g^{-1},i)$, and that the sandwich matrix may be normalised so that $p_{ii}=1$ and $p_{ji}=p_{ij}^{-1}$ for all $i,j\in I$ \cite{NS}. Its projections are the elements $(i,1,i)$.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:cs}
Every finite completely simple regular $*$-semigroup embeds in a finite partition monoid. Explicitly, let $S=\mathcal M[G;I,I;P]$ be in normal form, let $G\le\Sym(k)$ act on $[k]=\{1,\dots,k\}$ on the right, and let
\[
X=\{a_{i,s}: i\in I,\ s\in[k]\}\ \sqcup\ \{c_{ij,s}: i\ne j\in I,\ s\in[k]\},\qquad |X|=k|I|^2 .
\]
For $i\in I$ let $\pi_i$ be the partition of $X$ into the singletons $\{a_{i,s}\}$, the triples
\[
T_{i;l,s}=\{c_{il,s},\ a_{l,sp_{il}},\ c_{li,sp_{il}}\}\qquad(l\in I\setminus\{i\},\ s\in[k])
\]
and the singletons $\{c_{lm,s}\}$ with $i\notin\{l,m\}$. Let $\varphi(i,g,j)$ be the partition of $X\sqcup X'$ with transversal blocks $\{a_{i,s},a'_{j,sg}\}$ $(s\in[k])$, upper nontransversal blocks the blocks of $\pi_i$ other than the singletons $\{a_{i,s}\}$, and lower nontransversal blocks the primed copies of the blocks of $\pi_j$ other than the singletons $\{a_{j,s}\}$. Then $\varphi\colon S\to\P_X$ is an injective $*$-homomorphism.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
That $\pi_i$ is a partition of $X$ is checked by locating each point: $a_{l,s}$ with $l\ne i$ lies in $T_{i;l,sp_{li}}$ only, $c_{il,s}$ in $T_{i;l,s}$ only, $c_{li,s}$ in $T_{i;l,sp_{li}}$ only, and the other points are singletons. The blocks of $\pi_i$ other than the $\{a_{i,s}\}$ cover $X\setminus\{a_{i,s}:s\in[k]\}$, so $\varphi(i,g,j)$ is a partition of $X\sqcup X'$, of rank $k$. The description of $\varphi(i,g,j)^*$ is obtained by interchanging $i$ with $j$ and $g$ with $g^{-1}$, so $\varphi$ respects the involution; and $\varphi$ is injective, since the transversal blocks determine $i$, $j$ and the permutation $s\mapsto sg$, hence $g$.

For the product $\varphi(i,g,j)\varphi(l,h,m)$ consider the middle layer, which carries the partition $\pi_j$ (from the lower blocks of the first factor, with the points $a_{j,t}$ attached to the transversal blocks $\{a_{i,s},a'_{j,sg}\}$) and the partition $\pi_l$ (from the upper blocks of the second factor, with the points $a_{l,t}$ attached to the transversal blocks $\{a_{l,t},a'_{m,th}\}$). If $j=l$ the two partitions coincide, so the components of the middle layer are the blocks of $\pi_j$; the point $a_{j,sg}$ is a singleton of $\pi_j$ and is attached to $a_{i,s}$ above and $a'_{m,sgh}$ below, and no other middle block meets a transversal block. The product therefore has transversal blocks $\{a_{i,s},a'_{m,sgh}\}$, upper nontransversal blocks those of the first factor and lower ones those of the second: it is $\varphi(i,gh,m)=\varphi((i,g,j)(j,h,m))$, as $p_{jj}=1$. If $j\ne l$, put $t=sg\,p_{jl}$ and consider the set
\[
M_s=\{a_{j,sg},\ c_{jl,sg},\ c_{lj,t},\ a_{l,t}\}=T_{l;j,t}\cup T_{j;l,sg}\cup\{a_{j,sg}\}\cup\{a_{l,t}\},
\]
where $T_{l;j,t}=\{c_{lj,t},a_{j,tp_{lj}},c_{jl,tp_{lj}}\}=\{c_{lj,t},a_{j,sg},c_{jl,sg}\}$ is a block of $\pi_l$ and $T_{j;l,sg}=\{c_{jl,sg},a_{l,t},c_{lj,t}\}$ is a block of $\pi_j$. Each point of $M_s$ lies, in each of $\pi_j$ and $\pi_l$, in a block contained in $M_s$ (the points $a_{j,sg}$ and $a_{l,t}$ are singletons of $\pi_j$ and $\pi_l$ respectively), so $M_s$ is a component of the middle layer; it contains exactly one transversal end $a_{j,sg}$ of the first factor, attached to $a_{i,s}$, and exactly one transversal start $a_{l,t}$ of the second, attached to $a'_{m,th}$. Hence the product has the transversal blocks $\{a_{i,s},a'_{m,sgp_{jl}h}\}$, which are those of $\varphi(i,gp_{jl}h,m)$. The upper nontransversal blocks of the first factor have no points in the middle layer, so they are blocks of the product; every other upper point lies in a transversal block; so the upper nontransversal blocks of the product are those of $\varphi(i,\cdot,\cdot)$, and dually for the lower ones. Thus $\varphi(i,g,j)\varphi(l,h,m)=\varphi(i,gp_{jl}h,m)=\varphi((i,g,j)(l,h,m))$.
\end{proof}

Since every finite group embeds in some $\Sym(k)$, every finite completely simple regular $*$-semigroup embeds in some $\P_n$. Two remarks on what this rules out.

\begin{remark}\label{rem:orthodox}
Orthodoxy of the completely simple $*$-subsemigroups is not necessary either, although $\P_n$ for small $n$ looks ``flat'': the projections
\begin{align*}
p&: \{0\},\{1\}\ \text{transversal},\ \{2,3\}\ \text{nontransversal};\\
q&: \{1\},\{3\}\ \text{transversal},\ \{0,2\}\ \text{nontransversal};\\
r&: \{0\},\{3\}\ \text{transversal},\ \{1,2\}\ \text{nontransversal}
\end{align*}
of $\P_4$ satisfy $p\mt q\mt r\mt p$, but $pqr\ne pr$: the sandwich matrix of the rank-$2$ $\D$-class of $\P_4$ has a non-flat triangle, and the $*$-subsemigroup generated by $p,q,r$ is a non-orthodox completely simple regular $*$-semigroup $\mathcal M[\Z_2;3,3;P]$ with $p_{12}\ne1$.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[normal $*$-bands]\label{rem:normal}
For a strong semilattice $Y$ of square $*$-bands $A_\alpha\times A_\alpha$ with structure maps $f_{\alpha\beta}\colon A_\alpha\to A_\beta$ ($\alpha\ge\beta$), \eqref{eq:Q1} holds iff all $f_{\alpha\beta}$ are injective: a projection $(c,c)$ of a lower component lies below $(a,b)$ iff $f(a)=f(b)=c$. If $Y$ has a least element $0$, injectivity lets us regard every $A_\alpha$ as a subset of $A=A_0$, and the band becomes the $*$-subsemigroup $\{(\alpha,a,b): a,b\in A_\alpha\}$ of $Y\times(A\times A)$. Since semilattices and square bands embed in partition monoids and $\P_X\times\P_Y\le\P_{X\sqcup Y}$, a finite normal $*$-band embeds in a partition monoid iff it satisfies \eqref{eq:Q1}.
\end{remark}

%==============================================================================
\section{The digraph conditions and the triangle condition}\label{sec:digraph}

Before the wiring conditions we present a family of necessary conditions of a different shape, indexed by digraphs: $k$ pairwise commuting projections with common product $w$, $k$ pairwise commuting projections with common product $z$, matched crosswise according to the digraph, force $w=z$. The family has a single strongest member, the triangle condition, which implies all the others in every projection algebra, and its witness is a quotient of $\P_3$.

\subsection{The conditions and their validity}\label{subsec:DE}

Let $k\ge3$ and let $E$ be a set of ordered pairs $(i,j)$ of distinct elements of $[k]=\{1,\dots,k\}$, a loopless digraph. For projections $a_1,\dots,a_k,b_1,\dots,b_k$ of a regular $*$-semigroup consider the hypotheses
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(H\arabic*)},leftmargin=3em,nosep]
\item $a_ia_j=a_ja_i=:w$ and $b_ib_j=b_jb_i=:z$ for all $i\ne j$, the products being independent of the pair $\{i,j\}$;
\item $a_ib_i=b_ia_i=:m_i$ for all $i$;
\item $a_i\mt b_j$ for all $(i,j)\in E$,
\end{enumerate}
and let $(\mathrm D_E)$ be the quasi-identity ``(H1)--(H3) imply $w=z$''. By Lemma~\ref{lem:comm} all hypotheses are equations of the projection algebra, so $(\mathrm D_E)$ is a quasi-identity in $2k$ variables. (In (H1) it suffices to assume the ``cyclic'' equations $\theta_{a_2}(a_1)=\theta_{a_3}(a_2)=\theta_{a_1}(a_3)$ and so on: if $w$ denotes the common value, then $\theta_{a_1}(a_2)=a_1a_2a_1=(a_1a_2a_1)^2=a_1(a_2a_1a_2)a_1=a_1wa_1=a_1(a_3a_1a_3)a_1=(a_1a_3a_1)^2=w$, and the $a_i$ pairwise commute with product $w$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:comm}.) Two instances are of particular interest: $k=3$ with $E$ all six pairs, the \emph{hexagon condition}, in which the six matched pairs form the hexagon $a_1\,b_2\,a_3\,b_1\,a_2\,b_3$; and $k=3$ with $E=\{(1,2),(2,3),(3,1)\}$ a directed $3$-cycle, the \emph{triangle condition} \Tri\ (Figure~\ref{fig:tri}), which assumes only $a_1\mt b_2$, $a_2\mt b_3$, $a_3\mt b_1$ and therefore implies the hexagon condition trivially. Call $E$ a \emph{star} if it is empty, or all its edges have the same tail, or all have the same head; $E$ is not a star iff it has two distinct tails and two distinct heads. The hypothesis $k\ge3$ cannot be dropped: for two matched pairs $a_1\mt b_2$, $a_2\mt b_1$ the statement fails already in $\P_2$.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.9]
  \foreach \i/\y in {1/2,2/1,3/0} {
     \node (a\i) at (0,\y) {$a_\i$};
     \node (b\i) at (2.6,\y) {$b_\i$};
     \draw[comm] (a\i) -- (b\i);
  }
  \draw[matched] (a1) -- (b2);
  \draw[matched] (a2) -- (b3);
  \draw[matched] (a3) -- (b1);
  \node (w) at (-1.1,-1.2) {$w$};
  \node (z) at (3.7,-1.2) {$z$};
  \foreach \i in {1,2,3} { \draw[ord] (a\i) -- (w); \draw[ord] (b\i) -- (z); }
  \node at (1.3,-1.9) {$\Longrightarrow\ w=z$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The triangle condition \Tri: the matched pairs $a_i\mt b_j$ follow the directed $3$-cycle $1\to2\to3\to1$ of indices. Double lines are matched pairs, dashed lines commuting pairs; $w=a_ia_j$ and $z=b_ib_j$ for $i\ne j$.}\label{fig:tri}
\end{figure}


The matchings contribute through the following \emph{transport relations}.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:H4}
Under \textup{(H1)}--\textup{(H3)}, for every edge $(i,j)\in E$:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:H4}
a_im_ja_i=w,\qquad b_jm_ib_j=z,\qquad a_iza_i=m_i,\qquad b_jwb_j=m_j .
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
$a_im_ja_i=a_ia_jb_ja_i=a_j(a_ib_ja_i)=a_ja_i=w$; $b_jm_ib_j=b_jb_ia_ib_j=b_i(b_ja_ib_j)=b_ib_j=z$; $a_iza_i=a_ib_jb_ia_i=(a_ib_ja_i)b_i=m_i$; and $b_jwb_j=b_ja_ia_jb_j=(b_ja_ib_j)a_j=m_j$.
\end{proof}

Call $E$ \emph{admissible} if every vertex has an out-edge and an in-edge, and no vertex is incident to all edges. The triangle is admissible; so are the directed cycles $C_\ell$, $\ell\ge3$, and the union of two disjoint $2$-cycles on four vertices.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:DE}
Let $E$ be an admissible digraph on $[k]$. Then $(\mathrm D_E)$ holds in every partition monoid $\P_X$. Moreover, under \textup{(H1)}--\textup{(H3)}, $a_i=b_j$ for all $(i,j)\in E$, and if the bipartite graph $\{a_i\text{---}b_j:(i,j)\in E\}$ is connected then all $2k$ projections coincide.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $A_i=\supp a_i$, $B_i=\supp b_i$, $W=\supp w$, $Z=\supp z$. By admissibility every $i$ has an out-edge and an in-edge, so by Lemma~\ref{lem:H4} for every $i$ there are $j,j'$ with $a_im_ja_i=w$, $a_iza_i=m_i$, $b_im_{j'}b_i=z$ and $b_iwb_i=m_i$.

\emph{Step 0: supports.} By (H1) and Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(b), $A_i\cap A_j=W$ and $B_i\cap B_j=Z$ for $i\ne j$; by (H2), $A_i\cap B_i=\supp m_i$. In particular a point of $X$ lies in at most one $A_i$ unless it lies in $W$ (and then in all of them), and similarly for the $B_i$ and $Z$. Since $k\ge3$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cover}
\text{every } x\notin W\cup Z \text{ lies outside } A_i\cup B_i \text{ for some } i .
\end{equation}

\emph{Step 1: $\varepsilon_w$ and $\varepsilon_z$ agree outside $W\cup Z$.} By \eqref{eq:H4} and Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(a), applied to $z=\theta_{b_i}(m_{j'})$, $w=\theta_{a_i}(m_j)$, $m_i=\theta_{a_i}(z)$ and $m_i=\theta_{b_i}(w)$, for every $i$: the blocks of $z$ not contained in $B_i$ are exactly the $\N(b_i)$-blocks; the blocks of $w$ not contained in $A_i$ are exactly the $\N(a_i)$-blocks; and the blocks of $m_i$ not contained in $A_i$ (resp.\ $B_i$) are exactly the $\N(a_i)$-blocks (resp.\ $\N(b_i)$-blocks). Consequently, for $x\notin A_i\cup B_i$, the block of $z$ containing $x$ is the $\N(b_i)$-block of $x$, which is the block of $m_i$ containing $x$, which is the $\N(a_i)$-block of $x$, which is the block of $w$ containing $x$. By \eqref{eq:cover}, $\varepsilon_w$ and $\varepsilon_z$ have the same block at every point of $X\setminus(W\cup Z)$; moreover such a common block is an $\N(a_i)$-block and an $\N(b_i)$-block, hence contained in $X\setminus(A_i\cup B_i)\subseteq X\setminus(W\cup Z)$. It follows that a block of $w$ or of $z$ is either a common block contained in $X\setminus(W\cup Z)$, or is contained in $W\cup Z$.

\emph{Step 2: the $\T(w)$- and $\T(z)$-blocks correspond.} Fix an edge $(i,j)\in E$. From (H1) and \eqref{eq:H4}, $wzw=a_i(a_jza_j)a_i=a_im_ja_i=w$ and $zwz=b_j(b_iwb_i)b_j=b_jm_ib_j=z$, so $w\mt z$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(c), every class of $\varepsilon_w\vee\varepsilon_z$ meeting $W\cup Z$ contains exactly one $\T(w)$-block and exactly one $\T(z)$-block. Common blocks are classes by themselves, so the class of a $\T(w)$-block $\Omega$ consists of blocks contained in $W\cup Z$: these are $\T(w)$-blocks, $\N(w)$-blocks contained in $Z\setminus W$, $\T(z)$-blocks and $\N(z)$-blocks contained in $W\setminus Z$. An $\N(z)$-block contained in $W$ meets only $\T(w)$-blocks, so it meets only one of them (two would lie in a common class); hence every $\N(z)$-block contained in $W$ lies inside a single $\T(w)$-block, and dually. Therefore the class of $\Omega$ consists of $\Omega$, the $\N(z)$-blocks inside $\Omega$, the $\T(z)$-blocks meeting $\Omega$ and the $\N(w)$-blocks inside those, and since it contains exactly one $\T(z)$-block, \emph{there is exactly one $\T(z)$-block $Z_\Omega$ meeting $\Omega$}, and dually exactly one $\T(w)$-block $W_\Theta$ meeting a given $\T(z)$-block $\Theta$.

\emph{Step 3: $\Omega\subseteq Z_\Omega$.} Suppose $y\in\Omega\setminus Z_\Omega$. Then $y\notin Z$ (a $\T(z)$-block containing $y$ would meet $\Omega$, hence be $Z_\Omega$), so the block $N$ of $z$ containing $y$ is an $\N(z)$-block, and by Step~2, $N\subseteq\Omega\subseteq W\subseteq A_1\cap\dots\cap A_k$. For each $i$, $N$ is a block of $z=\theta_{b_i}(m_{j'})$, so by Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(a) either $N\subseteq B_i$ or $N$ is an $\N(b_i)$-block. As $N\cap Z=\emptyset$ and $B_i\cap B_l=Z$, $N\subseteq B_i$ holds for at most one index $i=m$; by admissibility there is an edge $(i,j)\in E$ with $m\notin\{i,j\}$, so that $N$ is both an $\N(b_i)$-block and an $\N(b_j)$-block. Since $N\subseteq A_i$ and $a_ib_i=b_ia_i$, Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(b) shows that $N$ is a union of $\T(a_i)$-blocks; let $\tau$ be one of them. Now $a_i\mt b_j$ by (H3). The class of $\varepsilon_{a_i}\vee\varepsilon_{b_j}$ containing $\tau$ contains the $b_j$-block $N\supseteq\tau$, hence all $\T(a_i)$-blocks inside $N$; by Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(c) there is only one, so $N=\tau$. But then $N$ is simultaneously a $\T(a_i)$-block and an $\N(b_j)$-block, which is impossible for a matched pair by Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(c). Hence $\Omega\subseteq Z_\Omega$.

\emph{Step 4: conclusion.} Dually $Z_\Omega\subseteq W_{Z_\Omega}$ (with the roles of $a$ and $b$ exchanged: an $\N(w)$-block $N\subseteq Z\subseteq B_j$ is a union of $\T(b_j)$-blocks because $b_j$ commutes with $a_j$, and $b_j\mt a_i$); since $\Omega$ meets $Z_\Omega$ and $W_{Z_\Omega}$ is the only $\T(w)$-block meeting $Z_\Omega$, we get $W_{Z_\Omega}=\Omega$ and so $\Omega=Z_\Omega$. Thus $\T(w)=\T(z)$ and $W=Z$. Inside $W=Z$ both $\varepsilon_w$ and $\varepsilon_z$ consist of these $\T$-blocks, and outside $W\cup Z$ they agree by Step~1. Hence $w=z$.

Finally, $w=z$ is a common lower bound of $a_i$ and $b_j$, so by \eqref{eq:Q1} $a_ib_j=b_ja_i$ for all $i,j$; for $(i,j)\in E$ this gives $a_i=a_ib_ja_i=a_ib_j$, i.e.\ $a_i\le b_j$, and dually $b_j\le a_i$, so $a_i=b_j$. If the bipartite graph of $E$ is connected, all $2k$ projections coincide.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The triangle condition is a master condition}\label{subsec:master}

In this subsection $a_1,\dots,a_k,b_1,\dots,b_k$ are projections of a regular $*$-semigroup satisfying (H1), (H2) for some $k\ge2$, and (H3) for a digraph $E$ when so stated. We show that the matchings of $E$ serve only to establish transport relations, and that once these are available, matchings can be manufactured at will, so that the triangle condition implies $(\mathrm D_E)$ for every non-star $E$. We shall use repeatedly that $\theta_p$ is monotone in every projection algebra: $x\le y$ implies $\theta_p(x)\le\theta_p(y)$, since in the binary notation the axioms give $(x*y)*(z*y)=x*(z*y)$ and $(x*y)*(y*z)=(x*y)*z$, whence $x*y=x$ implies $(x*p)*(y*p)=x*(y*p)=(x*y)*(y*p)=(x*y)*p=x*p$.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:rev}
If $za_iz=z$ then $b_jm_ib_j=z$ for all $j\ne i$; if $wb_iw=w$ then $a_jm_ia_j=w$ for all $j\ne i$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Put $e=b_jm_ib_j$. Writing $m_i=b_ia_i$ and $b_jb_i=z$ gives $e=za_ib_j$; writing $m_i=a_ib_i$ gives $e=b_ja_iz$. Hence $e=ze=ez$, so $e\le z$. Conversely $zez=zm_iz=za_iz=z$; so $z\le e$ and $e=z$. Dually for $w$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[bounded elements]\label{lem:bounded}
Let $e\le a_i$, $e'\le a_j$ with $i\ne j$, and $w\le e,e'$. Then $ee'=e'e=w$. Dually two elements under distinct $b$'s and above $z$ commute with product $z$. If $e\le a_i$, $f\le b_i$ and $m_i\le e,f$, then $ef=fe=m_i$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
$ee'e=e(a_ia_j)e'(a_ja_i)e=ewe'we=ewe=w$, and symmetrically $e'ee'=w$; so $ee'=e'e=w$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:comm}. Likewise $efe=e(a_ib_i)f(b_ia_i)e=em_ifm_ie=m_i=fef$.
\end{proof}

Call the family \emph{transported} if the four relations
\begin{equation}\label{eq:transport}
a_iza_i=m_i,\qquad b_iwb_i=m_i,\qquad za_iz=z,\qquad wb_iw=w
\end{equation}
hold at every index $i$. In a transported family $w\mt z$ (as $wzw=a_i(a_jza_j)a_i=a_im_ja_i=w$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:rev}, and dually), and $b_jm_ib_j=z$, $a_jm_ia_j=w$ for all $i\ne j$.

\begin{theorem}[non-star patterns]\label{thm:nonstar}
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item If $E$ is not a star, then \textup{(H1)}--\textup{(H3)} imply, in every projection algebra, that \eqref{eq:transport} holds at every non-isolated index $i$ of $E$; moreover each isolated index $i$ can be replaced by the pair $a_i':=\theta_{a_i}(b_{j_0})$, $b_i':=\theta_{b_i}(a_{j_0})$, where $j_0$ is any non-isolated index, after which \textup{(H1)}--\textup{(H3)} still hold with the same $w,z$ and the family is transported.
\item If $E$ is a star, then $\P_2$ contains projections satisfying \textup{(H1)}--\textup{(H3)} for $E$ with $w\ne z$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(a) For an index $i$ with an out-edge $(i,l)$ we have $a_iza_i=a_ib_lb_ia_i=(a_ib_la_i)b_i=m_i$ and $za_iz=zb_la_ib_lz=z$; dually an in-edge $(l,i)$ gives $b_iwb_i=m_i$ and $wb_iw=w$.

\emph{Step 1: $b_iwb_i=m_i$ at every tail $i$.} Let $i\ne i'$ be tails. Since $w\le a_i$, $wb_iw=wa_ib_ia_iw=wm_iw=w(a_iza_i)w=wzw$, and likewise $wb_{i'}w=wzw$. Hence, $b_iwb_i$ being a projection,
\begin{align*}
b_iwb_i&=b_i(wb_iw)b_i=b_i(wb_{i'}w)b_i=b_ia_ia_{i'}b_{i'}a_{i'}a_ib_i=m_i\,m_{i'}\,m_i\\
&=a_ib_i\,(b_{i'}a_{i'}b_{i'})\,b_ia_i=a_iza_{i'}za_i=a_iza_i=m_i ,
\end{align*}
using $b_ib_{i'}=z$, $za_{i'}z=z$ and $a_iza_i=m_i$. Dually $a_jza_j=m_j$ at every head $j$, since there are two distinct heads.

\emph{Step 2: $w\mt z$.} Choose a tail $i$ and a head $j$ with $i\ne j$. Then $wzw=a_ia_jza_ja_i=a_im_ja_i=a_ia_jb_ja_ja_i=wb_jw=w$ by Step~1 and the in-edge at $j$, and $zwz=b_jb_iwb_ib_j=b_jm_ib_j=b_jb_ia_ib_ib_j=za_iz=z$ by Step~1 and the out-edge at $i$.

\emph{Step 3: the remaining relations.} At a tail $i$ we now have $a_iza_i=m_i$, $za_iz=z$ and $b_iwb_i=m_i$, and $wb_iw=w$ follows from $w\mt z$ by monotonicity: $z\le b_i$ gives $w=wzw\le wb_iw\le w$. Dually at a head. So \eqref{eq:transport} holds at every non-isolated index.

\emph{Step 4: isolated indices.} Let $i$ be isolated and $j_0$ non-isolated. Since $w\le a_i$, monotonicity gives $z=zwz\le za_iz\le z$, so $za_iz=z$, and dually $wb_iw=w$; by Lemma~\ref{lem:rev}, $b_lm_ib_l=z$ and $a_lm_ia_l=w$ for $l\ne i$. The other two relations of \eqref{eq:transport} may fail at $i$, but the two sandwiches are equal: $u_i:=a_iza_i=b_iwb_i=m_im_{j_0}m_i$. Indeed $z=b_im_{j_0}b_i$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:rev} at $j_0$), and writing $m_{j_0}=a_{j_0}b_{j_0}$, $b_{j_0}b_i=z$ in one copy and $m_{j_0}=b_{j_0}a_{j_0}$ in the other, $z=b_ia_{j_0}z=za_{j_0}b_i$; hence $a_iza_i=a_i(b_ia_{j_0}z)(za_{j_0}b_i)a_i=m_i(a_{j_0}za_{j_0})m_i=m_im_{j_0}m_i$, and dually $b_iwb_i=m_i(b_{j_0}wb_{j_0})m_i=m_im_{j_0}m_i$. Thus $u_i=\theta_{m_i}(m_{j_0})\le m_i$, and for $l\ne i$ we get $b_lu_ib_l=z$: it is $\le b_lm_ib_l=z$ by monotonicity, and $z(b_lu_ib_l)z=zu_iz=za_iza_iz=z$; dually $a_lu_ia_l=w$.

Put $a_i'=a_ib_{j_0}a_i$ and $b_i'=b_ia_{j_0}b_i$. Then $a_i'\le a_i$ and $w\le a_i'$, since $a_i'wa_i'=a_i(b_{j_0}wb_{j_0})a_i=a_im_{j_0}a_i=w$; dually $z\le b_i'\le b_i$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:bounded}, $a_i'$ commutes with every $a_l$ ($l\ne i$), and with $a_l'$ if $l$ is also isolated, with product $w$, and dually for $b_i'$: (H1) holds for the new family, with the same $w$ and $z$. For (H2),
\[
a_i'b_i'a_i'=a_ib_{j_0}(m_ia_{j_0}m_i)b_{j_0}a_i=a_ib_{j_0}\bigl(b_i(a_ia_{j_0}a_i)b_i\bigr)b_{j_0}a_i=a_i(b_{j_0}u_ib_{j_0})a_i=a_iza_i=u_i ,
\]
and dually $b_i'a_i'b_i'=b_iwb_i=u_i$; so $a_i'b_i'=b_i'a_i'=u_i=:m_i'$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:comm}. (H3) is unaffected, as $i$ lies on no edge. Finally \eqref{eq:transport} holds at $i$ for the new pair: $a_i'za_i'=a_ib_{j_0}(a_iza_i)b_{j_0}a_i=a_i(b_{j_0}u_ib_{j_0})a_i=u_i=m_i'$, dually $b_i'wb_i'=m_i'$, and $za_i'z=z$, $wb_i'w=w$ as before since $w\le a_i'$, $z\le b_i'$. The relations at the other indices only involve $a_l,b_l,m_l,w,z$ for $l\ne i$ and are unchanged.

(b) In $\P_2$ let $F$ be the projection with the single block $\{1,2,1',2'\}$ (rank $1$) and $e$ the projection with blocks $\{1,2\}$, $\{1',2'\}$ (rank $0$); then $e\le F$ and $F\mt F$. For an out-star with tail $v$ (or for $E=\emptyset$) take $a_v=F$, $a_i=e$ for $i\ne v$, and $b_j=F$ for all $j$: then $w=e$, $z=F$, all pairs commute, $a_v\mt b_j$ for all $j$, and $w\ne z$. For an in-star with head $v$ take $a_i=F$ for all $i$, $b_v=F$ and $b_j=e$ for $j\ne v$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[edge creation]\label{lem:create}
Let the family be transported, $k\ge2$, and $i\ne j$. Put $a_i'=\theta_{a_i}(b_j)$, $b_j'=\theta_{b_j}(a_i)$, and replace $a_i$ by $a_i'$ and $b_j$ by $b_j'$. The new family is again transported, with the same $w$, $z$ and $m_1,\dots,m_k$; $a_i'\mt b_j'$; and every pair $a_l,b_m$ with $l\ne i$, $m\ne j$ is unchanged.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
$a_i'\le a_i$, and $w,m_i\le a_i'$: indeed $a_i'wa_i'=a_i(b_jwb_j)a_i=a_im_ja_i=w$ and $a_i'm_ia_i'=a_i(b_jm_ib_j)a_i=a_iza_i=m_i$. Dually $b_j'\le b_j$ and $z,m_j\le b_j'$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:bounded}, $a_i'$ commutes with every $a_l$ ($l\ne i$) with product $w$, and with $b_i$ with product $m_i$; dually for $b_j'$; so (H1), (H2) hold with the same $w,z,m_l$. The transport relations at $i$: $a_i'za_i'=a_i(b_jm_ib_j)a_i=m_i$, $b_iwb_i=m_i$ is unchanged, $za_i'z=z$ since $z=zwz\le za_i'z\le z$ by monotonicity ($w\le a_i'$), and $wb_iw=w$ is unchanged; dually at $j$; the other indices are untouched. Finally $a_i'\mt b_j'$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:friends}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[master condition]\label{thm:master}
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item Every transported family on $k\ge3$ indices yields, on any three of its indices, a triangle configuration with the same $w$ and $z$. Consequently, in every projection algebra, the triangle condition implies $(\mathrm D_E)$ for every non-star digraph $E$.
\item For every set $X$ with $|X|\ge2$, $(\mathrm D_E)$ holds in $\P_X$ iff $E$ is not a star.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(a) Apply Lemma~\ref{lem:create} three times on the indices $1,2,3$: create $(1,2)$, replacing $a_1,b_2$; then $(2,3)$, replacing $a_2,b_3$; then $(3,1)$, replacing $a_3,b_1$. Each step leaves the pairs created before untouched, since they involve different elements: after the three steps $a_1'\mt b_2'$, $a_2'\mt b_3'$, $a_3'\mt b_1'$, and (H1), (H2) hold with the original $w,z$. For the consequence, take a configuration for a non-star $E$, replace its isolated indices (Theorem~\ref{thm:nonstar}(a)), obtaining a transported family with the same $w,z$, and build the triangle configuration; the triangle condition gives $w=z$. (b) \Tri\ holds in $\P_X$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:DE}, so the non-star conditions hold by (a); the stars fail already in $\P_2\le\P_X$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:nonstar}(b).
\end{proof}

Thus only the triangle needs the combinatorial proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:DE}; every other non-star instance follows from it algebraically. The hexagon condition, which was the original form of the theorem, is equivalent to the triangle condition on finite projection algebras, but for a subtle reason: no fixed sextuple of terms in $a_1,\dots,b_3$ satisfies the hexagon hypotheses in every triangle configuration (in the initial model of the Horn theory ``axioms plus triangle hypotheses'' neither $w=z$ nor any such hypothesis holds), and the six projections to which the hexagon condition is applied are found by an iteration.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:equiv}
Let $P$ be a finite projection algebra satisfying the hexagon condition, and let $a_1,\dots,a_k,b_1,\dots,b_k$ be a transported family in $P$, $k\ge3$. Then $w=z$. In particular, on finite projection algebras the hexagon condition is equivalent to the triangle condition and to $(\mathrm D_E)$ for every non-star $E$ on three vertices.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Call a projection $x$ \emph{bounded} if $x\le a_i$ and $w,m_i\le x$ for some $i$, or $x\le b_j$ and $z,m_j\le x$ for some $j$; the $2k$ vertices are bounded. Fix three indices, say $1,2,3$, and for $x\le a_1$ consider the \emph{walk}
\[
x\ \xrightarrow{\ \theta_{b_3}\ }\ x_1\ \xrightarrow{\ \theta_{a_2}\ }\ x_2\ \xrightarrow{\ \theta_{b_1}\ }\ x_3\ \xrightarrow{\ \theta_{a_3}\ }\ x_4\ \xrightarrow{\ \theta_{b_2}\ }\ x_5\ \xrightarrow{\ \theta_{a_1}\ }\ x_6=:\Phi(x)\le a_1 ,
\]
whose elements lie under $b_3,a_2,b_1,a_3,b_2,a_1$ respectively. (i) \emph{The walk preserves boundedness.} Let $x\le a_i$ be bounded and $y=\theta_b(x)$ with $b=b_{i-1}$ (indices mod~$3$). Since $z\le b$, $yzy=b\,x(bzb)x\,b=b(xzx)b$, and $xzx=x(a_iza_i)x=xm_ix=m_i$, so $yzy=bm_ib=z$, i.e.\ $z\le y$; similarly $ym_{i-1}y=b(xm_{i-1}x)b=b(x(a_im_{i-1}a_i)x)b=b(xwx)b=bwb=m_{i-1}$, using the transport relations $a_im_{i-1}a_i=w$ (Lemma~\ref{lem:rev}) and $b_{i-1}wb_{i-1}=m_{i-1}$. The step $y\le b_j\mapsto x'=\theta_{a_{j-1}}(y)$ is the same computation with $a$ and $b$ exchanged. (ii) \emph{Stabilisation.} If $x_0\to x_1=\theta_{p_1}(x_0)\to\dots\to x_n=\theta_{p_n}(x_{n-1})=x_0$ is a closed walk of projections in a regular $*$-semigroup $S$ with finitely many projections, then $x_{j-1}\mt x_j$ for all $j$. Indeed, put $s_j=x_{j-1}p_j$, so that $s_j^*s_j=x_j$ and $s_js_j^*=\theta_{x_{j-1}}(p_j)\le x_{j-1}$, and $s=s_1s_2\cdots s_n$. Since $s_j^*s_js_{j+1}=x_js_{j+1}=s_{j+1}$, we get $s^*s=x_n=x_0$, and $ss^*\le s_1s_1^*=\theta_{x_0}(p_1)\le x_0$. Now $t\mapsto s^*ts$ is an order isomorphism from the projections below $ss^*$ onto the projections below $s^*s$; as $ss^*\le s^*s$ and there are finitely many projections, $ss^*=s^*s$. Hence $\theta_{x_0}(p_1)=x_0$, so $x_0x_1x_0=(x_0p_1x_0)(p_1x_0)=x_0$, while $x_1x_0x_1=x_1$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:friends}; rotating the cycle gives the other pairs. (iii) By Imaoka's theorem $P=\PA(S)$ for a regular $*$-semigroup $S$. The operator $\Phi$ is a composite of maps $\theta_p$, hence monotone, and $\Phi(a_1)\le a_1$; so $a_1\ge\Phi(a_1)\ge\Phi^2(a_1)\ge\cdots$ is eventually constant, and there is $x^*=\Phi^n(a_1)$ with $\Phi(x^*)=x^*$. By (i) all elements of the walk from $x^*$ are bounded, and the walk $x^*\to x_1\to\dots\to x_6=x^*$ is closed, so by (ii) consecutive elements are matched. Put $A_3=x_1\le b_3$, $A_1=x_3\le b_1$, $A_2=x_5\le b_2$, $B_2=x_2\le a_2$, $B_3=x_4\le a_3$, $B_1=x_6\le a_1$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:bounded} the $A_j$ pairwise commute with product $z$, the $B_i$ with product $w$, and $A_iB_i=B_iA_i$; the six consecutive matchings are exactly the matchings $A_i\mt B_j$, $i\ne j$. So $(B_1,B_2,B_3\mid A_1,A_2,A_3)$ satisfies the hexagon hypotheses with products $w$ and $z$, and the hexagon condition gives $w=z$. The last assertion follows with Theorem~\ref{thm:nonstar}(a), since every $E$ on three vertices has fewer hypotheses than the hexagon.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:hinf}
The finiteness in Proposition~\ref{prop:equiv} cannot be dropped. ``Unrolling'' the hexagon of the witness $\PA(Q_0)$ below along its cycle of matchings, replacing each vertex by a descending chain $e_{i,0}>e_{i,1}>\cdots$, $f_{i,0}>f_{i,1}>\cdots$ with all matchings taken level by level except one, which shifts the level by one, produces a countable projection algebra $H_\infty$ satisfying \eqref{eq:Q1} and the hexagon condition (and $(\mathrm D_E)$ for every non-star $E$ on $k\ge4$ vertices) in which $(e_{1,0},e_{2,0},e_{3,0}\mid f_{1,0},f_{2,0},f_{3,0})$ is a triangle configuration with $w\ne z$. So the hexagon condition does not imply the triangle condition in the variety of projection algebras, and a regular $*$-semigroup with projection algebra $H_\infty$ satisfies \eqref{eq:Q1} and the hexagon condition but embeds in no partition monoid. In $H_\infty$ the walk of the proof closes after one turn but the stabilisation step (ii) fails. The verification of the axioms for $H_\infty$ is a finite computation (the operation is determined by the types and levels of its arguments, with level shifts bounded by the depth of the terms involved); we omit it.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The witness}\label{subsec:Q0}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:witness}
Let $\mu$ be the relation on $\P_3$ identifying two elements of rank $\le1$ iff they have the same kernel and the same cokernel (the partitions of $\{1,2,3\}$ induced by their upper and lower blocks). Then $\mu$ is a $*$-congruence, $\P_3/\mu$ has $103$ elements, and the $*$-subsemigroup $Q_0$ of $\P_3/\mu$ generated by the images of the six projections of rank $2$ has $61$ elements, satisfies \eqref{eq:Q1}, and embeds in no partition monoid; its projection algebra $\PA(Q_0)$ has $11$ elements and violates the triangle condition.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
That $\mu$ is a $*$-congruence (it is the congruence that collapses the ideal of rank $\le1$ onto the $5\times5$ square band $\Part_3\times\Part_3$ of pairs of partitions of $\{1,2,3\}$, one of the congruences below the Rees congruence of that ideal in the classification of \cite{EMRT}), that the quotients satisfy \eqref{eq:Q1} and the element counts were checked by direct computation. Write $a_i$ for the image of the rank-$2$ projection of $\P_3$ whose transversal blocks are $\{i\}$ and $\{1,2,3\}\setminus\{i\}$, and $b_i$ for the image of the rank-$2$ projection whose transversal blocks are the two singletons $\{j\}$, $\{k\}$ ($\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}$) and whose nontransversal block is $\{i\}$. In $\P_3/\mu$ the hypotheses (H1)--(H3) hold with $E$ the set of all six pairs: $w$ is the class of the partition of $\{1,2,3\}$ into one block, $z$ that of the discrete partition, $m_i$ that of the partition $\{i\}\mid\{j,k\}$, and the products $a_ib_j$ ($i\ne j$) have rank $2$; all this is a direct verification in $\P_3$. But $w\ne z$, since the two partitions are different. By Theorem~\ref{thm:DE} no $*$-homomorphism $Q_0\to\P_X$ is injective on $\{w,z\}$. The projection algebra $\PA(Q_0)$ consists of $a_1,\dots,b_3$ and the five ``low'' elements $w,z,m_1,m_2,m_3$, with $\theta_\ell(x)=\ell$ for every low $\ell$, $\theta_{a_i}(w)=w$, $\theta_{a_i}(z)=\theta_{a_i}(m_i)=m_i$, $\theta_{a_i}(m_j)=w$ ($j\ne i$), and dually for the $b_i$.
\end{proof}

Concretely, $Q_0=D\cup(\Part_3\times\Part_3)$, where $D$ is the rank-$2$ $\D$-class of $\P_3$ with its $\Hh$-classes collapsed, a $6\times6$ trace with trivial groups whose zero pattern is the $6$-cycle $a_1b_2a_3b_1a_2b_3$ of matched pairs. The obstruction lives in the projection algebra: $\PA(Q_0)$ is a minimal non-embeddable projection algebra (its six maximal proper subalgebras, obtained by omitting one of $a_i$, $b_i$, embed in $\PA(\P_4)$), and it is the smallest projection algebra satisfying \eqref{eq:Q1} and violating \Tri: a Mace4 search shows that no projection algebra of order $\le10$ does, and that the one of order $11$ is $\PA(Q_0)$ (Section~\ref{sec:comp}). The analogous quotients of $\P_4$ contain those of $\P_3$ and give nothing new; the further obstructions of the next section were found instead by enumerating small projection algebras.

%==============================================================================
\section{Wiring conditions}\label{sec:wiring}

\subsection{The shape of the conditions}\label{subsec:shape}

All the conditions of this section are statements about a matched path $a\mt c\mt b$, with the conclusion $a\mt b$, under hypotheses on one, two or three auxiliary projections. In $\P_X$ the hypotheses $a\mt c$, $c\mt b$ pair the $\T$-blocks $A_i\leftrightarrow C_i\leftrightarrow B_i$ through the classes $\Gamma_i$ and $\Delta_i$, but a $\T(a)$-block and a $\T(b)$-block in corresponding classes need not meet or even be connected through $a$ and $b$: the matching does not transport. The conditions say that it does transport as soon as the auxiliaries are wired to the path in one of the following ways, which we name for later reference: an auxiliary $x$ \emph{commutes} with a vertex ($xp=px$); it \emph{lies below} or \emph{above} a vertex; it \emph{sees two vertices alike} ($\theta_x(p)=\theta_x(q)$); it \emph{is seen alike} by two vertices ($\theta_p(x)=\theta_q(x)$); or it is \emph{half-matched} with a vertex ($\theta_p(x)=p$). The conditions are the following; \Wire\ and \Rcond\ are the first two we found, and are consequences of \Qcond\ and \Rp\ respectively (Corollaries~\ref{cor:R} and~\ref{cor:QS}), which is the order in which they are proved below.
\begin{align*}
\Wire:\quad& a\mt c\mt b\mt d,\ cd=dc,\ \theta_a(cd)=\theta_b(cd)\ \Longrightarrow\ a\mt d;\\
\Rcond:\quad& a\mt c\mt b,\ xa=ax,\ xc=cx,\ \theta_x(b)=\theta_x(c)\ \Longrightarrow\ a\mt b;\\
\Vcond:\quad& bcb=b,\ ata\le b,\ ctc\le a\ \Longrightarrow\ ata=btb;\\
\Rp:\quad& a\mt c\mt b,\ xc=cx,\ \theta_x(a)=\theta_x(c),\ ya=ay,\ \theta_x(b)=\theta_y(b)\ \Longrightarrow\ a\mt b;\\
\Pone:\quad& a\mt c\mt b\mt d,\ xa=ax,\ xc=cx,\ \theta_a(x)=\theta_b(x),\ \theta_x(c)=\theta_x(d)\ \Longrightarrow\ a\mt d;\\
\Qcond:\quad& a\mt c\mt b\mt d,\ xa=ax,\ \theta_c(x)=\theta_d(x),\ ya=ay,\ yb=by,\ \theta_y(c)=\theta_y(b)\ \Longrightarrow\ a\mt d;\\
\Kfour:\quad& a\mt c\mt b,\ aya=a,\ b\le z,\ yc=cy,\ \theta_z(y)=\theta_b(a),\ \theta_z(a)=\theta_a(b)\ \Longrightarrow\ a\mt b;\\
\Kzero:\quad& a\mt c\mt b,\ yc=cy,\ \theta_y(b)=\theta_b(a),\ \theta_b(z)=b,\ \theta_z(a)=\theta_z(b),\ \theta_y(z)=\theta_a(b)\ \Longrightarrow\ a\mt b.
\end{align*}
\Wire\ and \Vcond\ look different: in \Wire\ the path has length three and the auxiliary is the product $cd$ of two commuting vertices; \Vcond\ has an order conclusion and a single half-matching hypothesis. But both are of the same kind. \Wire\ is a consequence of \Qcond\ (Corollary~\ref{cor:QS}), and \Vcond\ says that, when $b$ is half-matched with $c$ and the restrictions $\theta_a(t)\le b$ and $\theta_c(t)\le a$ are wired crosswise, the restrictions $\theta_a(t)$ and $\theta_b(t)$ of $t$ coincide; its proof (Theorem~\ref{thm:V}) reduces it by equational reasoning to the statement $\theta_c(\theta_b(t))=\theta_c(t)$, that $c$ sees $t$ and $\theta_b(t)$ alike, which is of the same kind as the hypotheses above. The two conditions \Eone\ and \Esix\ of the census (Section~\ref{subsec:census}) are consequences of \Kzero\ and \Kfour, respectively, together with \eqref{eq:Q1} and the chain condition (W$_2$) (Remark~\ref{rem:E1E6}).

Figure~\ref{fig:wiring} draws the eight conditions in a common vocabulary. Table~\ref{tab:witnesses} lists, for each condition, the projection algebra that violates it and satisfies all the others; these are the nine minimal non-embeddable algebras of Theorem~\ref{thm:main-census}; they are defined in Sections~\ref{subsec:census} and \ref{subsec:graphsg}, and Proposition~\ref{prop:witnesses} records which conditions each of them satisfies.

\input{fig-wiring}

\toprule
condition & variables & witness & order\\
\midrule
\Wire & $4$ & $\mathfrak P$ & $6$\\
\Rcond & $3+1$ & $\mathfrak P_7$ & $7$\\
\Vcond & $4$ & $\mathfrak P_8^7$ & $8$\\
\Rp & $3+2$ & $\mathfrak P_8^3$ & $8$\\

\subsection{The closing lemma}\label{subsec:closing}

Several proofs end in the same way. For a path $a\mt c\mt b$ in $\PA(\P_X)$ consider
\[
\text{(G1)}\quad \varepsilon_c\le\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_b,\qquad\qquad \text{(G2)}\quad\text{no class of }\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_b\vee\varepsilon_c\text{ contains two }\T(c)\text{-blocks.}
\]

\begin{lemma}[closing lemma]\label{lem:closing}
If $a\mt c$, $c\mt b$, \textup{(G1)} and \textup{(G2)} hold in $\P_X$, then $a\mt b$: every class of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_b$ meeting $\supp a\cup\supp b\cup\supp c$ contains exactly the $\T$-blocks $A_i,B_i,C_i$ for one $i$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By (G1), $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_b=\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_b\vee\varepsilon_c\ge\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_c,\ \varepsilon_b\vee\varepsilon_c$. Let $D$ be the class of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_b$ containing $A_i$. Then $\Gamma_i\subseteq D$, so $C_i\subseteq D$, and $\Delta_i\subseteq D$, so $B_i\subseteq D$. If $A_j\subseteq D$ or $B_j\subseteq D$ for some $j\ne i$, then $C_j\subseteq D$ as well, contradicting (G2). The same applies to the class of any $B_i$ or $C_i$, so the classes meeting $\supp a\cup\supp b$ pair the $A$'s with the $B$'s bijectively, and $a\mt b$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(c).
\end{proof}

A second recurring step concerns an auxiliary commuting with both ends of a matched pair: it splits $X$ into a part where the two projections coincide and a part where they are compared through their products with the auxiliary.

\begin{lemma}[commuting auxiliary]\label{lem:commaux}
Let $a\mt c$ in $\P_X$ and let $x$ be a projection with $xa=ax=:e$ and $xc=cx=:f$. Put $Y=X\setminus\supp x$. Then every block of $a$ and of $c$ lies inside $\supp x$ or is an $\N(x)$-block, $\varepsilon_e=\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_a$ and $\varepsilon_f=\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_c$; and with $U_1,\dots,U_t$ the $\T(e)$-blocks and $V_1,\dots,V_{t'}$ the $\T(f)$-blocks:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)}]
\item $A_i\subseteq\supp e$ iff $C_i\subseteq\supp f$, and for the remaining $i$ we have $A_i=C_i\subseteq Y$;
\item an $\N(x)$-block is an $\N(a)$-block iff it is an $\N(c)$-block.
\end{enumerate}
Thus $a$ and $c$ have the same blocks inside $Y$, with the same labels.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
By Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(b) the classes of $\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_a$ are of four types: the $\T(e)$-blocks $U_l$ (each a union of $A$'s and a union of $\T(x)$-blocks), the $\N(a)$-blocks inside $\supp x$ (unions of $\T(x)$-blocks), the $\N(x)$-blocks inside $\supp a$ (unions of $A$'s), and the common $\N$-blocks; and $\varepsilon_e=\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_a$. Likewise for $c$. In particular every block of $a$ and of $c$ lies inside $\supp x$ or inside $Y$; every $A_i$ lies in some $U_l$ or in an $\N(x)$-block, and every $C_i$ in some $V_k$ or in an $\N(x)$-block.
