An $\N(x)$-block $M$ containing a $\T(c)$-block $C_i$ is a union of $C$'s; it is not an $\N(a)$-block (an $\varepsilon_a$-block which is a union of $\varepsilon_c$-blocks would be a class of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_c$ containing $C_i$ and no $A$, contradicting $a\mt c$), so it is a union of $A$'s as well, and the class of $C_i$ in $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_c$, being made of the $A$'s and $C$'s inside $M$, is $A_i=C_i$. Hence if $C_i\not\subseteq\supp f$ then $A_i=C_i\subseteq Y$, so $A_i\not\subseteq\supp e$; by symmetry this gives (a). For (b), an $\N(x)$-block which is an $\N(a)$-block but not an $\N(c)$-block is a union of $C$'s, hence contains some $C_i$ and is then a union of $A$'s, which is absurd.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The condition \Rp\ and its corollary \Rcond}\label{subsec:Rp}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:Rp}
\Rp\ holds in every partition monoid: if $a,b,c,x,y$ are projections of $\P_X$ with
\[
a\mt c,\quad c\mt b,\quad xc=cx,\quad \theta_x(a)=\theta_x(c),\quad ya=ay,\quad \theta_x(b)=\theta_y(b),
\]
then $a\mt b$.
\end{theorem}

The proof has two parts. An algebraic part, valid in every regular $*$-semigroup whose projection algebra satisfies \eqref{eq:Q1}, derives a number of identities from the hypotheses and reduces the theorem to the case $x\le y$; the combinatorial part is a chain argument in $\P_X$ which shows that no class of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_b\vee\varepsilon_c$ contains two $\T(c)$-blocks, followed by a count. Most of the algebraic part was found with Prover9, and the combinatorial statements were found by testing candidates against all instances of the hypotheses in $\PA(\P_4)$.

\begin{proof}
Throughout this proof, $r=\theta_x(b)=\theta_y(b)$, $f=xc=cx$ and $e=ya=ay$; the letters $e,f,r,m$ and the block names $U_l,V_k,\tilde N$ are local to it.

\smallskip
\emph{Step 1: algebraic consequences.} The identities of this step hold in every regular $*$-semigroup satisfying \eqref{eq:Q1}, under the hypotheses of the theorem.

(i) $r\le x$ and $r\le y$, so $xy=yx=:m$ by \eqref{eq:Q1}, and $r\le m$.

(ii) $\theta_x(c)=xcx=xc=f$, so the hypothesis $\theta_x(a)=\theta_x(c)$ reads $xax=f$. Hence $xa=xa\,ax\,xa=(xax)a=fa$ and, applying $*$, $ax=af$.

(iii) \emph{$f\le y$ and $cy=yc=f$.} First, $\theta_a(r)=a(yby)a=(ay)b(ya)=ebe$. Next, for every element $s$, $\theta_x(\theta_e(s))=x\,ya\,s\,ay\,x=m\,asa\,m=\theta_m(\theta_a(s))$, since $xya=ma$ and $ayx=am$. Applied to $s=b$, with $\theta_a(r)=ebe$: $\theta_m(aba)=\theta_x(ebe)=\theta_x(\theta_a(r))=xa\,xbx\,ax=(xax)b(xax)=fbf$, and $fbf=f$ because $f\le c$ and $cbc=c$ (for $q\le c$, $qbq=qc\,b\,cq=q(cbc)q=q$). So $f=\theta_m(aba)\le m\le y$. Now $f$ is a common lower bound of $c$ and $y$, so $cy=yc$ by \eqref{eq:Q1}; the projection $q=cy$ lies below $c$, so $qbq=q$ by the identity just used, and $qbq=c(yby)c=crc=c(xbx)c=(cx)b(xc)=fbf=f$. Thus $cy=yc=f$.

(iv) \emph{$\theta_a(x)=\theta_a(y)=e$ and $e\mt f$.} By (ii) and (iii), $axa=afa=a\,cy\,a=ac\,ya=aca\,y=ay=e$. Using $ay=ya$, $cy=yc$, $aca=a$ and $cac=c$: $efe=ya\,yc\,ya=y\,ac\,ya=y\,aca\,y=e$ and $fef=yc\,ya\,yc=y\,cac\,y=f$.

(v) \emph{$yax=ax$, $xya=xa$, $axb=yab$ and $xab=xcb$.} We have $cbf=cb\,cx=(cbc)x=cx=f$ and, by (ii), $ax=af=acx$; hence $axbf=afbf=a(fbf)=af=ax$. Since $r\le y$, $y\,xbx=yr=r=xbx$; therefore $yax=y\,axbf=y\,a(xbx)c=a\,y(xbx)\,c=a(xbx)c=axbf=ax$. Applying $*$ gives $xay=xa$, i.e.\ $xya=xa$. Next $xb=(xbx)b=rb=(yby)b=yb$, so $axb=ayb=yab$. Finally $xcb=(xax)b=x(axb)=x(yab)=(xya)b=xab$.

(vi) \emph{$y\,ab=y\,acb$.} Multiplying $xab=xcb$ on the left by $a$ and using $axa=e$ and $axc=afc=af$ (as $f\le c$): $e\,b=af\,b$; and $y\,acb=(ya)(cb)=a(yc)b=afb$. So $eb=y\,ab=y\,acb=ecb$.

(vii) \emph{Reduction to $x\le y$.} The projection $m=xy$ satisfies the hypotheses in place of $x$: $mc=xyc=xf=f=fy=cxy=cm$, as $f\le x$ and $f\le y$; $\theta_m(c)=mcm=f$, and $\theta_m(a)=xy\,a\,yx=xex=x(afa)x=(xa)f(ax)=(fa)f(af)=f(afa)f=fef=f$ by (ii) and (iv); and $\theta_m(b)=\theta_m(\theta_x(b))=\theta_m(r)=r=\theta_y(b)$, since $m\le x$ and $r\le m$. As the conclusion does not involve $x$, we assume from now on that $x\le y$.

\smallskip
\emph{Step 2: the setting in $\P_X$.} We use the notation of Section~\ref{subsec:PX}: $A_i$, $C_i$, $B_i$, $\Gamma_i$, $\Delta_i$; for a block $E$ of $c$ we write $\Gamma(E)$ and $\Delta(E)$ for its classes in $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_c$ and $\varepsilon_b\vee\varepsilon_c$. Put $Y=X\setminus\supp y$. We shall show that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Rp-goal}
\text{no class of }\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_b\vee\varepsilon_c\text{ contains two }\T(c)\text{-blocks,}
\end{equation}
and then conclude by a count. Since $A_i,B_i,C_i$ lie in one class of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_b\vee\varepsilon_c$, \eqref{eq:Rp-goal} implies that every class of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_b$ contains at most one $\T(a)$-block and at most one $\T(b)$-block, and that a class containing $A_i$ contains no $\T(b)$-block other than $B_i$.

By Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(b) applied to the commuting pairs $y,a$ and $y,c$ and $x,c$: every block of $a$, of $c$ and of $x$ lies inside $\supp y$ or is an $\N(y)$-block; an $\N(y)$-block is an $\N(a)$-block or a union of $\T(a)$-blocks, and an $\N(c)$-block or a union of $\T(c)$-blocks; every $\N(c)$-block lies inside $\supp x$ (and is then a union of $\T(x)$-blocks) or is an $\N(x)$-block. Since $x\le y$, every block of $x$ is an $\N(y)$-block or a union of $\T(y)$-blocks (Lemma~\ref{lem:order}(a)). The classes of $\varepsilon_y\vee\varepsilon_a$ meeting $\supp y$ are the $\T(e)$-blocks $U_1,\dots,U_t$ (unions of $\T(a)$-blocks and of $\T(y)$-blocks) and the $\N(a)$-blocks inside $\supp y$; the classes of $\varepsilon_y\vee\varepsilon_c$ meeting $\supp y$ are the $\T(f)$-blocks $V_1,\dots,V_{t'}$ and the $\N(c)$-blocks inside $\supp y$.

\emph{Step 3: a second reduction.} Let $M$ be an $\N(y)$-block which is a union of $\T(a)$-blocks. If $M$ were an $\N(c)$-block, it would be a class of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_c$ by itself, containing $\T(a)$-blocks but no $\T(c)$-block, contradicting $a\mt c$; so $M$ is a union of $\T(c)$-blocks, the class of any $A_i\subseteq M$ lies inside $M$ and consists of $\T$-blocks only, and $\Gamma_i=A_i=C_i$. Symmetrically, an $\N(y)$-block which is a union of $\T(c)$-blocks is a union of $\T(a)$-blocks. Let $x',y'$ be obtained from $x,y$ by declaring all these blocks $M$ transversal. We claim that $a,b,c,x',y'$ still satisfy the hypotheses, with $x'\le y'$; as the conclusion does not change, we may then assume that \emph{every $\N(y)$-block is an $\N(a)$-block and an $\N(c)$-block}, so that all $A_i$ and all $C_i$ lie inside $\supp y$. Indeed, the partitions $\varepsilon_{x'}=\varepsilon_x$, $\varepsilon_{y'}=\varepsilon_y$ are unchanged, and each $M$ is a class by itself in $\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_a$, $\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_c$, $\varepsilon_y\vee\varepsilon_a$ and $\varepsilon_y\vee\varepsilon_c$, being a block of $x$ and of $y$ which is a union of blocks of $a$ and of $c$. Hence $x'c=cx'$, $y'a=ay'$ and $y'c=cy'$ by the converse of Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(b) (if every class of $\varepsilon_p\vee\varepsilon_q$ is of one of the four types, the description of $pq$ in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:PX} shows that $pq$ is symmetric, so $pq=qp$): the class $M$ is now of type (i), the others are unchanged. Further $\theta_{x'}(a)=\theta_{x'}(c)$, because the blocks of these projections are those of $\theta_x(a)=\theta_x(c)$ except that each $M$ becomes a transversal block of both; and $\theta_{x'}(b)=\theta_{y'}(b)$ will follow from Step~4(c) below, whose proof uses only $x\le y$ and $\theta_x(b)=\theta_y(b)$.

\emph{Step 4: the classes of $\varepsilon_y\vee\varepsilon_b$.} Let $\sigma=\varepsilon_y\vee\varepsilon_b$.

(a) \emph{$\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_b=\sigma$.} Let $W$ be a block of $x$ which is a union of $\T(y)$-blocks. If $W$ is a $\T(x)$-block, let $\tilde K$ be its class in $\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_b$; then $\tilde K\cap\supp x$ is a block of $\theta_x(b)=\theta_y(b)$ contained in $\supp y$, hence equal to $K\cap\supp y$ for a class $K$ of $\sigma$, and $W\subseteq K$. If $W$ is an $\N(x)$-block, it is itself a block of $\theta_x(b)=\theta_y(b)$ contained in $\supp y$, so again $W=K\cap\supp y$ for a class $K$ of $\sigma$. The remaining blocks of $x$ are $\N(y)$-blocks. So every block of $x$ lies in a single class of $\sigma$, i.e.\ $\varepsilon_x\le\sigma$, and $\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_b=\sigma$.

(b) \emph{Two kinds of classes.} By (a) and the two descriptions of $r$, for every class $K$ of $\sigma$ meeting $\supp y$ either $K\cap\supp y=\tilde N$ is an $\N(x)$-block (a union of $\T(y)$-blocks), and then $K\cap\supp b=\emptyset$ since $\tilde N$ is a non-transversal block of $\theta_x(b)$; or $K\cap\supp y=K\cap\supp x$. We call the $\N(x)$-blocks inside $\supp y$ the \emph{$\tilde N$-blocks}; each $\tilde N$-block is $K\cap\supp y$ for a class $K$ of $\sigma$ with $K\cap\supp b=\emptyset$, and every other class meeting $\supp y$ has $K\cap\supp y\subseteq\supp x$.

(c) In the situation of Step~3, the blocks of $\theta_{x'}(b)$ and $\theta_{y'}(b)$ are indexed by the same classes $K$ of $\sigma$ (by (a)), and they can differ only for a class $K\supseteq M$, where they are $(K\cap\supp x)\cup M$ and $(K\cap\supp y)\cup M$, both transversal iff $K$ meets $\supp b$. If these differ, then by (b) $K\cap\supp y$ is an $\tilde N$-block and $K\cap\supp b=\emptyset$; but then, for $A_i=C_i\subseteq M$, every block of $b$ meeting $K$ lies in $K$, and every block of $c$ meeting a block $Z$ of $b$ inside $K$ lies in $K$ (a block of $c$ in $Y$ is an $\N(y)$-block, hence lies in a single class of $\sigma$; a block of $c$ inside $\supp y$ meets $Z\cap\supp y\subseteq\tilde N$, so it is $\tilde N$ or contained in it), so a chain from $C_i\subseteq K$ to $B_i$ in $\Delta_i$ stays inside $K$, contradicting $K\cap\supp b=\emptyset$. This completes Step~3.

(d) \emph{Every $\tilde N$-block is an $\N(c)$-block, and its class $\Delta(\tilde N)$ contains no $\T(b)$-block and satisfies $\Delta(\tilde N)\cap\supp y=\tilde N$.} Let $K$ be the class of $\sigma$ with $K\cap\supp y=\tilde N$. As in (c), every block of $b$ or of $c$ reachable by a chain in $\varepsilon_b\vee\varepsilon_c$ from a block of $b$ inside $K$ lies inside $K$. If $\tilde N$ were a union of $\T(c)$-blocks (the alternative, as $x$ and $c$ commute), a block of $b$ meeting some $C_j\subseteq\tilde N$ would lie in $K$, and then $B_j\subseteq\Delta_j\subseteq K$, contradicting $K\cap\supp b=\emptyset$. So $\tilde N$ is an $\N(c)$-block, $\Delta(\tilde N)\subseteq K$, and the claims follow.

\emph{Step 5: the classes of $\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_a$.} By Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(a), the blocks of $\theta_x(a)$ inside $\supp x$ are the sets $K\cap\supp x$ for the classes $K$ of $\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_a$ meeting $\supp x$, transversal iff $K$ meets $\supp a$; those of $\theta_x(c)$ are the $\T(f)$-blocks $V_k$ (transversal) and the $\N(c)$-blocks $\tilde N'$ inside $\supp x$ (non-transversal; they are the classes of $\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_c$ of type (ii)). Since $\theta_x(a)=\theta_x(c)$: for every $k$ there is a class $K_k$ of $\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_a$ with $K_k\cap\supp x=V_k$, and for every $\N(c)$-block $\tilde N'\subseteq\supp x$ a class $K_{\tilde N'}$ with $K_{\tilde N'}\cap\supp x=\tilde N'$ and $K_{\tilde N'}\cap\supp a=\emptyset$. Consequently \emph{every block of $c$ lies inside a single class of $\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_a$}: a $\T(c)$-block lies in some $V_k\subseteq K_k$; an $\N(c)$-block inside $\supp x$ is some $\tilde N'\subseteq K_{\tilde N'}$; and the other $\N(c)$-blocks are $\N(x)$-blocks. Hence $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_c\le\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_a$, and for an $\N(c)$-block $\tilde N'\subseteq\supp x$ we get $\Gamma(\tilde N')\subseteq K_{\tilde N'}$; so \emph{$\Gamma(\tilde N')$ contains no $\T(a)$-block, hence no $\T(c)$-block, and $\tilde N'$ is the only block of $c$ inside $\supp x$ that it contains} (a block of $c$ in $\Gamma(\tilde N')$ inside $\supp x$ lies in $K_{\tilde N'}\cap\supp x=\tilde N'$).

\emph{Step 6: the four kinds of blocks of $c$ and their classes.} By Steps~2--4, every block of $c$ is of exactly one of the following kinds: a $\T(c)$-block $C_i$ (inside $\supp y$, by Step~3); an $\N(c)$-block inside $\supp x$; an $\tilde N$-block; or an $\N(y)$-block inside $Y$, which is an $\N(a)$-block by Step~3 and is therefore a class of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_c$ by itself. Collecting Steps~4(d) and 5:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item[(1)] the blocks of $c$ in $\Gamma_i$ are $C_i$ and $\tilde N$-blocks (an $\N(c)$-block inside $\supp x$ lies in a class without $\T(a)$-blocks, and an $\N(y)$-block in $Y$ is a class by itself);
\item[(2)] the blocks of $c$ in $\Delta_i$ are $C_i$, $\N(c)$-blocks inside $\supp x$ and $\N(y)$-blocks in $Y$ (an $\tilde N$-block lies in a class without $\T(b)$-blocks);
\item[(3)] for an $\N(c)$-block $\tilde N'\subseteq\supp x$, the blocks of $c$ in $\Gamma(\tilde N')$ are $\tilde N'$ and $\tilde N$-blocks, and $\Gamma(\tilde N')$ contains no $\T(a)$-block;
\item[(4)] for an $\tilde N$-block $\tilde N$, the blocks of $c$ in $\Delta(\tilde N)$ are $\tilde N$ and $\N(y)$-blocks in $Y$ (as $\Delta(\tilde N)\cap\supp y=\tilde N$), and $\Delta(\tilde N)$ contains no $\T(b)$-block;
\item[(5)] an $\N(y)$-block $E\subseteq Y$ is a class of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_c$ by itself.
\end{itemize}

\emph{Step 7: proof of \eqref{eq:Rp-goal}.} Two blocks $E,E'$ of $c$ lie in one class of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_b\vee\varepsilon_c$ iff they are joined by a sequence $E=E_0,E_1,\dots,E_n=E'$ of blocks of $c$ in which consecutive blocks lie in a common class of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_c$ or in a common class of $\varepsilon_b\vee\varepsilon_c$: if two blocks $W,W'$ (of $a$, $b$ or $c$) meet at a point $p$, and $E(p)$ is the block of $c$ containing $p$, then $W$ and $E(p)$ lie in a common class of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_c$ or of $\varepsilon_b\vee\varepsilon_c$ (according to whether $W$ is a block of $a$ or of $b$; if $W$ is a block of $c$ then $W=E(p)$), so a chain of blocks meeting consecutively can be replaced by the sequence of the blocks of $c$ at its meeting points. Now start such a sequence at $E_0=C_p$ and use (1)--(5) to see which blocks of $c$ can occur. From $C_p$ one can move, within $\Gamma_p$, only to $\tilde N$-blocks of $\Gamma_p$, and within $\Delta_p$ only to $\N(c)$-blocks $\tilde N'\subseteq\supp x$ of $\Delta_p$ and to $\N(y)$-blocks $E\subseteq Y$ of $\Delta_p$. From an $\tilde N$-block $\tilde N$ of $\Gamma_p$ one can move within $\Gamma(\tilde N)=\Gamma_p$ (back) or within $\Delta(\tilde N)$ to $\N(y)$-blocks $E\subseteq Y$; from such an $E$ one can move within $\Delta(E)=\Delta(\tilde N)$ only, and $\Gamma(E)=\{E\}$. From $\tilde N'\subseteq\supp x$ in $\Delta_p$ one can move within $\Delta(\tilde N')=\Delta_p$ (back) or within $\Gamma(\tilde N')$ to $\tilde N$-blocks of $\Gamma(\tilde N')$, from which one can move within $\Gamma(\tilde N')$ (back) or within their $\Delta$-classes to $\N(y)$-blocks in $Y$, which are dead ends as before. From an $\N(y)$-block $E\subseteq Y$ in $\Delta_p$ one can move within $\Delta_p$ only. So the blocks of $c$ reachable from $C_p$ are: $C_p$; $\tilde N$-blocks (of $\Gamma_p$, or of the classes $\Gamma(\tilde N')$ for $\tilde N'\subseteq\supp x$ in $\Delta_p$); $\N(c)$-blocks inside $\supp x$ lying in $\Delta_p$; and $\N(y)$-blocks in $Y$. None of these is a $\T(c)$-block other than $C_p$, which proves \eqref{eq:Rp-goal}.

\emph{Step 8: counting.} Let $l\in\{1,\dots,t\}$ and $I_l=\{p:A_p\subseteq U_l\}$; by Step~3 every $A_p$ lies in some $U_l$. Consider the class of $U_l$ in $\varepsilon_e\vee\varepsilon_f$ (where $\varepsilon_e=\varepsilon_y\vee\varepsilon_a$, $\varepsilon_f=\varepsilon_y\vee\varepsilon_c$); by $e\mt f$ (Step~1(iv)) it contains exactly one $\T(f)$-block, $V_{k(l)}$ say. Every $\Gamma_p$ is contained in a class of $\varepsilon_e\vee\varepsilon_f$ (its blocks of $a$ and of $c$ are contained in blocks of $e$ and of $f$), so $C_p\subseteq V_{k(l)}$ for $p\in I_l$, and conversely $C_q\subseteq V_{k(l)}$ implies $A_q\subseteq U_l$; thus $I_l=\{q:C_q\subseteq V_{k(l)}\}$. Now compare the two sides of $y\,ab=y\,acb$ (Step~1(vi)). The upper blocks of $ab$ are the $\N(a)$-blocks and the sets $D\cap\supp a$ for the classes $D$ of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_b$ meeting $\supp a$, the latter being transversal iff $D$ meets $\supp b$, with lower part $D\cap\supp b$. By \eqref{eq:Rp-goal}, $D\cap\supp a$ is a single $\T(a)$-block; write $D(A_p)$ for the class of $A_p$. In the product $y\cdot(ab)$ the middle join is $\varepsilon_y\vee\varepsilon_{ab}$, where $\varepsilon_{ab}$ is the upper partition of $ab$; the class of $U_l$ in it is $U_l$ itself, because $U_l$ is a union of $\T(y)$-blocks and of the blocks $A_p$ ($p\in I_l$) of $\varepsilon_{ab}$, and no $\N(a)$-block or $\N(y)$-block meets it. So $y\,ab$ has the upper block $U_l$, with lower part $\bigcup_{p\in I_l}(D(A_p)\cap\supp b)$ (transversal iff this is nonempty). In $y\,acb=(ya)(cb)=e\,(cb)$ the middle join is $\varepsilon_e\vee\varepsilon_c$, whose class containing $U_l$ contains no other $\T(e)$-block and, being contained in the class of $U_l$ in $\varepsilon_e\vee\varepsilon_f$, contains exactly the $\T(c)$-blocks $C_p$ with $p\in I_l$ (all of which it does contain, as $\Gamma_p$ is a chain in $\varepsilon_e\vee\varepsilon_c$); so $e\,cb$ has the upper block $U_l$ with lower part $\bigcup_{p\in I_l}B_p$. Hence
\[
\bigcup_{p\in I_l}\bigl(D(A_p)\cap\supp b\bigr)=\bigcup_{p\in I_l}B_p .
\]
By \eqref{eq:Rp-goal}, $D(A_p)\cap\supp b\subseteq B_p$ for each $p$, and the $B_p$ are pairwise disjoint and nonempty; so $D(A_p)\cap\supp b=B_p$, i.e.\ $B_p\subseteq D(A_p)$, for every $p\in I_l$ and every $l$, that is, for every $p$.

\emph{Conclusion.} Every class of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_b$ meeting $\supp a$ is some $D(A_p)$, which contains exactly one $\T(a)$-block and exactly one $\T(b)$-block ($B_p$) by \eqref{eq:Rp-goal} and Step~8; and every class meeting $\supp b$ contains some $B_p$, hence is $D(A_p)$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(c), $a\mt b$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:Rp}
The conclusion obtained is stronger than $a\mt b$: by \eqref{eq:Rp-goal} and Step~8 the classes of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_b\vee\varepsilon_c$ meeting $\supp a\cup\supp b\cup\supp c$ are exactly the classes containing the triples $A_i,B_i,C_i$, so that the three matchings are coherent and $ab=acb$. The count of Step~8 alone cannot replace \eqref{eq:Rp-goal}: it is blind to the merging of two $\T(a)$-blocks of the same $U_l$ into one class of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_b$, which is exactly what happens in the witness $\mathfrak P_8^3$.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:R}
\Rcond\ holds in every projection algebra satisfying \Rp; in particular in every $\PA(\P_X)$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Given the hypotheses of \Rcond, put $x'=xc=cx$ and $y'=x$. Then $x'\le c$, so $x'c=cx'$; $\theta_{x'}(a)=cx\,a\,xc=c(xax)c=c(xa)c=x(cac)=xc=\theta_{x'}(c)$, using $xa=ax$, $xc=cx$ and $cac=c$; $y'=x$ commutes with $a$; and $\theta_{x'}(b)=c\,\theta_x(b)\,c=c\,\theta_x(c)\,c=cxc=xc=\theta_x(c)=\theta_x(b)=\theta_{y'}(b)$. So \Rp\ gives $a\mt b$. (The substitution was found by Prover9.)
\end{proof}

\subsection{The condition \Vcond}\label{subsec:V}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:V}
\Vcond\ holds in every partition monoid: if $a,b,c,t$ are projections of $\P_X$ with $bcb=b$, $ata\le b$ and $ctc\le a$, then $ata=btb$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $z=ata$, $w=ctc$, $s=btb$ (letters local to this proof). We first reduce the claim, by computations valid in every projection algebra satisfying \eqref{eq:Q1}, to a single statement about $\P_X$.

\smallskip
\emph{Step 1: three identities.} (i) \emph{If $k\le a$ and $ab=ba$ then $bkb\le a$}: $a(bkb)a=(ab)k(ba)=(ba)k(ab)=b(aka)b=bkb$. (ii) \emph{If $ata\le b$ then $ab=ba$ and $ata=ete$, where $e=ab$}: $z=ata$ lies below $a$ and below $b$, so $a$ and $b$ commute by \eqref{eq:Q1}; $e=ab$ is a projection with $e\le a$, $e\le b$, and $eze=a(bzb)a=aza=z$, so $z\le e$ and $z=eze=e(ata)e=(ea)t(ae)=ete$. (iii) \emph{If $bcb=b$ then $s=b(csc)b$}: $s=btb=(bcb)t(bcb)=b\,c(btb)c\,b$.

\emph{Step 2: reduction.} Suppose we know that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lemD}
csc=w,\qquad\text{i.e.}\qquad \theta_c(\theta_b(t))=\theta_c(t) .
\end{equation}
Then $s=b(csc)b=bwb$ by (iii), and $bwb\le a$ by (i), since $w\le a$ and $ab=ba$ (by (ii), as $z\le b$). So $s\le a$ and $s\le b$, hence $s\le e=ab$ and $s=ese=e(btb)e=(eb)t(be)=ete$; and $z=ete$ by (ii). Thus $s=z$. It remains to prove \eqref{eq:lemD} in $\P_X$; in fact we show that $\theta_c(s)$ and $\theta_c(t)$ have the same blocks.

\smallskip
\emph{Step 3: the setting in $\P_X$.} Put $J_a=\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_t$, $J_b=\varepsilon_b\vee\varepsilon_t$, $J_c=\varepsilon_c\vee\varepsilon_t$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(a) the blocks of $z$ are the $\N(a)$-blocks and the sets $Z_K=K\cap\supp a$ for the classes $K$ of $J_a$ meeting $\supp a$, $Z_K$ being transversal iff $K$ meets $\supp t$; similarly the blocks of $w$ are the $\N(c)$-blocks and the sets $W_L=L\cap\supp c$ ($L\in J_c$), and those of $s$ are the $\N(b)$-blocks and the sets $S_H=H\cap\supp b$ ($H\in J_b$). From $w\le a$ and Lemma~\ref{lem:order}(a): every block of $a$ lies in a block of $w$; every block of $w$ is an $\N(a)$-block or a union of $\T(a)$-blocks; and every $\N(a)$-block is an $\N(w)$-block, i.e.\ an $\N(c)$-block or a set $W_L$ with $L\cap\supp t=\emptyset$. From $z\le b$: every block of $b$ lies in a block of $z$; every block of $z$ is an $\N(b)$-block or a union of $\T(b)$-blocks; and every $\N(b)$-block is a block of $z$, non-transversal. From $bcb=b$ and Lemma~\ref{lem:order}(b): every block of $c$ meets at most one $\T(b)$-block, and for every $\T(b)$-block $B$ the class $\Delta(B)$ of $\varepsilon_b\vee\varepsilon_c$ containing $B$ meets $\supp c$. Since blocks of $a$ lie in blocks of $w$, which lie in classes of $J_c$, we have $J_a\le J_c$ (every class of $J_a$ lies in a class of $J_c$); likewise $J_b\le J_a$. For a class $K$ of $J_a$ let $\lambda(K)$ be the class of $J_c$ containing it. Call a block of $c$ \emph{inner} if it meets $\supp a$ and \emph{outer} otherwise. An inner $\T(c)$-block lies in a set $W_L$ which is a union of $\T(a)$-blocks; an inner $\N(c)$-block is a union of $\T(a)$-blocks (being a block of $w$ that is not an $\N(a)$-block); an outer block of $c$ inside a class $L$ of $J_c$ with $W_L\ne\emptyset$ is either a $\T(c)$-block inside $W_L$, $W_L$ being then an $\N(a)$-block, or an $\N(c)$-block which is an $\N(a)$-block.

\emph{Step 4: two facts.} (1) \emph{No $\T(b)$-block lies in a block $M$ which is both an $\N(a)$-block and an $\N(c)$-block.} Otherwise let $B\subseteq M$ be one. The only block of $c$ meeting $B$ is $M$, and the blocks of $b$ meeting $M$ lie inside $M$ ($M$ is a block of $z$, and blocks of $b$ lie in blocks of $z$); so $\Delta(B)=M$ does not meet $\supp c$, contradicting Lemma~\ref{lem:order}(b). Consequently every block which is both an $\N(a)$-block and an $\N(c)$-block is an $\N(b)$-block (a block of $z$ containing no $\T(b)$-block).

(2) \emph{Let $K$ be a class of $J_a$ and $M\subseteq K$ an $\N(a)$-block containing a $\T(b)$-block. Then $M$ is the only block of $z$ inside $K$ that meets $\supp b$; in particular $Z_K$, if nonempty, is an $\N(b)$-block.} By (1) $M$ is not an $\N(c)$-block, so $M=W_L$ with $L=\lambda(K)$, $L\cap\supp t=\emptyset$; this already shows that no other $\N(a)$-block in $K$ contains a $\T(b)$-block (it would also equal $W_{\lambda(K)}$). Suppose $Z_K$ contains a $\T(b)$-block $B$. Every $\T(a)$-block in $K$ lies in $L$ but not in $W_L=M$ (which is disjoint from $\supp a$), hence in an $\N(c)$-block, which is then inner, a union of $\T(a)$-blocks, and contained in $L$. Take a chain $B=B_0,C_1,B_1,C_2,\dots$ from $B$ to a $\T(c)$-block in $\Delta(B)$ (it exists by Lemma~\ref{lem:order}(b)). $C_1$ meets $B\subseteq Z_K\subseteq\supp a$ in a point of a $\T(a)$-block of $K$, so $C_1$ is an inner $\N(c)$-block, $C_1\subseteq L\cap\supp a$; then $B_1$ meets $\supp a$ in a point of a $\T(a)$-block $A_1\subseteq C_1\subseteq L$, whose class $K_1$ satisfies $\lambda(K_1)=L$, and $B_1\subseteq Z_{K_1}$; so $C_2$ meets a $\T(a)$-block of $K_1$, which lies in $L$ but not in $W_L$, and $C_2$ is again an inner $\N(c)$-block. Inductively no block of the chain is a $\T(c)$-block, a contradiction.

\emph{Step 5: the blocks of $\theta_c(s)$ and $\theta_c(t)$ inside $\supp c$.} By Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(a) the blocks of $\theta_c(s)$ are the $\N(c)$-blocks and the sets $\Theta\cap\supp c$ for the classes $\Theta$ of $\varepsilon_c\vee\varepsilon_s$ meeting $\supp c$, transversal iff $\Theta$ meets $\supp s$; the blocks of $\theta_c(t)=w$ are the $\N(c)$-blocks and the $W_L$, transversal iff $L$ meets $\supp t$. Every block of $s$ lies in a class of $J_c$: an $\N(b)$-block is a block of $z$, hence lies in a class of $J_a$, and $S_H\subseteq H$, a class of $J_b\le J_a\le J_c$. Hence $\varepsilon_c\vee\varepsilon_s\le J_c$: every class $\Theta$ lies in a class $L$ of $J_c$, $\Theta\cap\supp c\subseteq W_L$, and if $\Theta$ meets $\supp s$ then $L$ meets $\supp t$ (a block $S_H\subseteq\Theta$ is transversal only if $H\subseteq L$ meets $\supp t$). So \eqref{eq:lemD} follows once we show, for every class $L$ of $J_c$ with $W_L\ne\emptyset$: \emph{(A) any two $\T(c)$-blocks in $W_L$ lie in a common class of $\varepsilon_c\vee\varepsilon_s$, and (B) if $L$ meets $\supp t$ then this class meets $\supp s$.} Fix $L$; then $W_L$ is a union of $\T(a)$-blocks (\emph{type T}) or an $\N(a)$-block $M$ (\emph{type N}, and then $L\cap\supp t=\emptyset$, so (B) is vacuous).

\emph{Type T.} Let $C,C'$ be $\T(c)$-blocks in $W_L$ and take a chain $C=C_0,T_1,C_1,\dots,T_r,C_r=C'$ in $J_c$ (blocks $C_i$ of $c$, blocks $T_i$ of $t$, all inside $L$). We claim that consecutive inner blocks of $c$ on the chain, say $C_i$ and $C_j$ with $C_{i+1},\dots,C_{j-1}$ outer, lie in a common class of $\varepsilon_c\vee\varepsilon_s$; since $C_0$ and $C_r$ are inner ($W_L\subseteq\supp a$), (A) follows. The outer blocks $C_{i+1},\dots,C_{j-1}$ are $\N(c)$-blocks which are $\N(a)$-blocks (the $\T(c)$-blocks of $L$ lie in $W_L\subseteq\supp a$), hence $\N(b)$-blocks by (1); the blocks $T_{i+1},C_{i+1},\dots,T_j$ lie in a single class $K''$ of $J_a$ (each lies in a class, and consecutive ones meet). Let $x\in T_{i+1}\cap C_i$ and $y\in T_j\cap C_j$; both lie in $\supp a$ ($C_i,C_j$ are inner: a $\T(c)$-block inside $W_L$, or an $\N(c)$-block which is a union of $\T(a)$-blocks), so $x,y\in Z_{K''}$. If $Z_{K''}$ is an $\N(b)$-block, it is a block of $s$ meeting $C_i$ and $C_j$. Otherwise $Z_{K''}$ is a union of $\T(b)$-blocks; let $B_x\ni x$, $B_y\ni y$ be the $\T(b)$-blocks; then $B_x,T_{i+1},C_{i+1},T_{i+2},\dots,T_j,B_y$ is a chain in $J_b$, so $B_x,B_y\subseteq H$ for one class $H$ of $J_b$, and the block $S_H\supseteq B_x\cup B_y$ of $s$ meets $C_i$ and $C_j$. This proves the claim. For (B), let $L$ meet $\supp t$, and take a chain $C=C_0,T_1,C_1,\dots,C_q,T_{q+1}$ in $L$ from $C$ to a $\T(t)$-block $T_{q+1}$. Let $C_j$ ($j\le q$) be the last inner block of $c$ on it; then $C_{j+1},\dots,C_q$ are $\N(b)$-blocks as above, and $T_{j+1},C_{j+1},\dots,T_{q+1}$ lie in one class $K'$ of $J_a$, which meets $\supp t$. The point $x\in T_{j+1}\cap C_j$ lies in $Z_{K'}$, a transversal block of $z$, hence a union of $\T(b)$-blocks (an $\N(b)$-block is a non-transversal block of $z$); the $\T(b)$-block $B_x\ni x$ is joined to $T_{q+1}$ by the chain $B_x,T_{j+1},C_{j+1},\dots,C_q,T_{q+1}$ in $J_b$, so its class $H$ meets $\supp t$, $S_H$ is a transversal block of $s$, and $S_H$ meets $C_j$, which lies in the class of $C$ by the claim.

\emph{Type N.} Here $W_L=M$ is an $\N(a)$-block and a block of $z$. If $M$ is an $\N(b)$-block, it is a block of $s$ containing every $\T(c)$-block of $W_L$, and (A) holds. Otherwise $M$ is a union of $\T(b)$-blocks $B_1,\dots,B_k$; each $\T(c)$-block $C\subseteq M$ lies inside one $B_i$ (it is covered by them and meets at most one), so (A) follows if we show that $B_1,\dots,B_k$ lie in a single class $H$ of $J_b$ (then $S_H\supseteq M$). Let $K_0$ be the class of $J_a$ containing $M$. By (2), $M$ is the only block of $z$ inside $K_0$ meeting $\supp b$; so every $\N(a)$-block in $K_0$ other than $M$ is an $\N(b)$-block, and $Z_{K_0}$ is an $\N(b)$-block if nonempty. Hence every block of $b$ inside $K_0$ is one of: a $\T(b)$-block $B_i$; an $\N(a)$-block $M'\ne M$ in $K_0$; or $Z_{K_0}$; and for a point $p\in K_0$ we write $\beta(p)$ for the block of $b$ containing it, which is $Z_{K_0}$ whenever $p\in\supp a$. Let $C\subseteq B_1$ and $C'\subseteq B_2$ be $\T(c)$-blocks and take a chain $C=C_0,T_1,C_1,\dots,T_r,C_r=C'$ in $L$. Every block of $t$ meeting $M$, $Z_{K_0}$ or an $\N(a)$-block in $K_0$ lies in $K_0$; in particular $T_1,T_r\subseteq K_0$. Consider a block $C_l$ of the chain with $T_l\subseteq K_0$, and let $u\in T_l\cap C_l$. If $C_l$ is a $\T(c)$-block, then $C_l\subseteq M$ lies inside one $B_i$, and $\beta(u)=\beta(v)=B_i$ for the point $v\in T_{l+1}\cap C_l$. If $C_l$ is an outer $\N(c)$-block, it is an $\N(a)$-block inside $K_0$ and an $\N(b)$-block by (1), and $\beta(u)=\beta(v)=C_l$. If $C_l$ is an inner $\N(c)$-block, then $u\in\supp a\cap K_0$ and $\beta(u)=Z_{K_0}$; if also $T_{l+1}\subseteq K_0$ then $\beta(v)=Z_{K_0}$; if not, let $T_{l'}$ ($l'>l+1$) be the next block of $t$ on the chain contained in $K_0$ (it exists, as $T_r\subseteq K_0$): the block $C_{l'-1}$ meets $T_{l'-1}\not\subseteq K_0$ and $T_{l'}\subseteq K_0$, so it meets two classes of $J_a$ and is therefore an inner $\N(c)$-block, and the point $v'\in T_{l'}\cap C_{l'-1}$ lies in $\supp a\cap K_0$, so $\beta(v')=Z_{K_0}=\beta(u)$. Since consecutive points of the chain on a common block $T_l\subseteq K_0$ have blocks $\beta$ joined by $T_l$ in $J_b$, we obtain, walking along the chain and skipping the excursions outside $K_0$, a chain in $J_b$ from $\beta(x_0)=B_1$ ($x_0\in T_1\cap C_0$) to $\beta(y_r)=B_2$ ($y_r\in T_r\cap C_r$). So $B_1,B_2$ lie in one class of $J_b$, as required.

\emph{Conclusion.} By Step~5 the blocks of $\theta_c(s)$ inside $\supp c$ are the sets $W_L$, transversal iff $L$ meets $\supp t$, and its other blocks are the $\N(c)$-blocks; so $\theta_c(s)=\theta_c(t)=w$, which is \eqref{eq:lemD}, and $ata=btb$ by Step~2.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:V}
(1) The hypothesis $bcb=b$ enters the proof of \eqref{eq:lemD} only through the facts (1) and (2) of Step~4. (2) The algebraic part shows more generally: in any projection algebra satisfying \eqref{eq:Q1}, if $bcb=b$, $ata\le b$, $ctc\le a$ and $c(btb)c=ctc$, then $ata=btb$. (3) In every projection algebra, \Vcond\ is equivalent to the three-atom clause $\theta_a(t)\le\theta_d(c),\ \theta_c(t)\le a\Rightarrow\theta_a(t)\le\theta_{\theta_d(c)}(t)$ (Prover9): the half-matching hypothesis $bcb=b$ is absorbed by writing $b=\theta_d(c)$, since $\theta_{\theta_d(c)}(c)=\theta_d(c)$ is an identity of projection algebras (Lemma~\ref{lem:friends}) and every $b$ with $bcb=b$ is of this form. For $d=c$ the clause is a consequence of \eqref{eq:Q1} ($\theta_a(t)$ is then a common lower bound of $a$ and $c$, so $ac=ca$ and $\theta_a(t)=\theta_{ac}(t)=\theta_c(t)$), and the witness $\mathfrak P_8^7$ satisfies this special case while violating \Vcond.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The condition \Pone}\label{subsec:P1}

We write the path of \Pone\ as $a\mt c\mt b\mt d$; the hypotheses are $xa=ax$, $xc=cx$, $\theta_a(x)=\theta_b(x)$ and $\theta_x(c)=\theta_x(d)$, and the conclusion is $a\mt d$. For a path of length three we extend the notation of Section~\ref{subsec:PX}: $D_i$ is the $\T(d)$-block in the class $\Lambda_i$ of $\varepsilon_b\vee\varepsilon_d$ containing $B_i$, so that $\Gamma_i$ contains exactly the $\T$-blocks $A_i,C_i$; $\Delta_i$ exactly $C_i,B_i$; and $\Lambda_i$ exactly $B_i,D_i$.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:P1}
\Pone\ holds in every partition monoid: if $a,b,c,d,x$ are projections of $\P_X$ with $a\mt c$, $c\mt b$, $b\mt d$, $xa=ax$, $xc=cx$, $\theta_a(x)=\theta_b(x)$ and $\theta_x(c)=\theta_x(d)$, then $a\mt d$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Write $e=xa=ax$ and $f=xc=cx$, and $Y=X\setminus\supp x$; these letters, and $U_l,V_k,\tilde N$, are local to this proof.

\smallskip
\emph{Step 1: $x$ commutes with $b$, and $xb=e$.} We have $\theta_b(x)=\theta_a(x)=axa=e$, so $e\le b$; as also $e\le x$, \eqref{eq:Q1} gives $xb=bx$, and then $\theta_b(x)=bxb=xb$, i.e.\ $xb=bx=e$.

\smallskip
\emph{Step 2: the blocks of $a$, $b$, $c$.} Since $x$ commutes with $a$, $b$ and $c$, every block of $a$, $b$ or $c$ lies inside $\supp x$ or is an $\N(x)$-block, and Lemma~\ref{lem:commaux}, applied to the matched pairs $a\mt c$ and $b\mt c$, shows that \emph{$a$, $b$ and $c$ have the same blocks inside $Y$, with the same labels}: for $i\notin I:=\{i:A_i\subseteq\supp x\}$ we have $A_i=B_i=C_i\subseteq Y$, and an $\N(x)$-block is an $\N(a)$-block iff it is an $\N(b)$-block iff it is an $\N(c)$-block. Inside $\supp x$ we compare the elements $xa$ and $xb$, which are equal by Step~1: by Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(b) the upper blocks of $xa$ are the $\N(x)$-blocks, the $\T(e)$-blocks $U_1,\dots,U_t$ (the classes of type (i) of $\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_a$, each a union of $\T(a)$-blocks and of $\T(x)$-blocks, with lower part $U_l'$) and the $\N(a)$-blocks inside $\supp x$ (type (ii)); likewise for $xb$. Hence \emph{the $\N(a)$-blocks inside $\supp x$ are exactly the $\N(b)$-blocks inside $\supp x$, and each $U_l$ is a union of $\T(a)$-blocks and also a union of $\T(b)$-blocks}. Altogether: $a$ and $b$ have the same $\N$-blocks, and $\supp a\cap\supp x=\supp b\cap\supp x=\supp e=U_1\cup\dots\cup U_t$. Finally, for $p\in I$ the block $C_p$ meets $\supp e$ only inside $A_p\cap B_p$: a point of $C_p\cap\supp e$ lies in some $A_q$ and some $B_r$, and $C_p$ meets no $\T(a)$-block other than $A_p$ and no $\T(b)$-block other than $B_p$.

\smallskip
\emph{Step 3: the blocks of $d$ inside $\supp x$.} By Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(a) the blocks of $\theta_x(d)$ inside $\supp x$ are the sets $K\cap\supp x$ for the classes $K$ of $\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_d$ meeting $\supp x$, transversal iff $K$ meets $\supp d$; the blocks of $\theta_x(c)=f$ inside $\supp x$ are the $\T(f)$-blocks $V_1,\dots,V_{t'}$ (unions of $\T(c)$-blocks; transversal) and the $\N(c)$-blocks inside $\supp x$ (non-transversal). Since $\theta_x(d)=\theta_x(c)$: \emph{every block $Z$ of $d$ meets $\supp x$ inside a single $V_k$, or inside a single $\N(c)$-block $\tilde N\subseteq\supp x$, or not at all; and in the second case $Z$ is an $\N(d)$-block}, because the class of $\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_d$ whose trace is $\tilde N$ does not meet $\supp d$. We call $Z$ of \emph{$V$-type}, \emph{$\tilde N$-type} or \emph{outer} accordingly.

Now let $Z$ be a block of $d$ lying in $\Lambda_s$. A point of $Z$ in $\supp b$ lies in some $B_r$, and then $r=s$: \emph{a block of $\Lambda_s$ meets $\supp b$ only inside $B_s$}; in particular, if $Z$ meets a $\T(a)$-block $A_i\subseteq Y$, then $Z$ meets $B_i=A_i$ and $i=s$, and if $Z$ meets $\supp e$ then $s\in I$ and $Z$ meets no $\T(a)$-block inside $Y$. Suppose $Z$ meets $\supp e$ at a point $q$; then $q\in B_s$. If $Z$ is of $V$-type, $q\in V_k\cap\supp e\subseteq\supp c\cap\supp e$ lies in some $C_p$, hence in $A_p\cap B_p$ by Step~2, and $p=s$: \emph{a $V$-type block of $\Lambda_s$ meets $\supp a$ only inside $A_s$}. If $Z$ is of $\tilde N$-type, $q\in\tilde N\cap B_s$, so $\tilde N\subseteq\Delta_s$ (a block of $c$ meeting $B_s$), and $\tilde N$ lies in a class $\Gamma(\tilde N)$ of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_c$ which contains at most one $\T(a)$-block, $A_r$ say; then \emph{$Z$ meets $\supp a$ only inside $\tilde N\cap A_r$}, and $\tilde N\subseteq\Gamma_r\cap\Delta_s$.

\smallskip
\emph{Step 4: the key invariant.} Call two blocks of $d$ \emph{linked} if they are joined by a chain of blocks of $d$ and $\N(a)$-blocks (consecutive blocks meeting); a linked family lies in a single class of $\varepsilon_b\vee\varepsilon_d$, since $\N(a)$-blocks are $\N(b)$-blocks (Step~2). We claim: \emph{a linked family $\mathcal F$ of blocks of $d$ meets at most one $\T(a)$-block; and if $\mathcal F$ contains a $\T(d)$-block $D_p$, it meets $\supp a$ only inside $A_p$.} If no block of $\mathcal F$ meets $\supp b$ there is nothing to prove (a $\T(a)$-block met by a block of $\mathcal F$ is then $A_i=B_i\subseteq Y$, and $\mathcal F\subseteq\Lambda_i$). Otherwise $\mathcal F\subseteq\Lambda_s$ for a unique $s$, and by Step~3 a block of $\mathcal F$ meets $\supp a$ only inside $A_s$ (if it is of $V$-type, or of $\tilde N$-type with $\tilde N\subseteq\Gamma_s$), or inside $A_r$ for an $\N(c)$-block $\tilde N\subseteq\supp x$ with $\tilde N\subseteq\Gamma_r\cap\Delta_s$, $r\ne s$ (if it is of $\tilde N$-type and meets $\supp e$), or inside a $\T(a)$-block $A_i\subseteq Y$, which forces $i=s$. So the claim follows if we show: \emph{if some $Z_0\in\mathcal F$ is of $\tilde N_0$-type with $\tilde N_0\subseteq\Gamma_r\cap\Delta_s$ and $r\ne s$ (or with $\Gamma(\tilde N_0)$ containing no $\T(a)$-block), then every block of $\mathcal F$ meeting $\supp x$ is of $\tilde N$-type for some $\tilde N\subseteq\Gamma_r\cap\Delta_s$ (resp.\ $\tilde N\subseteq\Gamma(\tilde N_0)\cap\Delta_s$).} Indeed, such a family meets $\supp a$ only inside $A_r$ (resp.\ not at all) by Step~3, and it contains no $\T(d)$-block: a $\T(d)$-block is not of $\tilde N$-type, and if it were outer, the chain joining it to $Z_0$ would have a first block meeting $\supp x$, joined to it through $\N(a)$-blocks lying in $Y$, which are $\N(x)$-blocks (the blocks of $d$ in $Y$ need not be), so that this first block would lie in a class of $\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_d$ meeting $\supp d$ and could not be of $\tilde N$-type. We prove the statement by induction along a chain $Z_0,N_1,Z_1,N_2,\dots$ in $\mathcal F$. Let $Z_j$ be of $\tilde N_j$-type with $\tilde N_j\subseteq\Gamma_r\cap\Delta_s$ and let $N_{j+1}$ be an $\N(a)$-block meeting $Z_j$. If $N_{j+1}\subseteq Y$, it is an $\N(x)$-block, so $Z_{j+1}$ lies in the same class of $\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_d$ as $Z_j$, whose trace on $\supp x$ is $\tilde N_j$: $Z_{j+1}$ is of $\tilde N_j$-type or outer, and in the latter case the next $\N(a)$-block meeting it lies in $Y$ as well (an $\N(a)$-block inside $\supp x$ cannot meet an outer block), so the class of $\varepsilon_x\vee\varepsilon_d$ persists until the next block meeting $\supp x$, which is then of $\tilde N_j$-type. If $N_{j+1}\subseteq\supp x$, it meets $Z_j$ inside $\tilde N_j$; being a block of $a$ meeting the block $\tilde N_j$ of $c$, it lies in $\Gamma_r$, and being a block of $b$ (Step~2) meeting $\tilde N_j$, it lies in $\Delta_s$. Let $Z_{j+1}$ meet $N_{j+1}$ at a point $q\in\supp x$, and let $E$ be the block of $c$ containing $q$. Then $E$ meets $N_{j+1}$, so $E\subseteq\Gamma_r\cap\Delta_s$; $E$ is not a $\T(c)$-block, since $C_r\subseteq\Delta_r\ne\Delta_s$, $C_s\subseteq\Gamma_s\ne\Gamma_r$ and $C_p\not\subseteq\Gamma_r$ for $p\ne r$ (in the case of a class without $\T(a)$-block, no $\T(c)$-block lies in it); so $E$ is an $\N(c)$-block, inside $\supp x$ as $q\in\supp x$, and by Step~3 $Z_{j+1}\cap\supp x\subseteq E$: $Z_{j+1}$ is of $E$-type with $E\subseteq\Gamma_r\cap\Delta_s$. This completes the induction and the proof of the claim.

\smallskip
\emph{Step 5: the classes of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_d$.} Let $\rho=\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_d$ and let $D(A_i)$ be the $\rho$-class of $A_i$.

(a) \emph{$D(A_i)$ contains no $\T(a)$-block other than $A_i$.} Let $A_i,A_j$ lie in one class and take a chain in $\rho$ from $A_i$ to $A_j$; between two consecutive $\T(a)$-blocks on it, the blocks are blocks of $d$ and $\N(a)$-blocks, so the blocks of $d$ there are linked, and by Step~4 the two $\T(a)$-blocks coincide. Hence $A_i=A_j$.

(b) \emph{$D_i\subseteq D(A_i)$.} Take a chain from $B_i$ to $D_i$ in $\Lambda_i$; its blocks of $b$ other than $B_i$ are $\N(b)$-blocks, i.e.\ $\N(a)$-blocks. If $i\notin I$ then $B_i=A_i$ and the chain is a chain in $\rho$. If $i\in I$, let $Z$ be the block of $d$ following $B_i$ on the chain; the blocks of $d$ on the chain are linked, and the family contains $D_i$, so by Step~4 $Z$ meets $\supp a$ only inside $A_i$; but $Z$ meets $B_i\subseteq\supp e\subseteq\supp a$, so $Z$ meets $A_i$, and replacing $B_i$ by $A_i$ gives a chain in $\rho$ from $A_i$ to $D_i$.

(c) \emph{$D(A_i)$ contains no $\T(d)$-block other than $D_i$.} Let $D_p\subseteq D(A_i)$ and take a chain in $\rho$ from $D_p$ to $A_i$; let $A_r$ be the first $\T(a)$-block on it. The blocks of $d$ before $A_r$ are linked to $D_p$, so by Step~4 the one meeting $A_r$ meets $\supp a$ only inside $A_p$, whence $r=p$; and by (a) applied along the rest of the chain, $A_i=A_r=A_p$.

\emph{Conclusion.} By (a)--(c) the classes $D(A_1),\dots,D(A_m)$ are distinct and $D(A_i)$ contains exactly the $\T$-blocks $A_i$ and $D_i$; every class of $\rho$ meeting $\supp a\cup\supp d$ contains some $A_i$ or some $D_i$, hence is one of them. By Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(c), $a\mt d$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:P1}
The proof is shorter than that of \Rp\ because the hypotheses make $a$ and $b$ almost equal: they have the same blocks outside $\supp x$ and the same $\N$-blocks, and differ only in how they partition each $\T(e)$-block into $\T$-blocks. Off the ``diagonal'' $A_p\cap B_p$, which is where the $\T(c)$-blocks meet $\supp e$, the overlaps $A_r\cap B_s$ lie in $\N(c)$-blocks, and Step~4 says that the blocks of $d$ reaching such an overlap form a closed family of $\N(d)$-blocks. Unlike \Rp, the conclusion is not accompanied by a separation statement for a coarser join: in $\PA(\P_4)$ the classes of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_d\vee\varepsilon_b$ may contain two $\T(a)$-blocks.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The condition \Qcond\ and its corollaries \Ptwo, \Wire}\label{subsec:Q}

We write the path of \Qcond\ as $a\mt c\mt b\mt d$, with the $\T$-blocks $A_i,C_i,B_i,D_i$ and the classes $\Gamma_i,\Delta_i,\Lambda_i$ as in Section~\ref{subsec:P1}; the hypotheses are $xa=ax$, $\theta_c(x)=\theta_d(x)$, $ya=ay$, $yb=by$, $\theta_y(c)=\theta_y(b)$, and the conclusion is $a\mt d$. The proof occupies the rest of this subsection; the statements of Steps~2--7 were found and checked on all instances of the hypotheses in $\PA(\P_4)$ and on sampled instances in $\PA(\P_5)$ and $\PA(\P_6)$ before being proved. Besides \Qcond\ itself we obtain the two conditions
\begin{align*}
\Ptwo:\quad& a\mt c\mt b\mt d,\ xa=ax,\ xb=bx,\ \theta_x(c)=\theta_x(b),\ \theta_c(x)=\theta_d(x)\ \Longrightarrow\ a\mt d,\\
\Wire:\quad& a\mt c\mt b\mt d,\ cd=dc,\ \theta_a(cd)=\theta_b(cd)\ \Longrightarrow\ a\mt d,
\end{align*}
of which \Ptwo\ is the case $y=x$ of \Qcond\ and \Wire\ is a consequence of \Ptwo\ (Corollary~\ref{cor:QS}).

\smallskip
\emph{Step 1: normalising the auxiliary $x$.} Put $e=xa=ax=\theta_a(x)$ (the letters $e$, $z$, $w$, the groups $G$, the classes $\Gamma_G,\Delta_G,\Lambda_G$ and the blocks $E_G$, $E_W$ are local to this proof). Then $e\le a$ and $\theta_c(e)=\theta_d(e)$. Indeed, for projections $p,q,r$ one has $\theta_{\theta_r(p)}(\theta_p(q))=\theta_r(\theta_p(q))$, since $rpr\,pqp\,rpr=(rprp)q(prpr)=rp\,q\,pr$ by the idempotence of $rp$ and $pr$ (Section~\ref{subsec:rss}); hence $\theta_c(e)=\theta_c(\theta_x(a))=\theta_{\theta_c(x)}(\theta_x(a))=\theta_{\theta_d(x)}(\theta_x(a))=\theta_d(\theta_x(a))=\theta_d(e)$. So we may and do assume $x=e\le a$. Since $z:=\theta_c(e)=\theta_d(e)$ is a common lower bound of $c$ and $d$, \eqref{eq:Q1} gives $cd=dc$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:order}(a) the projection $e\le a$ is determined by a partition $P$ of the index set $\{1,\dots,m\}$ into \emph{groups} $G$ and a label for each group: the blocks of $e$ are the $\N(a)$-blocks and the sets $A_G=\bigcup_{i\in G}A_i$, transversal iff $G$ is labelled $\T$. The labels carry no information (changing the label of a group from $\T$ to $\N$ preserves the hypothesis $\theta_c(e)=\theta_d(e)$, as Step~2 shows), but it is convenient to keep them. We write $C_G=\bigcup_{i\in G}C_i$, $B_G$, $D_G$ similarly.

\smallskip
\emph{Step 2: the structure of $c$ and $d$.} By Lemma~\ref{lem:transport}, $z=\theta_c(e)$ has the blocks $\N(c)\cup\{C_G:G\in P\}$, $C_G$ transversal iff $G$ is. Put $\Pi_1=\varepsilon_e\vee\varepsilon_c$. Its classes are the sets $\Gamma_G=\bigcup_{i\in G}\Gamma_i$ and the \emph{plain} classes of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_c$ (those without $\T$-blocks), and since $z\le c$, $z\le d$ and $z=\theta_d(e)$ we have $\Pi_1=\varepsilon_e\vee\varepsilon_d=\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_z$, so that $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_d\le\Pi_1$. More precisely, since $\varepsilon_a\le\varepsilon_e$, $\Pi_1=\varepsilon_e\vee\varepsilon_d=\rho\vee\varepsilon_e$ with $\rho=\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_d$, and $\rho\vee\varepsilon_e$ is obtained from $\rho$ by identifying the classes that meet the same block $A_G$; so a class of $\rho$ containing no $\T(a)$-block is a class of $\Pi_1$, and hence \emph{every class of $\rho$ contained in some $\Gamma_G$ contains a $\T(a)$-block $A_i$, $i\in G$}, and every class of $\rho$ not contained in any $\Gamma_G$ is a plain class. Comparing the two descriptions of $z$ (blocks $\N(c)\cup\{C_G\}$, and $\N(d)$ together with the traces $L\cap\supp d$ of the classes $L$ of $\Pi_1$) gives, for each group $G$, one of two cases:
\begin{itemize}[nosep]
\item[(nh)] $C_G\subseteq\supp d$ and $C_G=\Gamma_G\cap\supp d$ (a \emph{non-hub} group);
\item[(h)] $C_G$ is an $\N(d)$-block, and $\Gamma_G\cap\supp d$ is empty or a single $\N(c)$-block $E_G$ (a \emph{hub});
\end{itemize}
every other $\N(c)$-block is an $\N(d)$-block and conversely, and a plain class $W$ meets $\supp d$ in an $\N(c)$-block $E_W$ or not at all. The same construction from the side of $b$, with $w=\theta_b(z)\le b$ (which satisfies $\theta_c(w)=\theta_d(w)=z$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:transport} along $b\mt c$ and $b\mt d$) and \emph{the same partition $P$} (because $\theta_c(w)$ has the blocks $\N(c)\cup\{C_{G'}\}$ for the groups $G'$ of $w$), gives $\Delta_G=\Lambda_G$ for every $G$, where $\Delta_G=\bigcup_{i\in G}\Delta_i$ and $\Lambda_G=\bigcup_{i\in G}\Lambda_i$, with $\Lambda_G\cap\supp d=D_G$ (a block of $z=\theta_d(w)$, by transport along $b\mt d$); hence in case (nh) $C_G=D_G$, and in case (h) $D_G$ is a single $\N(c)$-block $E'_G\subseteq\Lambda_G$. Comparing the blocks $\N(c)\cup\{C_G\}$ of $z=\theta_c(e)$ with the blocks $\N(d)\cup\{D_G\}$ of $z=\theta_d(w)$, and using $C_G=D_G$ for non-hubs, we see that the $\N(c)$-blocks which are not $\N(d)$-blocks are exactly the sets $D_{G'}$, $G'$ a hub. Each nonempty trace $E_G$ or $E_W$ is an $\N(c)$-block contained in $\supp d$, hence one of these; conversely each $D_{G'}$, $G'$ a hub, is an $\N(c)$-block contained in $\supp d$, hence contained in the trace of its class of $\Pi_1$, which is a hub class or a plain class (as $D_{G'}\cap\supp c=\emptyset$), and equals that trace. So the nonempty traces $E_G$ of the hubs and $E_W$ of the plain classes are exactly the sets $D_{G'}=E'_{G'}$, $G'$ a hub, each occurring once; write $E_G=D_{\pi(G)}$, $E_W=D_{\pi(W)}$. In particular the $\T(d)$-blocks contained in a non-hub class $\Gamma_G$ are exactly $D_k$, $k\in G$, and those contained in a hub class $\Gamma_G$ form $D_{\pi(G)}$ for a hub $\pi(G)$. Consequently $\rho\le\Pi_1$ has, inside a non-hub class, $|G|$ blocks of each kind, and a proof of $a\mt d$ has to show that these are matched by $\rho$, and that $|\pi(G)|=|G|$ for hubs, again with a matching.

The hub $C_G$ lies in a single class $\Lambda_j$, $j\in G$, and $E_G$ in a single class $\Delta_{k'}$, $k'\in\pi(G)$. Hubs have $|G|\le2$, and a hub $G$ of size $2$ is \emph{cross-wired}: writing $G=\{k,l\}$ with $C_G\subseteq\Lambda_l$, we have $D_G\subseteq\Delta_k$, so that $C_k\subseteq\Delta_k\cap\Lambda_l$, $C_l\subseteq\Delta_l\cap\Lambda_l$, $D_k\subseteq\Delta_k\cap\Lambda_k$, $D_l\subseteq\Delta_k\cap\Lambda_l$, and in particular $C_k$ and $D_l$ do not meet $\supp b$ (the $\T(b)$-block $B_m$ lies in $\Delta_m\cap\Lambda_m$). Indeed, let $C_G\subseteq\Lambda_j$ and $D_G\subseteq\Delta_{j'}$ with $j,j'\in G$, and let $i\in G$. A path in $\Lambda_i$ from $D_i$ to $B_i$ alternates between blocks of $d$ and blocks of $b$; its interior blocks of $d$ are $\N(d)$-blocks, i.e.\ common blocks or hubs $C_{G''}$; every block of $b$, every common block (an $\N(c)$-block) and $D_i\subseteq D_G$ (contained in the $\N(c)$-block $D_G$) lies in a single class of $\varepsilon_c\vee\varepsilon_b$, while a hub $C_{G''}$ meets exactly the classes $\Delta_m$, $m\in G''$. So the class of $\varepsilon_c\vee\varepsilon_b$ changes along the path only at hubs $C_{G''}$, and only between indices in $G''$. The path starts in $\Delta_{j'}$ and ends in $\Delta_i$; if $i\ne j'$, the first change happens at a hub $C_{G''}$ with $j'\in G''$, i.e.\ $G''=G$, and since $C_G\subseteq\Lambda_j$ lies on a path in $\Lambda_i$, we get $i=j$. Hence $G\subseteq\{j,j'\}$, and if $|G|=2$ then $j\ne j'$; put $l=j$, $k=j'$. The map $\pi$ need not be the identity: in $\PA(\P_4)$ take $a=(01|23)$, $c=(0|\N1|2|\N3)$, $b=(03|12)$, $d=(\N0|1|\N2|3)$ (writing only the upper parts, $\N$ marking the $\N$-blocks), $e=a$ with its blocks relabelled $\N$, and $y$ the projection of rank $0$ with the single block $X$; then there are two singleton hubs, exchanged by $\pi$.

\smallskip
\emph{Step 3: the structure imposed by $y$.} Since $y$ commutes with $a$ and $b$, Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(b) describes $\varepsilon_y\vee\varepsilon_a$ and $\varepsilon_y\vee\varepsilon_b$; in particular every $\N(y)$-block is an $\N(a)$-block or a union of $\T(a)$-blocks, and an $\N(b)$-block or a union of $\T(b)$-blocks (four kinds: \emph{$ab$-common} blocks, the $\N(a)$-blocks that are $\N(b)$-blocks; \emph{bridges}, the $\N(b)$-blocks that are unions of $\T(a)$-blocks; \emph{co-bridges}; and unions of $\T(a)$-blocks that are unions of $\T(b)$-blocks), and every block of $a$ or $b$ lies in $\supp y$ or in an $\N(y)$-block. The hypothesis $\theta_y(c)=\theta_y(b)$ says (Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(a),(b)): the classes $K$ of $\varepsilon_y\vee\varepsilon_c$ meeting $\supp y$ have traces $K\cap\supp y$ equal to the classes of $\varepsilon_y\vee\varepsilon_b$ of type (i) (unions $Q$ of $\T(y)$-blocks which are unions of $\T(b)$-blocks; these $K$ meet $\supp c$; we call them classes \emph{of the first kind}) and of type (ii) (single $\N(b)$-blocks $F\subseteq\supp y$; these $K$ do not meet $\supp c$; \emph{of the second kind}). We shall use the following consequence repeatedly.

\textup{($*$)} \emph{Let $K$ be a class of $\varepsilon_y\vee\varepsilon_c$ of the second kind, with trace an $\N(b)$-block $F\subseteq\supp y$, and let $P$ be a path in a class $\Delta_m$ of $\varepsilon_c\vee\varepsilon_b$ (blocks of $c$ and of $b$ alternately, consecutive blocks meeting, all distinct) or in a class $\Lambda_k$ of $\varepsilon_b\vee\varepsilon_d$ (blocks of $d$ and of $b$), whose first block is $F$ or a block of $c$ lying in $K$. Then every block of $c$ on $P$ or meeting a block of $b$ of $P$ lies in $K$; in particular $P$ contains no $\T(c)$-block; every block of $d$ on $P$ is a common block (an $\N(c)$-block which is an $\N(d)$-block; it lies in $K$), except possibly the $\T(d)$-block $D_k$, which is then not contained in $\supp c$, so that the $\N(c)$-block containing it lies in $K$.}

Call a set \emph{covered} if each of its points lies in a block of $y$ or of $c$ belonging to $K$; $F$ is covered (its points lie in $\T(y)$-blocks, which are in $K$ as $F$ is the trace), and so is a block of $c$ in $K$. We show inductively that every block of $P$ is covered, which gives all assertions: let $W$ be covered and let $W'$ be the next block of $P$, meeting $W$ at $q$, so that $q$ lies in a block $Y$ of $y$ or of $c$ with $Y\subseteq K$. If $W'$ is a block of $c$, then $W'=Y$ or $W'$ meets the $y$-block $Y$, so $W'\subseteq K$; the same applies to any block of $c$ meeting $W$ if $W$ is a block of $b$, and shows that a block of $c$ meeting a covered set lies in $K$. If $W'$ is an $\N(b)$-block, it is an $\N(y)$-block, and then $W'\subseteq K$ ($W'=Y$, or $W'$ meets the block $Y$ of $c$), or a union of $\T(y)$-blocks, and then the $\T(y)$-block through $q$ lies in $K$, hence in $F$, so $W'=F$; in both cases $W'$ is covered. If $W'$ is the $\T(b)$-block $B_m$ (resp.\ $B_k$), it is not contained in $\supp y$ (the $\T(y)$-block through $q$ would lie in $K$, hence in $F$, but $F\cap W'=\emptyset$), so it lies in an $\N(y)$-block $Q'$, which meets $Y$ and lies in $K$, and $W'$ is covered. If $W'$ is a block of $d$, it is a common block (a block of $c$, covered by the first case), or a hub, or $D_k$; a hub is a union of $\T(c)$-blocks, and the $\T(c)$-block through $q$ would lie in $K$, which does not meet $\supp c$, impossible; and for $D_k$ the same argument shows $D_k\not\subseteq\supp c$, so $D_k$ lies in an $\N(c)$-block (Step~2), which lies in $K$.

From this: (a) an $\N(b)$-block $F$ lying in the class $\Delta_m$ of a $\T(c)$-block is an $\N(y)$-block: otherwise it is a union of $\T(y)$-blocks, the class $K$ with trace $F$ is of the second kind, and a path in $\Delta_m$ from $F$ to $C_m$ contradicts ($*$); (a$'$) an $\N(b)$-block $F$ lying in the class $\Lambda_k$ of a $\T(d)$-block is an $\N(y)$-block: otherwise, with $K$ as before, a path in $\Lambda_k$ from $F$ to $D_k$ shows by ($*$) that $D_k$ lies in an $\N(c)$-block $E\subseteq K$, which is $E_{G'}$ or $E_W$ for a hub $G'$ or a plain class $W$, hence equals $D_{G''}$ for a hub $G''$ (Step~2) and lies in a class $\Delta_{k''}$, $k''\in G''$ (as $D_{G''}\subseteq\Lambda_{G''}=\Delta_{G''}$), and a path in $\Delta_{k''}$ from $E$ to $C_{k''}$ contradicts ($*$); (b) a $\T(y)$-block meeting $\supp c$ lies in a class $K$ of the first kind, hence in a set $Q$ as above, hence in $\supp b$; so a $\T(y)$-block meets $C_i$ only inside $B_i\cap C_i$ (the only $\T(b)$-block meeting $C_i\subseteq\Delta_i$ is $B_i$); (b$'$) a $\T(y)$-block $Y$ meeting $D_k$ lies in $\supp b$, and meets $D_k$ only inside $B_k\cap D_k$: if $D_k\subseteq\supp c$ this is (b), and otherwise $D_k$ lies in an $\N(c)$-block $E\subseteq\Delta_{k''}$ as in (a$'$), and if the class $K$ of $Y$ were of the second kind, $E\subseteq K$ and a path in $\Delta_{k''}$ from $E$ to $C_{k''}$ would contradict ($*$); (c) the $\T(c)$-blocks in the class with trace $Q$ are exactly the $C_i$ with $B_i\subseteq Q$; (d) every $\N(b)$-block meeting $\supp c\cup\supp d$ lies in some $\Delta_m$ or $\Lambda_k$, hence is an $\N(y)$-block by (a), (a$'$), hence an $\N(a)$-block or a bridge; (e) every block $Z$ of $a$ satisfies $Z\cap(\supp c\cup\supp d)\subseteq\supp b$, or $Z$ is contained in an $\N(b)$-block: if $Z\subseteq\supp y$ this is (b), (b$'$), and otherwise $Z$ lies in an $\N(y)$-block, which is an $\N(b)$-block or a union of $\T(b)$-blocks.

\smallskip
\emph{Step 4: plain classes contain no $\T(d)$-block.} Let $W$ be a plain class; then $W\cap\supp a=W\cap\supp c=\emptyset$, every block of $a$ or of $c$ meeting $W$ lies in $W$, and so does every block of $y$ meeting $W$: an $\N(y)$-block meeting $W$ is an $\N(a)$-block (it cannot be a union of $\T(a)$-blocks), and a $\T(y)$-block lies in a union of $\T(a)$-blocks or in an $\N(a)$-block (Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(b), as $ya=ay$), the latter alternative being forced if it meets $W$. Hence \emph{$W$ is a union of classes of $\varepsilon_y\vee\varepsilon_c$}. Suppose now $D_j\subseteq E_W\subseteq W$. Take a path in $\Lambda_j$ from $D_j$ to $B_j$. Its $\N(b)$-blocks lie in $\Lambda_j$, so they are $\N(y)$-blocks (Step~3(a$'$)), hence $\N(a)$-blocks or bridges; a bridge is a union of $\T(a)$-blocks and cannot meet $W$, and an $\N(a)$-block meeting $W$ lies in $W$; the interior $\N(d)$-blocks of the path are common blocks (blocks of $c$, which lie in $W$ if they meet it) or hubs (unions of $\T(c)$-blocks, which cannot meet $W$). So inductively every block of the path except possibly $B_j$ lies in $W$, and $B_j$ meets $W$. If $B_j$ lies in an $\N(y)$-block $Q$, then $Q$ meets $W$, so $Q$ is an $\N(a)$-block contained in $W$ and $B_j\subseteq W$; a path in $\Delta_j$ from $B_j$ to $C_j$ then lies in $W$ by the same argument (its $\N(b)$-blocks are $\N(y)$-blocks by Step~3(a), and its blocks of $c$ lie in $W$ as soon as they meet it), so $C_j\subseteq W$, contradicting $W\cap\supp c=\emptyset$. If $B_j\subseteq\supp y$, take $q\in B_j\cap W$ and let $Y$ be the $\T(y)$-block of $q$ and $K$ its class in $\varepsilon_y\vee\varepsilon_c$; then $K\subseteq W$. The trace $K\cap\supp y$ is a class of $\varepsilon_y\vee\varepsilon_b$ containing $Y$, which meets $B_j$, so it is not a single $\N(b)$-block and contains $B_j$: $K$ is of the first kind, with trace $Q\supseteq B_j$, and $C_j\subseteq K\subseteq W$ by Step~3(c), again contradicting $W\cap\supp c=\emptyset$. Consequently every hub $G$ has $E_G\ne\emptyset$, and $\pi$ is a permutation of the hubs.

\smallskip
\emph{Step 5: types, index pairs and contacts.} By Step~2, $\rho\le\Pi_1$, every class of $\rho$ contained in a class $\Gamma_G$ contains some $A_i$, $i\in G$, and a class of $\rho$ not contained in any $\Gamma_G$ is a plain class, which contains no $\T$-block of $a$ (Step~2) or of $d$ (Step~4). By Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(c), $a\mt d$ is therefore equivalent to: \emph{for every group $G$, every class of $\rho$ contained in $\Gamma_G$ contains exactly one $\T(a)$-block and exactly one $\T(d)$-block}. This is proved for non-hub groups in Step~6 and for hubs in Step~7. Inside $\Gamma_G$ the blocks of $a$ are $A_i$ ($i\in G$) and $\N(a)$-blocks, and the blocks of $d$ are the \emph{common} blocks (the $\N(c)$-blocks which are $\N(d)$-blocks; in a non-hub class these are all the $\N$-blocks of $c$ and of $d$ in $\Gamma_G$, and $C_G=D_G$ is the union of the $\T$-blocks of both), and, in a hub class, the hub $C_G$ and the $\T(d)$-blocks $D_m$, $m\in\pi(G)$, whose union is the $\N(c)$-block $E_G$.

\emph{Types of blocks.} By Step~3 every block $Z$ of $a$ either lies in $\supp y$, or lies in an $\N(y)$-block $Q$, which is then an $\N(b)$-block or a union of $\T(b)$-blocks. Call $Z$ of \emph{$\N$-type} if it lies in an $\N(y)$-block which is an $\N(b)$-block (if $Z$ is an $\N(a)$-block this means $Z$ itself is an $\N(b)$- and $\N(y)$-block; if $Z=A_i$ it means that $A_i$ lies in a bridge $Q$), and of \emph{$b$-type} otherwise; a $b$-type block lies in $\supp y$ or in an $\N(y)$-block contained in $\supp b$, so by Step~3(b), (b$'$) it meets each $C_m$ only inside $B_m\cap C_m$ and each $D_m$ only inside $B_m\cap D_m$. For an $\N(b)$-block $Q$ write $\delta(Q)=m$ if $Q\subseteq\Delta_m$ and $\lambda(Q)=k$ if $Q\subseteq\Lambda_k$ (undefined if the class of $Q$ in $\varepsilon_c\vee\varepsilon_b$, resp.\ $\varepsilon_b\vee\varepsilon_d$, contains no $\T$-block); an $\N$-type block $Z\subseteq Q$ meets $C_m$ only if $\delta(Q)=m$ and $D_k$ only if $\lambda(Q)=k$. A block of $a$ meeting $\supp c\cup\supp d$ at a point outside $\supp b$ is of $\N$-type (Step~3(e), (d)).

In a non-hub class every point of $C_G=D_G$ lies in a unique $\T(c)$-block $C_m$ and a unique $\T(d)$-block $D_k$ (its \emph{cell} $(m,k)$), and a point of $C_G$ lying in $\supp b$ lies in $B_m\cap C_m\cap D_m$ for some $m$: its cell is \emph{diagonal}. Hence a $b$-type block of $a$ meets $C_G$ only in diagonal cells, and since it meets at most one $\T(c)$-block (it lies in one class $\Gamma_m$), only in the cell $(m,m)$; while an $\N$-type block $Z\subseteq Q$ meets $C_G$ only in points outside $\supp b$, in the cell $(\delta(Q),\lambda(Q))$.

In a hub class, with $C_G\subseteq\Lambda_j$ ($j\in G$) and $E_G\subseteq\Delta_{k'}$ ($k'\in\pi(G)$) as in Step~2: a $b$-type block meets $C_G$ only in the sets $B_i\cap C_i$, $i\in G$, and $E_G$ only in the sets $B_m\cap D_m$, $m\in\pi(G)$; an $\N$-type block $Z\subseteq Q$ meets $C_G$ only if $\lambda(Q)=j$, and then inside $C_{\delta(Q)}$, and meets $E_G$ only if $\delta(Q)=k'$, and then inside $D_{\lambda(Q)}$.

\emph{Index pairs.} For a common block $E$ let $\lambda(E)$ be the index $k$ with $E\subseteq\Lambda_k$ (undefined if the class of $E$ in $\varepsilon_b\vee\varepsilon_d$ contains no $\T(d)$-block) and $\delta(E)$ the index $m$ with $E\subseteq\Delta_m$ (undefined likewise). (i) \emph{If $E$ meets a $b$-type block of $a$ at a point $q$, then either $q\in B_m$ for some $m$ and $(\lambda(E),\delta(E))=(m,m)$, or $q\notin\supp b$ and both indices are undefined.} Indeed, if $q\in B_m$ then $E$ meets $B_m$. Otherwise $q$ lies in $\supp y$ (a $b$-type block not contained in $\supp y$ lies in $\supp b$), in a $\T(y)$-block $Y$, which lies in an $\N(b)$-block $F\subseteq\supp y$ (Step~3), and the class $K$ of $\varepsilon_y\vee\varepsilon_c$ with trace $F$ is of the second kind and contains $E$ (a block of $c$ meeting $Y$). If $\delta(E)=m$ were defined, a path in $\Delta_m$ from $E$ to $C_m$ would contradict ($*$). If $\lambda(E)=k$ were defined, a path in $\Lambda_k$ from $E$ to $D_k$ shows by ($*$) that $D_k$ lies in an $\N(c)$-block $E'\subseteq K$, which lies in a class $\Delta_{k''}$ (Step~2, as in Step~3(a$'$)), and a path in $\Delta_{k''}$ from $E'$ to $C_{k''}$ contradicts ($*$). (ii) If $E$ meets an $\N$-type block $Z\subseteq Q$ then $(\lambda(E),\delta(E))=(\lambda(Q),\delta(Q))$ (as indices or as ``undefined''), as $E$ meets the $b$-block $Q$. (iii) Two common blocks meeting the same $\N$-type $\N(a)$-block have the same pair. Consequently, along a path $E_0,F_1,E_1,\dots,F_r,E_r$ alternating between common blocks and $\N(a)$-blocks (consecutive blocks meeting; no hub involved), the pair is preserved across $\N$-type $\N(a)$-blocks and, at a $b$-type $\N(a)$-block, the pairs of the two adjacent common blocks are each either diagonal ($(m,m)$) or undefined in both coordinates. In particular (\emph{rigidity}): \emph{if some common block of such a path, or some $\N$-type $\N(a)$-block of it, has a defined non-diagonal pair $(k,m)$, $k\ne m$, then every $\N(a)$-block of the path is of $\N$-type and every block of the path has the pair $(k,m)$; and a $\T(a)$-block met by a common block of the path is then of $\N$-type with the pair $(k,m)$ as well} (by (i) and (ii), which apply to $\T(a)$-blocks too).

\smallskip
\emph{Step 6: non-hub classes.}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:Qnh}
In the situation of Steps~\textup{1--5}, let $G$ be a non-hub group. Then every class of $\rho$ contained in $\Gamma_G$ contains exactly one $\T(a)$-block and exactly one $\T(d)$-block.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let $\mathcal K$ be the set of components of the graph on the $\N(a)$-blocks and the common blocks contained in $\Gamma_G$ (blocks joined when they meet); each $\kappa\in\mathcal K$ is contained in a single class $\Gamma_m$, so it touches at most one $\T(a)$-block, $A_m$, and its $\N(a)$-blocks meet $C_G$ only in $C_m$. Say that $D_k$ is \emph{attached} to $A_i$ if $A_i$ meets $D_k$, or if some $\kappa$ touching $A_i$ (a common block of $\kappa$ meets $A_i$) contains an $\N(a)$-block meeting $D_k$. The classes of $\rho$ inside $\Gamma_G$ are generated by these adjacencies, so it suffices to prove:
\begin{enumerate}[nosep,label=(\alph*)]
\item every $\kappa$ contains $\N(a)$-blocks meeting at most one $\T(d)$-block;
\item at most one $\T(d)$-block is attached to each $A_i$;
\item at least one $\T(d)$-block is attached to each $A_i$.
\end{enumerate}
Given (a)--(c): by (b), (c) the attachment defines a map $\tau\colon G\to G$; every $D_k$ lies in a class of $\rho$ containing some $A_i$ (Step~5), and a $\rho$-path from $D_k$ to $A_i$ starts with $D_k$, a block of $a$ meeting it, and, if that block is an $\N(a)$-block, continues inside its $\kappa$ until the first $\T$-block met, which by (a) is a $\T(a)$-block; so every $D_k$ is attached to some $A_i$, $\tau$ is surjective, hence bijective; and then the $\rho$-class of $A_i$ contains the $\T$-blocks $A_i$ and $D_{\tau(i)}$ only (a further $\T(a)$-block $A_j$ in it would be reached through some $D_k$, which would be attached to both, and a further $\T(d)$-block through some $\kappa$ or $A$, contradicting (a) or (b)), and every class of $\rho$ in $\Gamma_G$ is of this form.

(c): Take a path in $\Gamma_i$ from $A_i$ to $C_i$; its blocks of $c$ are common blocks and its blocks of $a$ are $\N(a)$-blocks, so they lie in one $\kappa$ touching $A_i$, and the last block of $a$ on it (or $A_i$ itself) meets $C_i\subseteq C_G=D_G$, hence some $D_k$.

(b): Let $D_k$ be attached to $A_i$. \emph{Case $A_i$ of $b$-type.} A direct contact $A_i\cap D_k\ne\emptyset$ is in the cell $(i,i)$, so $k=i$. Otherwise $\kappa$ touches $A_i$ at a common block $E$ whose pair is diagonal or undefined (i), and contains an $\N(a)$-block $F$ meeting $D_k$ at a point of $C_i$. If $F$ is of $b$-type, the contact is in the cell $(i,i)$ and $k=i$. If $F$ is of $\N$-type, $F$ is an $\N(b)$-block in $\Lambda_k\cap\Delta_i$, so the common block of $\kappa$ adjacent to $F$ on a path from $E$ has the pair $(k,i)$; but along the path from $E$ all pairs are diagonal or undefined, so $k=i$. \emph{Case $A_i\subseteq Q$ of $\N$-type.} A direct contact lies in the cell $(i,\lambda(Q))$, so $k=\lambda(Q)$ and $\delta(Q)=i$. Otherwise the common block $E$ of $\kappa$ meeting $A_i$ has the pair $(\lambda(Q),\delta(Q))$, and along the path from $E$ to the contact block $F$ the pair is preserved up to the first $b$-type $\N(a)$-block, if any. If there is none: $F$ is of $\N$-type with $(\lambda(F),\delta(F))=(k,i)$ equal to $(\lambda(Q),\delta(Q))$, or of $b$-type with $k=i$ and the pair $(\lambda(Q),\delta(Q))$ diagonal or undefined; if there is one, then $(\lambda(Q),\delta(Q))$ is diagonal or undefined and the contact gives $k=i$ (for an $\N$-type $F$ the adjacent pair $(k,i)$ is diagonal). So every attached $D_k$ is $D_{\lambda(Q)}$, and then $\delta(Q)=i$, or $D_i$, and then $\lambda(Q)=\delta(Q)$ or both are undefined; if both kinds occur then $\lambda(Q)=\delta(Q)=i$. Hence at most one $\T(d)$-block is attached.

(a): Let $\kappa$ contain $F$ meeting $D_k$ and $F'$ meeting $D_l$, in cells $(\gamma,k)$ and $(\gamma,l)$ where $\kappa\subseteq\Gamma_\gamma$. If $F$ is of $b$-type then $k=\gamma$, and likewise for $F'$. If $F'$ is of $\N$-type, it is an $\N(b)$-block in $\Lambda_l\cap\Delta_\gamma$, and the common block next to $F'$ on a path from $F'$ to $F$ has the pair $(l,\gamma)$; following the path, if it contains no $b$-type block before $F$ then either $F$ is of $\N$-type and $(k,\gamma)=(l,\gamma)$, or $F$ is of $b$-type and the pair $(l,\gamma)$ is diagonal, $l=\gamma=k$; if it contains a $b$-type block, the pair $(l,\gamma)$ is diagonal, $l=\gamma$, and by symmetry (the case $F$ of $\N$-type gives $k=\gamma$ in the same way) $k=\gamma$. In all cases $k=l$.
\end{proof}

\smallskip
\emph{Step 7: hub classes.}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:Qh}
In the situation of Steps~\textup{1--5}, let $G$ be a hub. Then $|\pi(G)|=|G|$, and every class of $\rho$ contained in $\Gamma_G$ contains exactly one $\T(a)$-block and exactly one $\T(d)$-block.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write $G'=\pi(G)$; by Steps~2 and~4, $E_G=D_{G'}$ is a nonempty $\N(c)$-block contained in $\Gamma_G$, hence in a single class $\Gamma_e$, $e\in G$, and in a single class $\Delta_{k'}$, $k'\in G'$; and $C_G\subseteq\Lambda_j$, $j\in G$. (In this proof $e$ denotes an index, not the projection of Step~1, which is no longer needed.) If $|G|=2$ we write $G=\{k,l\}$ with $l=j$, so that (cross wiring, Step~2) $C_k\subseteq\Delta_k\cap\Lambda_l$, $C_l\subseteq\Delta_l\cap\Lambda_l$ and $C_k\cap\supp b=\emptyset$. If $|G'|=2$ we write $G'=\{k',l'\}$, so that (cross wiring for $G'$) $D_{k'}\subseteq\Delta_{k'}\cap\Lambda_{k'}$, $D_{l'}\subseteq\Delta_{k'}\cap\Lambda_{l'}$ and $D_{l'}\cap\supp b=\emptyset$. Let $\mathcal N$ be the set of components $\nu$ of the graph on the $\N$-blocks of $a$ and $d$ inside $\Gamma_G$ (the $\N(a)$-blocks, the common blocks and $C_G$; joined when they meet), and say that $D_m$ ($m\in G'$) is \emph{attached} to $A_i$ ($i\in G$) if there is a path in the graph of blocks of $a$ and $d$ from $A_i$ to $D_m$ all of whose interior blocks are $\N$-blocks, i.e.\ $A_i$ meets $D_m$, or some $\nu$ touching $A_i$ contains an $\N(a)$-block meeting $D_m$. All paths considered below are shortest paths between their endpoints in the graph in question, hence simple, and an attachment path from $A_i$ to $D_m$ is always taken shortest, so that its interior is a shortest path in a component $\nu$. A component $\nu$ not containing $C_G$ lies in a single class $\Gamma_i$; a shortest path in a component $\nu$ between two blocks lying in the same class $\Gamma_i$ (or a shortest attachment path with both ends in $\Gamma_i$) passes through $C_G$ at most once, and if it does, both neighbours of $C_G$ on it are $\N(a)$-blocks in $\Gamma_i$ meeting $C_G$ inside $C_i$, and the two parts of the path avoid $C_G$ (each part lies in a single class of $\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_c$, being a chain of blocks of $a$ and of $c$); a path in $\nu$ between blocks in different classes $\Gamma_k$, $\Gamma_l$ passes through $C_G$. We write $G=\{k,l\}$, $G'=\{k',l'\}$ as above; when $G'=G$ we have $k'=k$ and $l'=l$, because $E_G=D_G$ lies in $\Delta_{k'}$ and in $\Delta_k$. By Step~5, inside $\Gamma_G$:
\begin{enumerate}[nosep,label=(\roman*)]
\item a block of $a$ meeting $C_G$ in $C_i$ is of $\N$-type, contained in an $\N(b)$-block $Q$ with $(\lambda(Q),\delta(Q))=(j,i)$, or it is of $b$-type, $i=j$ and the contact lies in $B_j\cap C_j$;
\item a block of $a$ meeting $D_{k'}$ is of $\N$-type with pair $(k',k')$, or of $b$-type with contact in $B_{k'}\cap D_{k'}$;
\item if $|G'|=2$, a block of $a$ meeting $D_{l'}$ is of $\N$-type with pair $(l',k')$.
\end{enumerate}

\emph{Claim 1: the $\N(a)$-blocks of a single component $\nu$ meet at most one $\T(d)$-block.} Suppose $Z,Z'\in\nu$ meet $D_m$, $D_{m'}$ with $m\ne m'$, so $|G'|=2$, say $m'=l'$, $m=k'$. Both lie in $\Gamma_e$; take a shortest path in $\nu$ from $Z'$ to $Z$. If it avoids $C_G$, then by (iii) and rigidity all its blocks have the pair $(l',k')$, contradicting (ii) for $Z$. If it passes through $C_G$, the part from $Z'$ to the neighbour $Z_1$ of $C_G$ avoids $C_G$, so $Z_1$ has the pair $(l',k')$ by rigidity, and $Z_1$ meets $C_G$ inside $C_e$, so $(\lambda,\delta)=(j,e)$ by (i); thus $k'=e\in G\cap G'$, so $G'=G$, $|G|=2$, $k'=k$, $e=k$, $l'=l=j$. Then the other neighbour $Z_2$ of $C_G$ meets $C_G$ inside $C_k$, so it is of $\N$-type with pair $(l,k)$ by (i) (as $C_k\cap\supp b=\emptyset$), and rigidity along the part from $Z_2$ to $Z$ gives $Z$ the pair $(l,k)$, contradicting (ii).

\emph{Claim 2: at most one $\T(d)$-block is attached to each $A_i$.} Suppose $D_{k'}$ and $D_{l'}$ are attached to $A_i$, so $|G'|=2$. By (iii) the contact block of $D_{l'}$ (which is $A_i$ itself or an $\N(a)$-block $Z'$) has the pair $(l',k')$. \emph{Case $G'\ne G$.} An attachment path from $A_i$ to $D_{l'}$ cannot pass through $C_G$: the neighbour of $C_G$ on the side of $D_{l'}$ would have the pair $(l',k')$ by rigidity and $\delta=i''\in G$ by (i), but $k'\notin G$. So by rigidity $A_i$ is of $\N$-type, $A_i\subseteq Q$ with $(\lambda(Q),\delta(Q))=(l',k')$. Now consider an attachment path from $A_i$ to $D_{k'}$. It does not start with a direct contact ($\lambda(Q)=l'\ne k'$), nor with $C_G$ ($\lambda(Q)=l'\ne j$, as $l'\notin G$), so it starts with a common block meeting $A_i$, of pair $(l',k')$, and by rigidity all its blocks have this pair until it reaches $C_G$ or the contact block of $D_{k'}$; the former is excluded as before ($k'\notin G$), the latter by (ii). \emph{Case $G'=G$}, so $|G|=2$, $k'=k$, $l'=l=j$. Suppose first $i=k$. If $A_k$ meets $D_l$, then $A_k$ is of $\N$-type with pair $(l,k)$ by (iii) and $e=k$. Otherwise take a shortest attachment path from $A_k$ to $D_l$; its contact block has the pair $(l,k)$. If the path passes through $C_G$, the neighbour of $C_G$ on the side of the contact block has the pair $(l,k)$ by rigidity, so by (i) it meets $C_G$ inside $C_k$ and lies in $\Gamma_k$, and so does the contact block, i.e.\ $e=k$; and the neighbour on the side of $A_k$ then also lies in $\Gamma_k$, meets $C_k$ and has the pair $(l,k)$ by (i) ($C_k\cap\supp b=\emptyset$). If the path avoids $C_G$, all its blocks lie in $\Gamma_k$, so again $e=k$. In both cases rigidity along the part of the path next to $A_k$ (or (i), if that part is empty and $A_k$ meets $C_G$) shows that $A_k$ is of $\N$-type with pair $(l,k)$. The attachment path from $A_k$ to $D_k$ then starts with the pair $(l,k)$ (a direct contact is excluded by $\lambda=l\ne k$; a common block meeting $A_k$ has the pair $(l,k)$; and $C_G$, if it comes first or later, has both neighbours in $\Gamma_k$, of pair $(l,k)$, as $e=k$), so by rigidity it never reaches a block of pair $(k,k)$ or of $b$-type, contradicting (ii). Suppose now $i=l$. If the attachment path from $A_l$ to $D_l$ avoids $C_G$, then $e=l$, $A_l$ is of $\N$-type with pair $(l,k)$ and, as the neighbours of $C_G$ in $\Gamma_l$ have $\delta=l$ by (i), no path from $A_l$ with pair $(l,k)$ reaches $C_G$; so the attachment path from $A_l$ to $D_k$ keeps the pair $(l,k)$ by rigidity and cannot reach a contact block as in (ii); a direct contact is excluded by $\lambda(Q)=l\ne k$. If it passes through $C_G$, then $e=k$ (a shortest path between blocks of $\Gamma_l$ through $C_G$ would have both neighbours of $C_G$ in $\Gamma_l$, of $b$-type or with $\delta=l$ by (i), whereas rigidity from the contact block gives the neighbour on its side the pair $(l,k)$); a direct contact $A_l\cap D_k$ is then impossible ($A_l\subseteq\Gamma_l$, $D_k\subseteq\Gamma_k$), and an attachment path from $A_l\subseteq\Gamma_l$ to $D_k\subseteq\Gamma_k$ passes through $C_G$ with its neighbour on the side of $D_k$ in $\Gamma_k$, of pair $(l,k)$ by (i), which by rigidity cannot reach a contact block as in (ii).

\emph{Consequences.} Every $D_m$, $m\in G'$, is attached to some $A_i$: its class of $\rho$ contains some $A_i$ (Step~5), and on a shortest $\rho$-path from $D_m$ to $A_i$ the first $\T$-block after $D_m$ is reached through $\N$-blocks of one component $\nu$, which contains an $\N(a)$-block meeting $D_m$ (unless the block following $D_m$ is already a $\T(a)$-block), so by Claim~1 it is a $\T(a)$-block. Hence, by Claim~2, attachment is a partial map $\tau\colon G\rightharpoonup G'$ which is surjective, and $|\pi(G)|\le|G|$ for every hub $G$. Since $\pi$ is a permutation of the hubs (Step~4), $\sum_G|\pi(G)|=\sum_G|G|$, so $|\pi(G)|=|G|$ for every hub, and $\tau$ is a bijection $G\to G'$. In particular $|G'|=2$ when $|G|=2$.

\emph{Claim 3: every component $\nu$ touches at most one $\T(a)$-block.} Suppose $\nu$ touches $A_k$ and $A_l$, so $|G|=2=|G'|$. Then $\nu$ contains $C_G$, and a shortest path in $\nu$ from a block meeting $A_k$ to a block meeting $A_l$ passes through $C_G$; its neighbour of $C_G$ on the side of $A_k$ lies in $\Gamma_k$ and meets $C_k$, so it is of $\N$-type with pair $(l,k)$ by (i), and by rigidity $A_k$ is of $\N$-type with pair $(l,k)$ (this includes the case that $A_k$ meets $C_G$ directly, by (i)). \emph{Case $e=k$.} The block $D_{k'}$ is attached to some $A_i$, and every attachment path to $D_{k'}$ ends at a contact block as in (ii), of pair $(k',k')$ or of $b$-type, lying in $\Gamma_k$. An attachment path from $A_k$: a direct contact would need $\lambda=l=k'$, impossible ($k'=k$ if $G'=G$, $k'\notin G$ otherwise); otherwise it starts with the pair $(l,k)$, and if it passes through $C_G$ both neighbours lie in $\Gamma_k$ and have the pair $(l,k)$; so by rigidity it cannot reach the contact block. A direct contact $A_l\cap D_{k'}$ is impossible ($A_l\subseteq\Gamma_l$), and an attachment path from $A_l\subseteq\Gamma_l$ to $D_{k'}\subseteq\Gamma_k$ passes through $C_G$ with its neighbour on the side of $D_{k'}$ in $\Gamma_k$, of pair $(l,k)$, and rigidity gives the same contradiction. So $D_{k'}$ is attached to no $A_i$, contradicting the consequences of Claims~1 and~2. \emph{Case $e=l$.} The block $D_{l'}$ is attached to some $A_i$, through a contact block of pair $(l',k')$ in $\Gamma_l$ (iii). A direct contact $A_k\cap D_{l'}$ is impossible ($A_k\subseteq\Gamma_k$), and an attachment path from $A_k\subseteq\Gamma_k$ passes through $C_G$, whose neighbour on the side of $D_{l'}$ lies in $\Gamma_l$ and meets $C_l$, so by (i) it is of $b$-type or has the pair $(l,l)$, whereas rigidity from the contact block gives it the pair $(l',k')$, $l'\ne k'$: impossible. So $D_{l'}$ is attached to $A_l$, and by rigidity (the neighbours of $C_G$ in $\Gamma_l$ have $\delta=l$ by (i), and $k'=l$ is impossible: it would give $l\in G'$, $G'=G$, $k'=k$) the attachment path avoids $C_G$ and $A_l$ is of $\N$-type with pair $(l',k')$; a direct contact $A_l\cap D_{l'}\ne\emptyset$ gives the same. But then $A_l$ is not touched by $\nu$: a block of $\nu$ meeting $A_l$ is $C_G$ (excluded, as $\lambda=l'$ and $\lambda=j=l$ would give $l\in G'$, $G'=G$, and then $\delta=k'=k$, so the contact would lie in $C_k\subseteq\Gamma_k$) or a common block of pair $(l',k')$, from which, by rigidity, no path in $\nu$ reaches a neighbour of $C_G$.

\emph{Conclusion.} Let $R$ be a class of $\rho$ contained in $\Gamma_G$; it contains some $A_i$ (Step~5). We claim that its $\T$-blocks are exactly $A_i$ and $D_{\tau(i)}$. Otherwise take a shortest path in the graph of blocks of $a$ and $d$ from $\{A_i,D_{\tau(i)}\}$ to another $\T$-block; its interior consists of $\N$-blocks. It cannot lead from $A_i$ to a $\T(d)$-block (this would be attached to $A_i$, hence equal to $D_{\tau(i)}$), nor from $A_i$ to a $\T(a)$-block $A_{i'}$ (Claim~3), nor from $D_{\tau(i)}$ to a $\T(a)$-block $A_{i'}$ ($D_{\tau(i)}$ would be attached to $A_{i'}$, so $i'=i$ as $\tau$ is injective), nor from $D_{\tau(i)}$ to a $\T(d)$-block (Claim~1). So every class of $\rho$ in $\Gamma_G$ contains exactly one $\T(a)$-block and one $\T(d)$-block.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:Q}
\Qcond\ holds in every partition monoid: if $a\mt c\mt b\mt d$, $xa=ax$, $\theta_c(x)=\theta_d(x)$, $ya=ay$, $yb=by$ and $\theta_y(c)=\theta_y(b)$ in $\P_X$, then $a\mt d$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By Step~1 we may assume $x=e\le a$. By Step~5 it suffices that every class of $\rho=\varepsilon_a\vee\varepsilon_d$ contained in a class $\Gamma_G$ contains exactly one $\T(a)$-block and one $\T(d)$-block, which is Proposition~\ref{prop:Qnh} for the non-hub groups and Proposition~\ref{prop:Qh} for the hubs.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{cor:QS}
In every projection algebra, \Qcond\ implies \Ptwo\ and \Ptwo\ implies \Wire. In particular \Ptwo\ and \Wire\ hold in every $\P_X$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Putting $y=x$ in \Qcond\ gives exactly \Ptwo. For \Ptwo$\Rightarrow$\Wire, let $a\mt c\mt b\mt d$, $cd=dc=:g$ and $\theta_a(g)=\theta_b(g)$ be the hypotheses of \Wire, and apply \Ptwo\ to the reversed path $d\mt b\mt c\mt a$ with the auxiliary $g$: $g\le d$ and $g\le c$, so $g$ commutes with $d$ and with $c$; $\theta_g(b)=gbg=g\,cbc\,g=gcg=g=\theta_g(c)$, since $g=gc=cg$ and $cbc=c$; and $\theta_b(g)=\theta_a(g)$. The conclusion is $d\mt a$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:Q}
