and for $m\in B$ let $\varepsilon_m$ be the partition of $X$ whose non-singleton blocks are $\{z_m,z_m'\}$ and, for every $C\in\mathcal C$ one of whose colours is $m$, the block $\{y_C\}\cup\{x_u:u\in C\setminus V_m\}$ (the point $y_C$ together with the points of the side of $C$ \emph{not} coloured $m$). These blocks are pairwise disjoint: a vertex $u\in V_n$ lies in at most one component of $G[V_m,V_n]$, and components with different second colours involve points $x_u$ of different colours. Note that $x_v$ is a singleton of $\varepsilon_{\bot v}$.

\emph{Claim.} Let $v\in V_m$ and $v'\in V_n$. Then $x_v$ and $x_{v'}$ lie in the same class of $\varepsilon_m\vee\varepsilon_n$ iff $v\mt v'$. Indeed, if $m=n$ the join is $\varepsilon_m$, in which both points are singletons, and $v\mt v'$ iff $v=v'$ since the colouring is proper. If $m\ne n$ and $v$ lies in a component $C\in\mathcal C$ of $G[V_m,V_n]$, then the set $\{y_C\}\cup\{x_u:u\in C\}$ is a union of blocks of $\varepsilon_m$ (the block of $y_C$, which is $\{y_C\}\cup\{x_u:u\in C\cap V_n\}$, and the singletons $x_u$, $u\in C\cap V_m$) and likewise of $\varepsilon_n$, so it is a class of the join; if $v$ lies in no such component, $\{x_v\}$ is a class. So $x_v\sim x_{v'}$ iff $v,v'$ lie in a common component of $G[V_m,V_n]$ iff $vv'\in E$, by completeness of the components.

Now define $\varphi\colon Q(G,\bot)\to\P_X$: $\varphi(v,v')$ is the partition of $X\sqcup X'$ whose blocks are the blocks of $\varepsilon_{\bot v}$ on $X$ and of $\varepsilon_{\bot v'}$ on $X'$, except that the two singletons $\{x_v\}$ and $\{x'_{v'}\}$ are merged into one transversal block; and $\varphi(m,n)$ consists of the blocks of $\varepsilon_m$ on $X$ and of $\varepsilon_n$ on $X'$ (rank $0$). Clearly $\varphi(x^*)=\varphi(x)^*$, and $\varphi$ is injective: $\varphi(x)$ determines its rank, its upper and lower partitions (which determine the colours, since $\{z_m,z_m'\}$ is a block of $\varepsilon_m$ only) and, in rank $1$, the transversal pair $(x_v,x'_{v'})$. For the products, consider $\varphi(v,q)\varphi(r,s)$ with $v,q,r,s\in V$. The only point of the upper row of $\varphi(v,q)$ connected to the middle row is $x_v$, and its partner there is $x_q$; the middle row is glued by $\varepsilon_{\bot q}\vee\varepsilon_{\bot r}$, so by the Claim $x_q$ reaches the point $x_r$, and through it the lower point $x'_s$, iff $q\mt r$. Hence the product has the upper blocks of $\varepsilon_{\bot v}$, the lower blocks of $\varepsilon_{\bot s}$, and the transversal block $\{x_v,x'_s\}$ if $q\mt r$ and no transversal block otherwise: it is $\varphi(v,s)$ or $\varphi(\bot v,\bot s)$, as required. If one of the factors has rank $0$ there is no transversal block in the product, whose upper and lower partitions are those of the first and the last factor: $\varphi(m,n)\varphi(r,s)=\varphi(m,\bot s)$, $\varphi(v,q)\varphi(m,n)=\varphi(\bot v,n)$, $\varphi(m,n)\varphi(m',n')=\varphi(m,n')$. So $\varphi$ is an injective $*$-homomorphism.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:graphthm}
(1) For an induced subgraph $G'=G[V']$ the semigroup $Q(G',\bot|_{V'})$ is a $*$-subsemigroup of $Q(G,\bot)$. So a non-embeddable $Q(G,\bot)$ with $\bot$ proper contains a copy of $\mathfrak Q$, and $\mathfrak Q$ is the smallest non-embeddable graph semigroup satisfying \eqref{eq:Q1}; the list of forbidden coloured subgraphs consists of the monochromatic edge and the bichromatic path $a-c-b-d$. (2) The construction is not economical: for $\mathfrak Q$ with $\bot$ injective it uses $|X|=15$, while $\P_4$ suffices. (3) The triangle condition holds in every $Q(G,\bot)$ with $\bot$ proper, for a trivial reason: three pairwise commuting projections with a common pairwise product $w$ are either all equal or three pairwise non-adjacent vertices of one colour $w\in B$, so a triangle configuration with $w\ne z$ has $a_i\in V_w$ and $b_i\in V_z$ with $w\ne z$, and then $a_i$ and $b_i$ cannot commute. Thus the family $Q(G,\bot)$ sees only \eqref{eq:Q1} and \Wire, and by the theorem it can witness no further obstruction: an embedding, when it exists, uses only projections of rank $\le1$, whose $\T$-blocks are single points.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Layered graph semigroups}\label{subsec:layered}

The following generalisation replaces the bottom band of $Q(G,\bot)$ by another graph semigroup, and so on, so that the product of two unmatched projections may lie several levels below them. It turns out that this family, too, sees only \eqref{eq:Q1} and \Wire.

\begin{definition}\label{def:layered}
A \emph{layered graph} is a finite set $V=V_1\sqcup\dots\sqcup V_L$ ($L\ge1$) with a reflexive symmetric relation $\mt$ on each level $V_i$, complete on the bottom level $V_L$, and maps $\bot\colon V_i\to V_{i+1}$ ($1\le i<L$) which preserve $\mt$. For $p\in V_i$ and $\ell\ge i$ write $p_\ell=\bot^{\ell-i}p$ ($p$ descended to level $\ell$), call $p_L$ the \emph{root} of $p$ and $T_r=\{p:p_L=r\}$ the \emph{tree} of the root $r\in V_L$; the trees partition $V$. For $q,r\in V$ let
\[
f(q,r)=\min\{\ell\ge\max(\operatorname{lv}q,\operatorname{lv}r): q_\ell\mt r_\ell\},
\]
which exists because the bottom level is complete. The \emph{layered graph semigroup} $Q(V)$ is the set of pairs $(p,q)$ with $p,q$ on a common level, with $(p,q)^*=(q,p)$ and $(p,q)(r,s)=(p_\ell,s_\ell)$, $\ell=f(q,r)$.
\end{definition}

For $L=2$ this is $Q(G,\bot)$ ($V_1=V$, $V_2=B$); every map into a complete graph preserves $\mt$.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:layered}
$Q(V)$ is a regular $*$-semigroup; its projections are the pairs $(p,p)$, identified with $V$, and
\[
\theta_p(q)=p_{f(p,q)},\qquad q\le p\iff q=p_\ell\text{ for some }\ell .
\]
Moreover:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)},nosep]
\item \eqref{eq:Q1} holds in $Q(V)$ iff distinct matched elements have different roots;
\item two elements $p,q$ commute iff $p_f=q_f$ for $f=f(p,q)$, and then $pq=p_f$; under \eqref{eq:Q1} this says that $p,q$ lie in a common tree, and $p_f$ is their least common ancestor;
\item assuming \eqref{eq:Q1}, \Wire\ holds in $Q(V)$ iff for every level $i$ and every two roots $r\ne r'$ the bipartite graph of matched pairs between $T_r\cap V_i$ and $T_{r'}\cap V_i$ is a disjoint union of complete bipartite graphs and isolated vertices;
\item assuming \eqref{eq:Q1}, the triangle condition holds in $Q(V)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Preservation of $\mt$ gives $q_\ell\mt r_\ell\Rightarrow q_{\ell'}\mt r_{\ell'}$ for $\ell'\ge\ell$, hence $f(q_k,r)=\max(k,f(q,r))$ for $k\ge\operatorname{lv}q$. So $((p,q)(r,s))(t,u)=(p_{\ell_2},u_{\ell_2})$ with $\ell_2=f(s_{\ell_1},t)=\max(f(q,r),f(s,t))$, $\ell_1=f(q,r)$, and symmetrically $(p,q)((r,s)(t,u))=(p_{\ell_4},u_{\ell_4})$ with $\ell_4=\max(f(s,t),f(q,r))$. The identities $(xy)^*=y^*x^*$ and $xx^*x=x$ are clear ($f$ is symmetric and $f(q,q)=\operatorname{lv}q$). The projections are the symmetric pairs, and $\theta_p(q)=(p,p)(q,q)(p,p)=(p_f,q_f)(p,p)=(p_f,p_f)$ with $f=f(p,q)$, since $f(q_f,p)=f$; so $\theta_p(q)=q$ iff $q=p_f$.

(b) is the formula for $\theta$ together with Lemma~\ref{lem:comm}. Within a tree, $p_f\mt q_f$ with $p_f\ne q_f$ would be two distinct matched elements with a common descendant, so under \eqref{eq:Q1} ``matched'' means ``equal'' inside a tree and $f(p,q)$ is the level of the least common ancestor of $p$ and $q$.

(a) Matched elements lie on a common level, so ``no common descendant'' means ``different roots''. If $p\mt q$ are distinct with a common root then $r=p_L=q_L$ satisfies $r\le p,q$ but $\theta_p(q)=p\ne q=\theta_q(p)$. Conversely let $r\le p$, $r\le q$ with $p\ne q$, say $r=p_\ell=q_\ell$; at the level $f=f(p,q)\le\ell$ the matched elements $p_f,q_f$ have the common descendant $r$, so $p_f=q_f$ and $\theta_p(q)=p_f=q_f=\theta_q(p)$.

(c) Matched elements lie on a common level, so in an instance of \Wire\ all of $a,b,c,d$ lie on one level $i$. If $c=d$ the instance is trivial as in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:graphsg}. If $c\ne d$ then $cd=dc$ says by (b) that $c,d$ lie in a common tree, with $z:=c_f=d_f$, $f=f(c,d)$; since $a_f\mt c_f=z$ we get $f(a,z)=f$ and $\theta_a(z)=a_f$, similarly $\theta_b(z)=b_f$, so the last hypothesis says that $a,b$ lie in a common tree and their least common ancestor is at level $\le f$. By \eqref{eq:Q1} the tree of $a,b$ differs from that of $c,d$. Thus, writing $\mu(p,q)$ for the level of the least common ancestor of two elements of a tree, \Wire\ says: for every path $a-c-b-d$ of matchings with $a,b\in T_r$, $c,d\in T_{r'}$, $r\ne r'$, if $\mu(a,b)\le\mu(c,d)$ then $a\mt d$. Applied to the reversed path $d-b-c-a$ it says the same under $\mu(c,d)\le\mu(a,b)$; one of the two always holds. So \Wire\ is equivalent to: every path $a-c-b-d$ with $a,b\in T_r\cap V_i$ and $c,d\in T_{r'}\cap V_i$ closes up; that is, the bipartite graph in (c) has no induced $4$-vertex path, which (as in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:graphsg}) means that it is a disjoint union of complete bipartite graphs.

(d) Let $a_1,a_2,a_3,b_1,b_2,b_3$ be a triangle configuration. By (b) the $a_i$ pairwise commute, so they lie in one tree; likewise the $b_i$; and $a_i$ commutes with $b_i$, so all six lie in one tree. Then $a_1\mt b_2$ forces $a_1=b_2$ by (a), and similarly $a_2=b_3$, $a_3=b_1$. Hence $\{a_1,a_2,a_3\}=\{b_1,b_2,b_3\}$ and $w=a_1a_2=b_2b_3=z$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:layered}
For a layered graph $V$ the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)},nosep]
\item $Q(V)$ $*$-embeds in some partition monoid;
\item the projection algebra $V$ of $Q(V)$ embeds in some $\PA(\P_X)$;
\item $V$ satisfies \eqref{eq:Q1} and \Wire;
\item distinct matched elements have different roots, and for every level $i$ and every two roots $r\ne r'$ the bipartite graph of matched pairs between $T_r\cap V_i$ and $T_{r'}\cap V_i$ is a disjoint union of complete bipartite graphs and isolated vertices.
\end{enumerate}
When they hold, $Q(V)$ embeds in $\P_X$ with $|X|=|V|+|V_L|+\beta$, where $\beta$ is the number of complete bipartite components in \textup{(iv)}, and every $\T$-block of the image is a single point.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
(i)$\Rightarrow$(ii) is clear, (ii)$\Rightarrow$(iii) is \eqref{eq:Q1} and Corollary~\ref{cor:QS}, and (iii)$\Leftrightarrow$(iv) is Lemma~\ref{lem:layered}(a),(c). For (iv)$\Rightarrow$(i) we generalise the construction of Theorem~\ref{thm:graphsg}, with trees in the role of colours and the non-root elements in the role of vertices. Let $\mathcal C$ be the set of complete bipartite components in (iv), over all levels $i<L$ and pairs of roots; each $C\in\mathcal C$ lies on one level and meets exactly two trees. Let
\[
X=\{x_v: v\in V\setminus V_L\}\sqcup\{y_C:C\in\mathcal C\}\sqcup\{z_r,z_r': r\in V_L\},
\]
and for a root $r$ let $\varepsilon_r$ be the partition of $X$ whose non-singleton blocks are $\{z_r,z_r'\}$ and, for each $C\in\mathcal C$ meeting $T_r$, the block $\{y_C\}\cup\{x_u: u\in C\setminus T_r\}$. These blocks are disjoint (an element lies in at most one component per level and pair of trees, and for a fixed $r$ the elements $x_u$ in the blocks belong to the other tree of $C$). As in the Claim in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:graphsg}, for $u\in T_r$ and $v\in T_{r'}$, $r\ne r'$, the points $x_u,x_v$ lie in a common class of $\varepsilon_r\vee\varepsilon_{r'}$ iff $u,v$ lie in a common component $C\in\mathcal C$, iff $u\mt v$; the class of $x_u$ is $\{y_C\}\cup\{x_w:w\in C\}$ or $\{x_u\}$, and in particular contains no other point $x_{u'}$ with $u'\in T_r$ on a different level. Every $x_v$, $v\in T_r$, is a singleton of $\varepsilon_r$.

For $p\in T_r$ let $\hat p$ be the projection with partition $\varepsilon_r$ whose $\T$-blocks are the singletons $\{x_{p_j}\}$, $\operatorname{lv}p\le j<L$ (the chain from $p$ down to, but excluding, the root); so $\hat r$ has rank $0$, $\rk\hat p=L-\operatorname{lv}p$, and $\supp\hat p$ determines $p$. We check $\theta_{\hat p}(\hat q)=\widehat{p_f}$, $f=f(p,q)$, using Lemma~\ref{lem:PX}(a). If $p,q\in T_r$, the join is $\varepsilon_r$ itself, whose classes meeting $\supp\hat p$ are the singletons $\{x_{p_j}\}$, transversal in $\theta_{\hat p}(\hat q)$ iff $x_{p_j}\in\supp\hat q$, i.e.\ iff $p_j=q_k$ for some $k\ge\operatorname{lv}q$, i.e.\ iff $j\ge f$; so $\theta_{\hat p}(\hat q)$ is $\varepsilon_r$ with the $\T$-blocks $\{x_{p_j}\}$, $j\ge f$, which is $\widehat{p_f}$. If $p\in T_r$, $q\in T_{r'}$ with $r\ne r'$, the classes of $\varepsilon_r\vee\varepsilon_{r'}$ meeting $\supp\hat p$ are the classes $K_j$ of the points $x_{p_j}$, each containing only this one $\T$-block of $\hat p$; $K_j$ meets $\supp\hat q=\{x_{q_k}:k\ge\operatorname{lv}q\}$ iff $p_j\mt q_k$ for some $k\ge\operatorname{lv}q$, which forces $k=j$, iff $j\ge\operatorname{lv}q$ and $p_j\mt q_j$, iff $j\ge f$. So again $\theta_{\hat p}(\hat q)=\widehat{p_f}$. (For $q=r'$ a root, $\supp\hat q=\emptyset$ and $\theta_{\hat p}(\hat q)=\hat r=\widehat{p_L}$; and $\theta_{\hat r}(\hat q)=\hat r$ for every $q$ since $\hat r$ has rank $0$.) Injectivity: $\varepsilon_r$ determines $r$ (the block $\{z_r,z'_r\}$), and the $\T$-blocks determine $p$. So $p\mapsto\hat p$ embeds the projection algebra.

For the semigroup, send $(p,q)$ (with $p,q$ on level $i$) to the element $\varphi(p,q)$ whose upper and lower partitions are those of $\hat p$ and $\hat q$, with the transversal blocks $\{x_{p_j},x'_{q_j}\}$, $i\le j<L$. This is injective and $*$-compatible, and $\varphi(p,q)\varphi(r,s)=\varphi(p_\ell,s_\ell)$, $\ell=f(q,r)$: in the product, the upper point $x_{p_j}$ is joined to the middle point $x_{q_j}$, whose class in the middle join contains a point $x_{r_k}$, $k\ge\operatorname{lv}r$ (necessarily $k=j$), iff $j\ge f(q,r)$, by the two computations above, and then $x_{p_j}$ is joined to $x'_{s_j}$; for $j<\ell$ the block of $x_{p_j}$ in the product is the nontransversal singleton $\{x_{p_j}\}$, and the other blocks are unchanged. This is $\varphi(p_\ell,s_\ell)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:layered}
In a layered graph every principal down-set is a chain, every value $\theta_p(q)$ lies in that chain, and under \eqref{eq:Q1} the commuting pairs are the pairs in a common tree. This is why the triangle condition is invisible here: it needs two commuting triples with a common meet, which in a tree forces the triples to coincide. The three-level layered graph with $V_1=\{a,b,c,d\}$ ($a\mt c$, $b\mt c$, $b\mt d$), $V_2=\{m,n,k\}$ ($m\mt n$, $m\mt k$), $V_3=\{w,z\}$ and $\bot\colon a,b\mapsto m$, $c\mapsto n$, $d\mapsto k$, $m\mapsto w$, $n,k\mapsto z$ gives a $29$-element regular $*$-semigroup whose projection algebra $\mathfrak P_9$ (order $9$) satisfies \eqref{eq:Q1}, \Tri\ and the weaker form ``$a\mt c\mt b\mt d$, $cd=dc$, $\theta_a(cd)=\theta_b(cd)=\theta_a(d)\Rightarrow a=b$'' of \Wire\ in which the obstruction $\mathfrak P$ was first found, but violates \Wire\ itself: the pair $a,d$ is only half wired, $\theta_a(d)=m$ lying strictly between $w$ and $a$. It is a minimal non-embeddable projection algebra, the only minimal one of order $9$ that violates \Wire\ (Section~\ref{sec:comp}). Any semigroup separating \eqref{eq:Q1}$+$\Wire\ from embeddability must therefore have projections with non-chain down-sets, or must force $\T$-blocks of size $>1$; the constructions of this section use only singleton $\T$-blocks.
\end{remark}

%==============================================================================
\section{Computations}\label{sec:comp}

We record the computations behind the census, with the three levels of evidence of the introduction kept apart: what is proved, what is verified in $\PA(\P_m)$ for stated $m$, and what is a failure to find an embedding. The programs are in the repository accompanying the paper, \url{https://github.com/mkkinyon/starsemigroup}.

\emph{Why quasi-identities suffice.} For finite semigroups the search for quasi-identities is complete in principle:

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:finite}
A finite regular $*$-semigroup embeds in some partition monoid if and only if it satisfies every quasi-identity in the language $\{\cdot,{}^*\}$ that holds in all partition monoids; and a finite projection algebra embeds in some $\PA(\P_X)$ iff it satisfies every quasi-identity valid in all $\PA(\P_X)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Necessity is clear. Conversely, a finite $S$ satisfying all quasi-identities of the class $\{\P_X\}$ lies in the quasivariety it generates, so $S$ embeds in a product $\prod_j A_j$ of ultraproducts $A_j$ of partition monoids $\P_{X_{ij}}$, $i\in I_j$, over ultrafilters $\mathcal U_j$. As $S$ is finite, finitely many factors suffice, and by \L o\'s's theorem each $*$-homomorphism $S\to A_j$ is, for $\mathcal U_j$-almost all $i$, induced by a $*$-homomorphism $S\to\P_{X_{ij}}$ with the same kernel. Taking one such $i$ for each of the finitely many $j$ and using $\P_X\times\P_Y\le\P_{X\sqcup Y}$ gives an embedding of $S$ into a partition monoid. The same argument applies to projection algebras.
\end{proof}

\emph{Regular $*$-semigroups of small order.} All regular $*$-semigroups of order $n\le8$ were enumerated up to $*$-isomorphism by a backtracking search on multiplication tables with propagation of associativity, $(xy)^*=y^*x^*$ and $xx^*x=x$; for $n=7,8$ only the semigroups with a nontrivial involution were generated, since $x^*=x$ for all $x$ forces commutativity and hence an inverse semigroup, which embeds by the Wagner--Preston representation. For each non-inverse semigroup satisfying \eqref{eq:Q1}, a $*$-embedding into $\P_m$, $m\le5$, was searched for by assigning images to the projections first and forcing the images of all products.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}rrrrrr@{}}
\toprule
order & classes & inverse & non-inverse, \eqref{eq:Q1} & non-inverse, not \eqref{eq:Q1} & of these, in $\P_5$\\
\midrule
4 & 17 & 16 & 1 & 0 & 1\\
5 & 55 & 52 & 2 & 1 & 2\\
6 & 222 & 208 & 9 & 5 & 9\\
7 & $259^{\dagger}$ & 196 & 35 & 28 & 35\\
8 & $1310^{\dagger}$ & 970 & 178 & 162 & 178\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}

\smallskip
{\small $^\dagger$ nontrivial involution only. The last column counts the non-inverse semigroups satisfying \eqref{eq:Q1} that embed in $\P_5$.}
\end{center}
Thus, up to order $8$, a regular $*$-semigroup embeds in a partition monoid iff it satisfies \eqref{eq:Q1}, and then it embeds in $\P_5$. The smallest known regular $*$-semigroup satisfying \eqref{eq:Q1} that embeds in no partition monoid has order $19$ (a semigroup with projection algebra $\mathfrak P_7$); the order-$9$ enumeration was not completed, so the smallest such semigroup has order between $9$ and $19$. A cheap bound: if $p\mt q$ with $p\ne q$ then $pq$ and $qp$ are distinct non-projections determined by $\{p,q\}$, so $|S|\ge|\PA(S)|+2\cdot\#\{\{p,q\}:p\mt q,\ p\ne q\}$; for the nine minimal non-embeddable projection algebras of order $\le8$ this gives $|S|\ge14$, so a counterexample of order $\le13$ would have an embeddable projection algebra (Question~\ref{q:pa} below).

\emph{Quotients of $\P_3$ and $\P_4$.} The ten $*$-congruences of $\P_3$ have quotients of orders $203$, $179$, $103$, $79$, $67$, $43$, $7$, $3$, $2$, $1$. The quotients of orders $179,79,43$ have a zero and are not inverse, so they fail \eqref{eq:Q1}; those of orders $7,3,2,1$ are inverse; the two remaining proper quotients, of orders $103$ and $67$, satisfy \eqref{eq:Q1} and are the semigroups of Theorem~\ref{thm:witness} ($\P_3/\mu$ and its quotient collapsing the rank-$2$ $\Hh$-classes). For $\P_4$ the classification of \cite{EMRT} leaves two proper $*$-congruences whose quotients have no zero, both satisfying \eqref{eq:Q1} and failing \Tri\ because they contain the corresponding quotients of $\P_3$; so the congruences of $\P_4$ give nothing new.

\emph{Projection algebras.} The projection algebras of order $\le9$ were enumerated as described in Section~\ref{subsec:census}: Mace4 with isofilter for the orders $\le7$ (directly, from the axioms and the clauses for \eqref{eq:Q1} and \Wire), and the one-point extension method of Lemma~\ref{lem:extension} from order $7$ on (with \eqref{eq:Q1}, \Wire, \Rcond, and separately with \eqref{eq:Q1} alone up to order $9$); the two methods agree where both apply. Embeddings into $\PA(\P_m)$ were searched for by backtracking for $m\le4$ and by a SAT encoding for $5\le m\le8$ (presence variables for the blocks of the images, the joins computed by iterated squaring, solved with CaDiCaL or kissat); for the seven algebras of order $8$ the unsatisfiability at $m=8$ takes between $25$ seconds and $2.6$ hours. The validity of the quasi-identities in $\PA(\P_m)$ was verified by a SAT search for a violating instance, variables not assumed distinct, for $m\le7$ (\Qcond, the hardest, takes $11$ hours at $m=7$); the proofs of Sections~\ref{sec:wiring} and \ref{sec:chain} were found afterwards, and the intermediate statements of the proofs were tested on all instances of the hypotheses in $\PA(\P_4)$ and on samples in $\PA(\P_5)$, $\PA(\P_6)$, by the dropped-hypothesis test of Remark~\ref{rem:K4}.

\emph{Order $9$.} Of the $80\,039$ projection algebras of order $9$ satisfying \eqref{eq:Q1}, \Wire, \Rcond, exactly $163$ contain one of $\mathfrak P_8^1,\dots,\mathfrak P_8^7$ as a subalgebra, and one further algebra, which we call $\mathfrak R_9$, violates \Rp\ without containing any of them. Of the remaining $79\,875$, all but seven embed in $\PA(\P_8)$ ($79\,783$ already in $\PA(\P_7)$; the $39$ semilattices with $8$ join-irreducible elements and $46$ others need $m=8$). The seven embed in no $\PA(\P_m)$ with $m\le8$. Four of them are settled: one violates \Wn[2] (it is $\mathfrak P^{\mathrm{ch}}_2$), one violates \Kzero, one \Kfour, and one the variant \Hmp of \Kzero\ (Corollary~\ref{cor:K0pp}); three remain open (Section~\ref{sec:open}). The \eqref{eq:Q1}-only extension census of order $9$ (from the $10\,821$ embeddable algebras of order $8$: $213\,084$ labelled extensions, $81\,259$ up to isomorphism, of which $1\,227$ violate \Wire\ or \Rcond) shows that every projection algebra of order $9$ satisfying \eqref{eq:Q1} but violating \Wire\ or \Rcond\ contains one of the nine minimal non-embeddable algebras of order $\le8$, with a single exception: the algebra $\mathfrak P_9$ of Remark~\ref{rem:layered}, a second witness of \Wire. So the minimal non-embeddable projection algebras of order $9$ satisfying \eqref{eq:Q1} are exactly $\mathfrak P_9$, $\mathfrak R_9$, $\mathfrak P^{\mathrm{ch}}_2$, the three algebras settled by \Kzero, \Kfour, \Hmp, and those of the three open candidates that embed in no $\PA(\P_X)$: between six and nine algebras; their tables are in the repository (\texttt{paper/order9-tables.md}). In particular the number of points needed to embed an embeddable projection algebra of order $n$ reaches $n-1$ at $n=9$.

\emph{Prover9 and Mace4.} The algebraic parts of the proofs (the identities of Step~1 of \Rp, the substitution of Corollary~\ref{cor:R}, $\theta_z(c)=b$ in \Kfour, the equivalence of \Vcond\ with a three-atom clause) were found or confirmed with Prover9, and the implications \Kzero$+$\Wn[2]$\Rightarrow$\Eone, \Kfour$\Rightarrow$\Esix, \Wn[2]$\Rightarrow$\Rpp rest on Prover9 proofs (Remarks~\ref{rem:E1E6}, \ref{rem:W2}). Mace4 provided the separating models: $\mathfrak P_8^1$ and $\mathfrak P_8^6$ as the unique models of order $\le8$ of all proved conditions violating \Kzero, resp.\ \Kfour; the order-$9$ model separating \Hmp from \Kzero\ (Section~\ref{subsec:K0}); the model of order $11$ separating \Wn[3] from \Wn[2]; and the nonexistence of a projection algebra of order $\le10$ satisfying \eqref{eq:Q1} and violating \Tri.

%==============================================================================
\section{Open problems}\label{sec:open}

\begin{question}[order $9$]\label{q:nine}
Three projection algebras of order $9$ satisfying \eqref{eq:Q1}, \Tri, the eight wiring conditions and all chain conditions embed in no $\PA(\P_m)$ with $m\le8$, and all their proper subalgebras embed. Each violates a five-variable path-closing condition verified in $\PA(\P_m)$ for $m\le6$: for the first,
\[
a\mt c\mt b,\ \ bxb=b,\ \ \theta_y(a)=\theta_z(y),\ \ \theta_c(z)=\theta_x(a)=\theta_y(x),\ \ \theta_z(x)=\theta_b(a)\ \Longrightarrow\ a\mt b ,
\]
and the structurally simpler $a\mt c\mt b,\ c\sim x,\ x\sim y,\ z\le y,\ \theta_y(a)=\theta_y(z),\ z\sim b,\ \theta_a(x)=\theta_a(b),\ bxb=b\Rightarrow a\mt b$; for the third, $a\mt c\mt b,\ \theta_x(c)=a,\ byb=b,\ bzb=b,\ \theta_y(c)=\theta_y(b),\ \theta_z(c)=\theta_z(b),\ \theta_y(z)=\theta_z(y)=\theta_a(b),\ \theta_x(b)=\theta_b(a)\Rightarrow a\mt b$; the second has a five-element bottom band like $\mathfrak P_8^7$. Are these conditions valid in every $\P_X$? If so, Theorem~\ref{thm:census} extends to order $9$, with the additional conditions \Rp, \Wn[2], \Kzero, \Kfour, \Hmp and these three. Related: is the condition
\[
\text{K5}:\qquad a\mt c\mt b,\ \ \theta_x(c)=a,\ \ \theta_b(x)=b,\ \ zc=cz,\ \ \theta_z(x)=\theta_a(b),\ \ \theta_z(b)=\theta_b(a)\ \Longrightarrow\ a\mt b ,
\]
verified for $m\le6$ and the first condition found for the \Hmp-witness, valid in every $\P_X$?
\end{question}

\begin{question}[a uniform criterion]\label{q:criterion}
All the proofs of Section~\ref{sec:wiring} run by transport along the matchings and the propagation of an invariant along a join, and the hypotheses are all of five kinds (commutation, order, half-matching, seeing alike, being seen alike). Is there a combinatorial criterion deciding, for a configuration of auxiliaries attached to a path $a\mt c\mt b$, whether it forces $a\mt b$ in every $\P_X$, with one uniform proof? The chain conditions show that the list of such conditions is infinite, so a parametrised description is the best possible. Is there a hybrid chain family for every one of the eight conditions, as there is for \Rcond\ (the family \Wn) and for \Rp\ (the family $a\mt c\mt b,\ c\sim x_1\sim\dots\sim x_n,\ \theta_{x_i}(a)=\theta_{x_i}(c),\ ya=ay,\ \theta_{x_n}(b)=\theta_y(b)\Rightarrow a\mt b$, which has explicit witnesses of order $2n+6$ with embeddable maximal subalgebras, but is verified only for $m\le5$ and $n\le3$)?
\end{question}

\begin{question}[finite versus infinite]\label{q:infinite}
\Hmp implies \Kzero\ in every finite projection algebra (Corollary~\ref{cor:K0pp}), by finite descent; does it in every projection algebra? More generally, the proofs in $\P_X$ use rank counts, and Lemma~\ref{lem:descent} replaces them in finite algebras; is there a general mechanism turning a proof in $\P_X$ into a derivation valid in all finite projection algebras satisfying the other conditions? On the digraph side, the hexagon condition does not imply \Tri\ in general (Remark~\ref{rem:hinf}); does it imply $(\mathrm D_E)$ for the non-star digraphs on $k\ge4$ vertices?
\end{question}

\begin{question}[compression and decidability]\label{q:decidable}
Is embeddability of a finite projection algebra in some $\PA(\P_X)$ decidable? A recursive basis of quasi-identities would make non-embeddability semi-decidable, and embeddability is semi-decidable provided a compression lemma holds: if a finite $P$ embeds in some $\PA(\P_X)$, then it embeds in $\PA(\P_m)$ for some finite $m$, say $m\le f(|P|)$. This is not obvious, since restriction to a finite subset of $X$ does not preserve products. The census data ($m\le n-1$ suffices for every embeddable algebra of order $n\le9$) suggest that it is true, and a bound $f$ would turn ``verified for $m\le7$'' into proofs.
\end{question}

\begin{question}[the semigroup]\label{q:pa}
Is embeddability of a finite regular $*$-semigroup in a partition monoid determined by its projection algebra? All known obstructions are projection-algebra quasi-identities, and Theorem~\ref{thm:cs} shows that groups and sandwich matrices impose nothing; a regular $*$-semigroup is, however, far from determined by its projection algebra. What is the smallest regular $*$-semigroup satisfying \eqref{eq:Q1} that embeds in no partition monoid (its order is between $9$ and $19$)? By the bound of Section~\ref{sec:comp}, an example of order $\le13$ would have an embeddable projection algebra and answer the first question negatively.
\end{question}
